\begin{array}{l} \mathrm{regret}_{t}(\pi (\psi))\leq \underbrace{\left(\sum_{t = 1}^{m + 2h + n}\ell_{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\mathrm{alg}})\right)}_{\text{estimation~&~burn-in~loss}} + \underbrace{\left(\sum_{t = n + m + 2h + 1}^{t}\ell_{t}(\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\mathrm{alg}}) - \sum_{t = n + m + 2h + 1}^{t}f_{t}[\mathbf{m}_{t:t - h}\mid\widehat{g},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}]\right)}_{\text{loss~approximation-error}}, \\ + \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left[ \mathbf {m} _ {t : t - h} \mid \widehat {g} , \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1 : t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \right] - \inf  _ {m \in \mathcal {m}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (m \mid \widehat {g} , \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1 : t} ^ {\text {n a t}})\right)} _ {\widehat {f} \text {p o l i c y r e g r e t}} \\ + \underbrace {\left(\inf  _ {m \in \mathcal {m}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (m \mid \widehat {g} , \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1 : t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \inf  _ {m \in \mathcal {m}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {m} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {m})\right)} _ {\text {c o m p a r a t o r a p p r o x i m a t i o n - e r r o r}} \\ + \underbrace {\inf  _ {m \in \mathcal {m}} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {m} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {m}\right) - \inf  _ {\pi \in \pi (\psi)} \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\right)} _ {\text {p o l i c y a p p r o x i m a t i o n} := j (m) - j (\pi)} \tag {6.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

let us draw our attention two the main differences between the present decomposition and that in eq. (5.3): first, the burn-in phase contains the initial estimation stage  $n$ . during this phase, the system is excited by the gaussian inputs before estimation takes place. second, the truncation costs are replaced with approximation errors, which measure the discrepancy between using  $\widehat{g}$  and  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and using  $g_{\star}$  and  $\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ .

observe that the policy approximation error is exactly the same as that from the known-system regret bound, and is addressed by theorem 1. moreover, the policy regret can be bounded by a black-box reduction to the policy regret in the known-system cases:

lemma 6.2. assume condition 6.1. then, for  $\eta_t = \eta \propto \sqrt{d_{\mathrm{min}}} / lhr_{\mathrm{nat}}^2 r_{g_\star}^2 \sqrt{mt}$ , we have

$$
(\widehat {f} \text {- p o l i c y r e g r e t}) \lesssim \sqrt {t} l \sqrt {d _ {\min} m} h r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}
$$

proof. observe that the  $\widehat{f}$ -regret depends only on the  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $\widehat{g}$  sequence, but not on any other latent dynamics of the system. hence, from the proof lemma 5.5, we see can see more generally that if  $\max_t\| \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| \leq r_{\mathrm{nat}}'$  and  $\| \widehat{g}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq r_{g_\star}'$ , then  $\eta \propto \sqrt{d_{\mathrm{min}}} /lh(r_{\mathrm{nat}}')^2 (r_{g_\star}'')^2\sqrt{mt}$ ,

$$
(\widehat {f} \mathrm {- p o l i c y r e g r e t}) \lesssim l \sqrt {d _ {\mathrm {m i n}} m h ^ {2} t} (r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} (r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}
$$

in particular, under condition 6.1 and by lemma 6.1, we can take  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{\prime}\leq 2r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $r_{g_{\star}}^{\prime}\leq 2r_{g_{\star}}$

from lemma 6.1,  $\| \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}\| _2\leq r_{\mathrm{nat}} + r_{g_*}\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}}\leq 2r_{g_*}\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}}$  . assumption 2 then yields

lemma 6.3. under condition 6.1, we have that (estimation & burn-in)  $\leq 4l(m + 2h + n)r_{g_\star}^2\overline{r}_\mathbf{u}^2$

to conclude, it remains to bound the approximation errors. we begin with the following bound on the accuracy of estimated nature's  $y$ , proven in appendix d.3.1:

lemma 6.4 (accuracy of estimated nature's  $y$ ). assume condition 6.1, and let  $t \geq n + h + 1$ , we have that that  $\| \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} - \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} \| \leq 2r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}\epsilon_g$ .

we then use this to show that the estimation error is linear in  $t$ , but also decays linearly in  $\epsilon_g$ :

lemma 6.5 (approximation error bounds). under condition 6.1,

$$
\left(\text {l o s s a p p r o x i m a t i o n e r r}\right) + \left(\text {c o m p a r a t o r a p p r o x i m a t i o n e r r}\right) \lesssim l t r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g}
$$

proof sketch. for the "loss approximation error", we must control the error introduced by predicting using  $\widehat{g}$  instead of  $g_{\star}$ , and by the difference from affine term  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  in  $\mathbf{y}(\cdot \mid \widehat{g}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  from the true natures  $y\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . for the "comparator approximation error", we must also adress the mismatch between using the estimated  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  sequence of the controls in the functions  $f_t(\cdot \mid \widehat{g}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$ , and the true natures  $y$ 's  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  for the sequence  $(\mathbf{y}_t^m, \mathbf{u}_t^m)$ . a complete proof is given in appendix d.3.2

proof of theorem 3. assuming condition 6.1, taking  $n \geq m + h$  and combining lemma 6.3 with the substituting  $\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}}(\delta) \lesssim \max \{\sqrt{d_u + \log(1 / \delta)}, r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{g_\star}\}$ , and with lemmas 6.2 and 6.5 and theorem 1,

$$
\mathrm {r e g r e t} _ {t} (\psi) \lesssim l r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} \left(\left(\frac {d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)}{r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}} \vee 1\right) n + t \epsilon_ {g} (n, \delta) r _ {\mathcal {m}} + \sqrt {d m h ^ {2} t} + \frac {t \psi (m)}{r _ {\mathcal {m}}}\right).
$$

for  $\psi(m) \leq r_{\mathcal{m}} / \sqrt{t}$ , the last term is dominated by the second-to-last. now, for the constant  $c(\delta)$  as in the theorem statement, and for  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}r_{\mathcal{m}} \geq d_u + \log(1/\delta)$ , we have  $(\frac{d_u + \log(1/\delta)}{r_{\mathrm{nat}}^2r_{\mathcal{m}}^2} \vee 1)n + t\epsilon_g(n,\delta)r_{\mathcal{m}} \leq (n + c_\delta th^2 r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}} / \sqrt{n})$ . we see that if we have  $n = (th^2 r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}c_\delta)^{2/3}$ , then the above is at most

$$
\mathrm {r e g r e t} _ {t} (\psi) \lesssim l r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} \left((h ^ {2} t r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} c _ {\delta}) ^ {2 / 3} + \sqrt {d _ {\mathrm {m i n}} h ^ {2} m t}\right).
$$

finally, since  $\psi_{g_\star}(h + 1) \leq 1 / 10\sqrt{t}$ , one can check that condition 6.1 holds with probability  $1 - 3\delta - n^{-\log^2 n} = 1 - \delta - t^{-\omega (\log^2 t)}$  as soon as  $t \geq c'h^4 c_\delta^5 r_{\mathcal{m}}^2 r_{\mathrm{nat}}^2$  for a universal constant  $c'$ . when  $t \geq d_{\min}m^3$ , we can bound the above by  $\lesssim lr_{g_\star}^2 r_{\mathcal{m}}^2 r_{\mathrm{nat}}^2\left(h^2 r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}c_\delta\right)^{2/3} \cdot t^{2/3}$ .

# 7 logarithmic regret for known system

in this section, we prove theorem 4. the analogous result for the strongly-stabilized setting is proved in appendix e.

theorem 4 applies the same regret decomposition as theorem 2; the key difference is in bounding the  $f$ -policy regret in eq. (5.3). following the strategy of agarwal et al. [2019a], we first show that the persistent excitation induces strongly convex losses (in expectation). unlike this work, we do not assume access to gradients of expected functions, but only the based on losses and outputs reveal to the learner. we therefore reason about losses conditional on  $k \geq m$  steps in the past:

definition 7.1 (filtration and conditional functions). let  $\mathcal{f}_t$  denote the filtration generated by the stochastic sequences  $\{(\mathbf{e}_s^{\mathrm{stoch}},\mathbf{w}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}})\}_{s\leq t}$ , and define the conditional losses

$$
f _ {t; k} \left(m \mid g _ {\star}, \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) := \mathbb {e} \left[ f _ {t} \left(m \mid g _ {\star}, \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right].
$$

a key technical component is to show that  $f_{t;k}$  are strongly convex:

proposition 7.1. for  $\alpha_{f}$  as in theorem 4 and  $t\geq k\geq m$ $f_{t;k}(m\mid g_{\star},\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  is  $\alpha_{f}$ -strongly convex.

the above proposition is proven in appendix f.4, with the appendix f devoted to establishing a more general bound for strongly-stabilized (but not necessarily stable) systems.

typically, one expects strong-convex losses to yield log  $t$ -regret. however, only the condition expectations of the loss are strongly convex; the losses themselves are not. agarwal et al. [2019a] assume that access to a gradient oracle for expected losses, which circumvents this discrepancy. in this work, we show that such an assumption is not necessary if the unary losses are also  $\beta$ -smooth.

we now set up regularity conditions and state a regret bound (theorem 7) under which conditionally-strong convex functions yield logarithmic regret. the proof of theorem 4 follows by specializing these conditions to the problem at hand.

condition 7.1 (unary regularity condition (urc) for conditionally-strongly convex losses). suppose that  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$ . let  $f_t \coloneqq \mathcal{k} \to \mathbb{r}$  denote a sequence of functions and  $(\mathcal{f}_t)_{t \geq 1}$  a filtration. we suppose  $f_t$  is  $l_{\mathrm{f}}$ -lipschitz, and  $\max_{x \in \mathcal{k}} \| \nabla^2 f_t(x) \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \beta$ , and that  $f_{t;k}(x) \coloneqq \mathbb{e}[f_t(x) \mid \mathcal{f}_{t-k}]$  is  $\alpha$ -strongly convex on  $\mathcal{k}$ .

observe that proposition 7.1 precisely establishes the strong convexity requirement for urc, and we can verify the remaining conditions below. in the appendix, we prove a generic high-probability regret bounds for applying online gradient descent to urc functions (theorem 12). because we require bounds on policy regret, here we shall focus on a consequence of that bound for the "with-memory" setting:

condition 7.2 (with-memory regularity condition (wmrc)). suppose that  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  and  $h \geq 1$ . we let  $f_t \coloneqq \mathcal{k}^{h+1} \to \mathbb{r}$  be a sequence of  $l_c$  coordinatewise-lipschitz functions with the induced unary functions  $f_t(x) \coloneqq f_t(x, \ldots, x)$  satisfying condition 7.1.

our main regret bound in the with-memory setting is as follows:

theorem 7. let  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  have euclidean diameter  $d$ , consider functions  $f_t$  and  $f_t$  satisfying condition 7.2 with  $k \geq h \geq 1$ . consider gradient descent updates  $z_{t+1} \gets \pi_{\mathcal{k}}(z_t - \eta_{t+1}\nabla f_t(z_t))$ , with  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$  applied for  $t \geq t_0$  for some  $t_0 \leq k$ , with  $z_0 = z_1 = \dots = z_{t_0} \in \mathcal{k}$ . then, with probability  $1 - \delta$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}, z _ {t - 1}, \dots , z _ {t - h}\right) - \inf  _ {z \in \mathcal {k}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z) \\ \lesssim \alpha k d ^ {2} + \frac {(k + h ^ {2}) l _ {\mathrm {f}} l _ {\mathrm {c}} + k d l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k \beta l _ {\mathrm {f}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {1 + \log (e + \alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

the above bound is a special case of theorem 8 below, a more general result that addresses complications that arise when  $g_{\star}$  is unknown. our regret bound incurs a dimension factor  $d$  due to a uniform convergence argument<sup>9</sup>, which can be refined for structured  $\mathcal{k}$ .

to conclude the proof of theorem 4, it we simply apply theorem 7 with the appropriate parameters.

proof of theorem 4. from lemma 5.4, we can take  $l_{\mathrm{f}} \coloneqq 4l\sqrt{m} r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}r_{g_{\star}}^{2}r_{\mathcal{m}}$  and  $d \leq 2\sqrt{d} r_{\mathcal{m}}$ . we bound the smoothness in appendix e.1:

lemma 7.1 (smoothness). the functions  $f_{t}(m \mid g_{\star}, \mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  are  $\beta_{f}$ -smooth, where we define  $\beta_{f} \coloneqq mr_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}r_{g_{\star}}^{2}\beta_{\mathrm{loss}}$ .

this yields that, for  $\alpha \leq \alpha_{f}$  and step sizes  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ , the  $f$ -policy regret is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lesssim \alpha m d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} + \frac {(m d + h ^ {2}) l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + m \beta_ {f} l _ {\mathrm {f}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {m l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {1 + \log (e + \alpha_ {f} d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) \\ \lesssim \alpha m d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} + \frac {l ^ {2} m ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \left(\left(d + \frac {h ^ {2}}{m}\right) + \frac {\beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}} m ^ {1 / 2}}{l d r _ {\mathcal {m}}}\right) \max \left\{\log t, \log \left(\frac {1 + \log (e + \alpha_ {f} d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) \right\} \\ \lesssim \alpha m d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} + \frac {l ^ {2} m ^ {3} d r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \max  \left\{1, \frac {\beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}}}{l d r _ {\mathcal {m}}} \right\} \log \left(\frac {t + \log (e + \alpha d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) \quad (1 \leq h \leq m) \\ \lesssim \alpha m d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} + \frac {l ^ {2} m ^ {3} d r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \max  \left\{1, \frac {\beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}}}{l d r _ {\mathcal {m}}} \right\} \log \left(\frac {t}{\delta}\right) (t \geq \log (e + \alpha d r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2})) \\ \lesssim \frac {l ^ {2} m ^ {3} d r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2}}{\alpha \wedge l r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2}} \max  \left\{1, \frac {\beta_ {\text {l o s s}}}{l d r _ {\mathcal {m}}} \right\} \log \left(\frac {t}{\delta}\right), \tag {7.1} \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, combining the above with lemmas 5.2 and 5.3, and theorem 1,

$$
l r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} \left(m + h + \frac {\psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) t}{r _ {g _ {\star}}} + \frac {\psi (m) t}{r _ {\mathcal {m}}} + (\text {e q .} (7. 1))\right).
$$

applying  $\psi_{g_{\star}}(h + 1) \leq r_{g_{\star}} / t$ ,  $\psi(m) \leq r_{\mathcal{m}} / t$ , and  $h \leq m$ , the term in eq. (7.1) dominates.

![](images/24fae4daf1c766a229e52491b4b6a7a9c5a2acddd9f0dcc6a89a12b1af4cdfab.jpg)

# 8  $\sqrt{t}$ -regret for unknown system under strong convexity

in this section, we prove of theorem 5, which requires the most subtle argument of the four settings considered in the paper. we begin with a high level overview, and defer the precise steps to section 8.1. the core difficulty in proving this result is demonstrating that the error  $\epsilon_g$  in estimating the system propagates quadratically as  $t\epsilon_g^2 /\alpha$  (for appropriate  $\alpha = \alpha_{f} / 4$ ), rather than as  $t\epsilon_{g}$  in the weakly convex case. by setting  $n = \sqrt{t / \alpha}$ , we obtain regret bounds of roughly

$$
\operatorname {r e g r e t} _ {t} (\psi) \lesssim n + \frac {t}{\alpha} \epsilon_ {g} (n, \delta) ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\alpha} \log t \lesssim \sqrt {\frac {t}{\alpha}} + \frac {\log t}{\alpha}, \tag {8.1}
$$

where we let  $\lesssim$  denote an informal inequality, possibly suppressing problem-dependent quantities and logarithmic factors, and use  $\epsilon_g(n,\delta)\lesssim 1 / \sqrt{n}$ . to achieve this bound, we modify our regret decomposition by introducing the following a hypothetical "true prediction" sequence:

definition 8.1 (true prediction losses). we define the true prediction losses as

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {p r e d}} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \right] := \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} g _ {\star} ^ {[ i ]} \cdot \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} \left(m _ {t - i} \mid \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t - i} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right)
$$

$$
f _ {t} ^ {\text {p r e d}} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \right] := \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {p r e d}} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \right], \mathbf {u} _ {t} \left(m _ {t} \mid \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right)\right),
$$

and let  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m) = f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m, \ldots, m)$  denote the unary specialization, and define the conditional unary functions  $f_{t;k}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m) \coloneqq \mathbb{e}[f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m) \mid \mathcal{f}_{t-k}]$ .

note that the affine term of  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{pred}}$  is the true nature's  $y^{10}$ , and the inputs  $\mathbf{u}_{t - i}\left(m_{t - i} \mid \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t - i}^{\mathrm{nat}}\right)$  are multiplied by the true transfer function  $g_{\star}$ . thus, up to a truncation by  $h$ ,  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{pred}}$  describes the true counterfactual

output of system due to the control inputs  $\mathbf{u}_{t - i}\left(m_{t - i} \mid \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t - i}^{\mathrm{nat}}\right)$  selected based on the estimated nature's  $y$ 's.  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  and  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  then correspond to the counterfactual loss functions induced by these true counterfactuals.

while the algorithm does not access the unary losses  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  directly (it would need to know  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $g_{\star}$  to do so), we show in appendix e.2.2 that the gradients of  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  and  $f_{t}(m \mid \widehat{g}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  are  $\mathcal{o}(\epsilon_g)$  apart:

lemma 8.1. for any  $m \in \mathcal{m}$ , we have that

$$
\left\| \nabla f _ {t} (m \mid \widehat {g}, \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}) - \nabla f _ {t} ^ {\text {p r e d}} (m) \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq c _ {\text {a p p r o x}} \epsilon_ {g},
$$

where we define  $c_{\mathrm{approx}} \coloneqq \sqrt{m} r_{g_\star} r_\mathcal{m} r_{\mathrm{nat}}^2 (8\beta_{\mathrm{loss}} + 12l)$ .

as a consequence, we can view algorithm 1 as performing gradient descent with respect to the sequence  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$ , but with non-stochastic errors  $\pmb{\epsilon}_{t} \coloneqq \nabla f(m \mid \widehat{g}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}) - \nabla f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m)$ . the key observation is that online gradient descent with strongly convex losses is robust in that the regret grows quadratically in the errors via  $\frac{1}{\alpha}\sum_{t=1}^{t}\|\pmb{\epsilon}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}$ . by modifying the step size slightly, we also enjoy a negative regret term. the following bound applies to the standard strongly convex online learning setup:

proposition 8.1 (robustness of strongly convex ogd). let  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  be convex with diameter  $d$ , and let  $f_t$  denote a sequence of  $\alpha$ -strongly convex functions on  $\mathcal{k}$ . consider the gradient update rules  $z_{t+1} = \pi_{\mathcal{k}}(z_t - \eta_{t+1}(\nabla f_t(z_t) + \pmb{\epsilon}_t))$ , where  $\pmb{\epsilon}_t$  is an arbitrary error sequence. then, for step size  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ ,

$$
\forall z _ {\star} \in \mathcal {k}, \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) \leq \frac {6 l ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log (t + 1) + \alpha d ^ {2} + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

for our setting, we shall need a strengthening of the above theorem to the conditionally strongly convex with memory setting of condition 7.2. but for the present sketch, the above proposition captures the essential elements of the regret bound: (1) logarithmic regret, (2) quadratic sensitivity to  $\epsilon_{t}$ , yielding a dependence of  $t\epsilon_{g}^{2} / \alpha$ , and (3) negative regret relative to arbitrary comparators. with this observation in hand, we present our regret decomposition in eq. (8.2), which is described in terms of a comparator  $m_{\mathrm{approx}} \in \mathcal{m}$ , and restricted set  $\mathcal{m}_0 = \mathcal{m}(r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2, m_0) \subset \mathcal{m}$ , where  $m_0 = \left\lfloor \frac{m}{2} \right\rfloor - h$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {r e g r e t} _ {t} (\pi (\psi)) \leq \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {t = 1} ^ {m + 2 h + n} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}})\right)} _ {\mathrm {b u r n - i n l o s s}} + \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {t = m + 2 h + n + 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}) - \sum_ {t = m + 2 h + n + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} [ \mathbf {m} _ {t : t - h} ]\right)} _ {\mathrm {a l g o r i t h m t r u n c a t i o n e r r o r}}, \\ + \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {t = m + 2 h + n + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} [ \mathbf {m} _ {t : t - h} ] - \sum_ {t = m + 2 h + n + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} (m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}})\right)} _ {f ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} \mathrm {p o l i c y r e g r e t}} \\ + \underbrace {\sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} (m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}}) - \inf _ {m \in \mathcal {m} _ {0}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\star} , \mathbf {y} _ {1 : t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}})} _ {\widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \mathrm {c o n t r o l a p p r o x i m a t i o n e r r o r}} \\ + \underbrace {\left(\inf _ {m \in \mathcal {m} _ {0}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\star} , \mathbf {y} _ {1 : t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \inf _ {m \in \mathcal {m} _ {0}} \sum_ {t = n + m + 2 h + 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {m} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {m})\right)} _ {\mathrm {c o m p a r a t o r t r u n c a t i o n e r r o r}} \\ + \underbrace {\inf  _ {m \in \mathcal {m} _ {0}} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {m} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {m}\right) - \inf  _ {\pi \in \pi (\psi)} \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} , \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\right)} _ {\text {p o l i c y a p p r o x i m a t i o n} := j (m) - j (\pi)} \tag {8.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

the novelty in this regret decomposition are the "  $f^{\mathrm{pred}}$  policy regret" and  $\hat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  control approximation error" terms, which are coupled by a common choice of comparator  $m_{\mathrm{approx}}\in \mathcal{m}$ . the first is precisely the policy regret on the  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$ ,  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  sequence, which (as described above) we bound via viewing descent on  $f_{t}(\cdot \mid g_{\star},\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  as a running ogd on the former sequence, corrupted with nonstochastic error.

the term "  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  control approximation error" arises from the fact that, even though  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}$  describes (up to truncation) the true response of the system to the controls, it considers controls based on estimates of natures  $y$ 's, and not on nature's  $y$ 's themselves. to bound this term, we show that there exists a specific comparator  $m_{\mathrm{approx}}$  which competes with the best controller in the restricted class  $\mathcal{m}_0$  that access the true natures  $y$ 's. proposition 8.2 constructs a controller  $m_{\mathrm{approx}}$  which builds in a correction for the discrepancy between  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $\mathbf{y}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . we show that this controller satisfies for any  $c > 0$ :

$$
\text {‘} \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \text {c o n t r o l a p p r o x i m a t i o n e r r o r} ” \lesssim \frac {t \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{c} + c \sum_ {t} \| \mathbf {m} _ {t} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}. \tag {8.3}
$$

a proof sketch is given in section 8.1.2, which highlights how we use that  $\mathcal{m}$  overparametrizes  $\mathcal{m}_0$ . unlike the coarse argument in the weakly convex case, the first term eq. (8.3) has the desired quadratic sensitivity to  $\epsilon_g^2$ . however, the second term is a movement cost which may scale linearly in  $t$  in the worst case.

surprisingly, the proof of eq. (8.3) does not require strong convexity. however, in the presence of strong convexity, we can cancel the movement cost term with the negative-regret term from the  $f^{\mathrm{pred}}$  policy regret. as described above, the  $f^{\mathrm{pred}}$  policy regret can be bounded using a strengthening of proposition 8.1, to

$$
“ f ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} \mathrm {p o l i c y} \mathrm {r e g r e t} ” \lesssim \frac {1}{\alpha} \log t + \frac {t \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{\alpha} - \omega (\alpha) \sum_ {t} \| \mathbf {m} _ {t} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2},
$$

for appropriate strong convexity parameter  $\alpha$ . by taking  $c$  to be a sufficiently small multiple of  $\alpha$ ,

$$
\widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \text {c o n t r o l a p p r o x i m a t i o n e r r o r}" + " f ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} \text {p o l i c y r e g r e t}" \lessapprox \frac {1}{\alpha} \log t + \frac {t \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{\alpha}.}
$$

in light of eq. (8.1), we obtain the desired regret bound by setting  $n = \sqrt{t / \alpha}$ .

# 8.1 rigorous proof of theorem 5

# 8.1.1  $f^{\mathrm{pred}}$ -policy regret

we begin with by stating our general result for conditionally-strongly convex gradient descent with erroneous gradients. our setup is as follows:

condition 8.1. we suppose that  $z_{t + 1} = \pi_{\mathcal{k}}(z_t - \eta g_t)$ , where  $\pmb{g}_t = \nabla f_t(z_t) + \pmb{\epsilon}_t$ . we further assume that the gradient descent iterates applied for  $t \geq t_0$  for some  $t_0 \leq k$ , with  $z_0 = z_1 = \dots = z_{t_0} \in \mathcal{k}$ . we assume that  $\| g_t\| _2 \leq l_{\mathbf{g}}$ , and  $\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{k}) \leq d$ .

the following theorem is proven in appendix g.

theorem 8. consider the setting of condition 7.2 and 8.1, with  $k \geq 1$ . then with step size  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ , the following bound holds with probability  $1 - \delta$  for all comparators  $z_\star \in \mathcal{k}$  simultaneously:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) - \left(\frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \\ \lesssim \alpha k d ^ {2} + \frac {\left(k l _ {\mathrm {f}} + h ^ {2} l _ {\mathrm {c}}\right) l _ {\mathbf {g}} + k d l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k \beta l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {1 + \log (e + \alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

observe that when  $\epsilon_t = 0$ , we can take  $l_{\mathbf{g}} = l_{\mathrm{f}}$  and discard the second on third terms on the first line, yielding theorem 7. let us now specialize the above to bound the  $f^{\mathrm{pred}}$ -policy regret. first, we verify appropriate smoothness, strong convexity and lipschitz conditions; the following three lemmas in this section are all proven in appendix e.2.3.

lemma 8.2. under condition 6.1,  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m)$  are  $4\beta_{f}$ -smooth, for  $\beta_{f}$  as in lemma 7.1.

lemma 8.3. let  $\alpha_{f}$  as in proposition 7.1 and suppose that

$$
\epsilon_ {g} \leq \frac {1}{9 r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {g _ {\star}}} \sqrt {\frac {\alpha_ {f}}{m \alpha_ {\mathrm {l o s s}}}}
$$

then under condition 6.1, the losses  $f_{t;k}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m)$  are  $\alpha_f / 4$  strongly convex.

lemma 8.4 (lipschitzness: unknown & strongly convex). recall the lipschitz constant  $l_{f}$  from lemma 5.4. then under condition 6.1,  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m)$  is  $4l_{f}$ -lipschitz,  $f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}[m_{t:t - h}]$  is  $4l_{f}$  coordinate lipschitz. moreover,  $\max_{m\in \mathcal{m}}\| \nabla \widehat{f}_t(m;\widehat{g},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})\| _2\leq 4l_f$

specializing the above theorem to our setting, we obtain the following:

lemma 8.5 (strongly convex policy regret: unknown system). for the step size choose  $\eta_t = \frac{12}{\alpha t}$ , the following bound holds with probability  $1 - \delta$ :

$$
(f ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} \mathrm {- p o l i c y r e g r e t}) + \frac {\alpha}{4 8} \sum_ {t = n + h + m} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {m} _ {t} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \lesssim (\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)) + \frac {t c _ {\mathrm {a p p r o x}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} (n , \delta) ^ {2}}{\alpha}
$$

proof. in our setting,  $z_{t} \gets \mathbf{m}_{t}$ ,  $z_{\star} \gets m_{\mathrm{approx}}$ ,  $\pmb{\epsilon}_{t}$ , and  $\pmb{\epsilon}_{t} \gets \nabla f(m \mid \widehat{g}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}) - \nabla f_{t}^{\mathrm{pred}}(m)$ . moreover, we can bound the smoothness  $\beta \lesssim \beta_{f}$ , the strong convexity  $\alpha \gtrsim \alpha_{f}$ , and all lipschitz constants  $\lesssim l_{f}$ , where  $l_{f}$  was as in lemma 5.4. using the same diameter bound as in that lemma, we see that the term on the right hand side of theorem 8 can be bounded as in eq. (7.1)), up to constant factors. moreover, in light of lemma 8.1, we can bound the term  $\frac{6}{\alpha}\sum_{t = k + 1}^{t}\| \pmb{\epsilon}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}$  from theorem 8 by  $\lesssim \frac{1}{\alpha} t(c_{\mathrm{approx}}\epsilon_g)^2$ . this concludes the proof. lastly, we lower bound  $-\frac{\alpha}{12}\sum_{t = 1}^{t}\| z_{t} - z_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}$  by  $-\frac{\alpha}{48}\sum_{t = n + h + m}^{t}\| \mathbf{m}_{t} - m_{\mathrm{approx}}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}$ .

# 8.1.2  $\hat{y}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ -comparator approximation error

we prove the following theorem in appendix e.3:

proposition 8.2. let  $\mathcal{m}_0\coloneqq \mathcal{m}(m_0,r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2)$ , suppose that  $m\geq 2m_0 - 1 + h$ ,  $\psi_{g_{\star}}(h + 1)\leq r_{g_{\star}} / t$ , and that condition 6.1 holds. then there exists a universal constant  $c > 0$  such that, for all  $\tau >0$

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\hat {\mathbf {y}} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \mathrm {- a p p r o x e r r o r}) \leq 3 6 m ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {3} (m + t \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}) \max \{l, l ^ {2} / \tau \} \\ + \tau \sum_ {t = n + m + h + 1} ^ {t} \| \mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

proof sketch. let  $m_{\star}$  denote the optimal  $m \in \mathcal{m}_0$  for the loss sequence defined in terms of the true  $\mathbf{y}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $g_{\star}$ . first, consider what happens when the learner selects the controller  $\mathbf{m}_t = m_{\star}$  for each  $t \in [t]$ . by expanding appropriate terms, one can show that (up to truncation terms), the controller  $m_{\star}$  operating on  $\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  produces the same inputs as the controller  $m_{\mathrm{approx}} = m_{\star} + m_{\star} * (\widehat{g} - g_{\star}) * m_{\star}$  operating on  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ , where  $*$  denotes the convolution operator. since  $\mathcal{m}$  overparametrizes  $\mathcal{m}_0$ , we ensure that  $m_{\mathrm{approx}} \in \mathcal{m}$ .

realistically, the learner does not play  $\mathbf{m}_t = m_\star$  at each round. however, we can show that the quality in the approximation for playing  $\mathbf{m}_t$  instead of  $m_\star$  degrades as  $\sum_t \epsilon_g \cdot \| \mathbf{m}_t - m_\star \|_{\mathrm{f}}$ . by construction,  $\| m_{\mathrm{approx}} - m_\star \|_{\mathrm{f}}$  scales as  $\epsilon_g$ , so the triangle inequality gives  $\epsilon_g \cdot \| \mathbf{m}_t - m_\star \|_{\mathrm{f}} \lesssim \epsilon_g^2 + \epsilon_g \| \mathbf{m}_t - m_{\mathrm{approx}} \|_{\mathrm{f}}$ . by the elementary inequality  $ab \leq a^2 / \tau + \tau b^2$ , we find that the total penalty for the movement cost scales as  $\sum_t \epsilon_g^2 + \epsilon_g^2 / \tau + \tau \| \mathbf{m}_t - m_{\mathrm{approx}} \|_{\mathrm{f}}^2 = t \epsilon_g^2 (1 + 1 / \tau) + \tau \sum_t \| \mathbf{m}_t - m_{\mathrm{approx}} \|_{\mathrm{f}}^2$ ; this argument gives rise quadratic dependence on  $\epsilon_g^2$ , at the expense of the movement cost penalty.

# 8.1.3 concluding the proof of theorem 5

we assume that  $n$  and  $t$  satisfy, for an appropriately large universal constant  $c'$ ,

$$
n = m h ^ {2} c _ {\delta} r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \sqrt {m t (1 + \frac {l}{\alpha} + \frac {\beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}} ^ {2}}{l \alpha})} \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad t \geq c ^ {\prime} h ^ {4} c _ {\delta} ^ {6} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2}, \tag {8.4}
$$

since we also have  $\psi_{g_\star}(h + 1) \leq 1 / 10t$ , our choice of  $n$  ensures condition 6.1 holds with probability  $1 - \delta - n^{-\log^2 n} = 1 - \delta - t^{-\omega (\log^2 t)}$ . combining lemma 8.5 and proposition 8.2 with  $\tau = \frac{\alpha}{48}$ , we can cancel the movement cost in the second bound with the negative regret in the first:

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\widehat {\mathbf {y}} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \mathrm {- a p p r o x e r r o r}) + (f ^ {\mathrm {p r e d}} \mathrm {- p o l i c y r e g r e t}) \lesssim (\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)) + (t \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2} + m) \left(m ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} \max \{l, \frac {l ^ {2}}{\alpha} \} + \frac {c _ {\mathrm {a p p r o x}} ^ {2}}{\alpha}\right) \\ \lesssim (\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)) + (\frac {t c _ {\delta} ^ {2}}{n} + m) m ^ {2} h ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} (l + \frac {(l + \beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}}) ^ {2}}{\alpha}) \\ \lesssim (\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)) + \frac {t c _ {\delta} ^ {2}}{n} m ^ {2} h ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} (l + \frac {(l + \beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}}) ^ {2}}{\alpha}). \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the second line we recall from lemma 8.1 the bound  $c_{\mathrm{approx}} \lesssim \sqrt{m} r_{g_\star} r_{\mathcal{m}} r_{\mathrm{nat}}^2 (\beta_{\mathrm{loss}} + l)$ , and use  $\epsilon_g(n, \delta) = c_{\mathrm{est}} \frac{h^2 r_{\mathrm{nat}}}{\sqrt{n}} c_\delta$  for  $c_\delta \coloneqq \sqrt{d_{\mathrm{max}} + \log \frac{1}{\delta} + \log(1 + r_{\mathrm{nat}})}$ . in the third line, we use the assumption that  $t \geq m^2$  from the theorem.

from lemma 6.3, we can bound

$$
(\text {e s t i m a t i o n} \& \text {b u r n - i n} \text {l o s s}) \leq 4 l (m + 2 h + n) r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} ^ {2} \lesssim l n r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} ^ {2},
$$

where  $\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}}$  as in lemma 6.1. by assumption  $\sqrt{d_u + \log(1 / \delta)} \leq r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}$ , we have  $\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}} \lesssim r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}$ . thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\widehat {y} ^ {\text {n a t}} \text {- a p p r o x e r r o r}\right) + \left(f ^ {\text {p r e d}} \text {- p o l i c y r e g r e t}\right) + (\text {e s t i m a t i o n} \& \text {b u r n - i n l o s s}) \\ \lesssim (\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)) + l \left(\frac {t c _ {\delta} ^ {2}}{n} m ^ {2} h ^ {4} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {4} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {4} \left(1 + \frac {l + \beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}} ^ {2} / l}{\alpha}\right) + n r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

for our choice of  $n$ , the above is at most

$$
\lesssim \left(\mathrm {e q .} (7. 1)\right) + m h ^ {2} l r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {3} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {3} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {3} c _ {\delta} \sqrt {m \left(1 + \frac {l}{\alpha} + \frac {\beta_ {\mathrm {l o s s}} ^ {2}}{\alpha l}\right)} \cdot \sqrt {t}.
$$

finally, similar arguments as those in previous bounds show that the truncation costs and policy approximation error are dominated by the above regret contribution under the assumptions  $\psi\left(\lfloor \frac{m}{2}\rfloor -h\right) = \psi (m_0)\leq$ $r_{g_{\star}} / t$  and  $r_{\psi}\le r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2$  (note that the policy approximation is for the class  $\mathcal{m}_0 = \mathcal{m}(m_0,r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2)$ ), and  $\psi_{g_{\star}}(h + 1)\leq r_{g_{\star}} / t$

# 9 concluding remarks

this work presented a new adaptive controller we termed disturbance response control via gradient descent (drc-gd), inspired by a youla's parametrization. this method is particularly suitable for controlling system with partial observation, where we show an efficient algorithm that attains the first sublinear regret bounds under adversarial noise for both known and unknown systems.

this technique attains optimal regret rates for many regimes of interest. notably, this is the only technique which attains  $\sqrt{t}$ -regret for partially observed, non-stochastic control model with general convex losses. our bound is also the first technique to attain  $\sqrt{t}$ -regret for the classical lqg problem, and extends this bound to a more general semi-adversarial setting.

in future work we intend to implement these methods and benchmark them against recent novel methods for online control, including the gradient perturbation controller agarwal et al. [2019a]. we also intend to

compare our guarantees to techniques tailored to the stochastic setting, including certainty equivalence control mania et al. [2019], robust system level system dean et al. [2018], and sdp-based relaxations cohen et al. [2019]. we also hope to design variants of drc-gd which adaptively select algorithm parameters to optimize algorithm performance, and remove the need for prior knowledge about system properties (e.g. decay of the nominal system). lastly, we hope to understand how to use these convex parametrizations for related problem formulations, such as robustness to system mispecification, safety constraints, and distributed control.

acknowledgements the authors acknowledge tuhin sarkar and nikolai matni for their insightful feedback. elad hazan acknowledges funding from nsf grant 1704860. max simchowitz is generously supported by an open philanthropy graduate student fellowship.

# a appendix organization and notation

# a.1 organization

this appendix presents notation and organization. appendix b presents a  $\omega(\sqrt{t})$  lower bound for the online non-stochastic control problem, even with partial observant and benign conditions. it also gives a detailed comparison with prior work, detailed in tables 2 and 3.

appendix c introduces the general stabilized setting, where the nominal system need not be stable, but is placed in feedback with a stabilizing controller. all results from the stable setting (assumption 1) extend to the stabilized setting (assumption 1b) with appropriate modifications. statement which apply to the apply specifically to stabilized setting are denoted by the number of their corresponding statement for the stable setting, with the suffix 'b'. for example, assumption 1 stipulates the stable-system setting, and assumption 1b the stabilized setting.

appendix d addresses omitted proofs and stabilized-system generalizations of theorems 2 and 3, which give regret bounds for nonstochastic control for known and unknown systems respectively. appendix e does the same for the strongly-convex, semi-adversarial setting, namely theorems 4 and 5. this section relies on two technical appendices: appendix f verifies strong convexity of the induced losses under semi-adversarial noise, and appendix g derives the regret bounds for conditionally-strongly convex losses (see condition 7.1), and under deterministic errors in the gradients (see condition 8.1).

# a.2 notation for stable setting

we first present the relevant notation for the stable setting, where the transfer function  $g_{\star}$  of the nominal system is assumed to be stable. this is the setting assumed in the body of the text.

# a.3 notation for stabilized setting

in general, we do not require that  $g_{\star}$  be a stable matrix, but instead that  $g_{\star}$  is placed in feedback with a stabilizing controller  $\pi_0$ . in this case, we let  $g_{\star}$  denote the dynamics introduced by the feedback between the nominal system and  $\pi_0$ ; details are given in appendix c; at present, we summarize the relevant notation.

# a.4 relationship between parameters (assumption 4)

- in typical settings, we might imagine that  $(w_{t}, e_{t})$  are a sequence of noise which are possibly biased, by having mean say at most  $\sigma$  and subgaussian proxy  $\sigma^2$ . then, with probability  $1 - \delta$ , the  $\max \{\|xe_t\|, \|xw_t\|\} \leq \|x\|_{\mathrm{op}} \mathcal{o}(1) \cdot \sigma d \log(t / \delta)$  for any matrix  $x$  of rank at most  $d$ .  
- by inflating  $r_{g_\star}, \psi_{g_\star}$  if necessary, we can take  $\psi_{g_\star}(n)$  to be an upper bound on

$$
\mathcal {o} (1) \cdot \sum_ {i \geq n} \max  \left\{\| b a ^ {i - 1} c \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}, \| a ^ {i - 1} c \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \right\}.
$$

this together with the previous statement yields a bound of  $r_{\mathrm{nat}} \leq r_{g_\star} \sigma d \log (t / \delta)$ , where  $d = \max \{d_u, d_y\}$ .

- while this parameter regimes suggest suggests that  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  is large relative to  $r_{g_\star}$ , we recall that  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}, r_{g_\star}$  are upper bounds on various system norms, rather than exact characterizations any given norm. thus, we can satisfy the relation in the previous bullet by inflating  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  appropriately. note that our bound degrades gracefully in  $r_{\mathrm{nat}}$ , so this does not force an undue increase in regret.  
- the reason for the geometric decay is as follows: any stable matrix  $a$  with  $\rho(a) < 1$  admits a positive definite lyapunov matrix  $p \succeq i$ , for which  $a^\top pa \preceq (1 - \epsilon)p$ , for some appropriate  $\epsilon$ . this implies that, for a suitable constant  $\kappa > 0$  depending on  $\|c\|_{\mathrm{op}}, \|b\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , and  $\|p\|$ ,  $\max\{\|ca^{i-1}b\|, \|ca^{i-1}b\|\} \leq \kappa(1-\epsilon)^{i-1}$ , thus, using our inflated definition  $\psi_{g_\star} = \mathcal{o}(1) \cdot \sum_{i \geq n} \max\{\|ba^{i-1}c\|_{\mathrm{op}}, \|a^{i-1}c\|_{\mathrm{op}}\}$ , we have that  $\psi_{g_\star}(n) = \mathcal{o}(\kappa) \sum_{i \geq n} k(1-\epsilon)^{n-1} \leq \kappa(1-\epsilon)^{n-1}/\epsilon$ . absorbing these other factors into  $\kappa$  gives the desired geometric decrease.  
- most conditions can be relaxed up to constant factors, because online learning methods degrade gracefully when parameters are misspecified. the main exceptions are: (a) one needs to still choose  $h, m$  so that  $\psi_{g_\star}(h) \lesssim 1 / t$ , and similarly  $\psi(m) \lesssim 1 / t$ . if the decay parameters are not known exactly, then the learner must choose a larger  $h$  to be conservative. (b), for strongly convex losses, the effective strong convexity parameter used must be *less* than the true strong convexity modulus. lastly, (c), parameters out to be selected so as to ensure stability in the unknown system setting (see lemma 6.1)

<table><tr><td>transfer operators</td><td>definition (stabilized case)</td></tr><tr><td>π0</td><td>nominal stabilizing controller</td></tr><tr><td>ηt</td><td>“control-output” produced by nominal controller reduces to yt in stable case</td></tr><tr><td>utex</td><td>exogenous input to controller reduces to ut in stable case</td></tr><tr><td>gex→(y,u)</td><td>transfer function from exogenous inputs to system outputs and inputs (almost equivalent to gπ0,cl, see definition 2.2b)</td></tr><tr><td>gex→η</td><td>transfer function from exogenous inputs to control-output ηt (definition 2.2b)</td></tr><tr><td>input/output sequence</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>vt=(yt,ut)</td><td>output-input pair</td></tr><tr><td>vtalg=(yt,ut)</td><td>output-input pair produced by algorithm</td></tr><tr><td>vt nat=(yt nat, ut nat)</td><td>output-input pair with no exogenous input</td></tr><tr><td>ηt nat</td><td>nature&#x27;s “control-output” under zero output reduces to yt nat in stable case</td></tr><tr><td>utex,alg</td><td>exogenous input introduced by algorithm (not including nominal controller)</td></tr><tr><td>utex(mt | η1:t)</td><td>exogenous input from estimates of η nat see definition 4.1b for expression, and for below</td></tr><tr><td>vt[mt:t-h | gex→(y,u), η1:t nat, vhat]</td><td>prediction of vt under estimated dynamics and η nat.</td></tr><tr><td>ft[mt:t-h | gex→(y,u), η1:t nat, vhat]</td><td>prediction of loss under estimated dynamics and η nat.</td></tr><tr><td>ft(m | gex→(y,u), η1:t nat, vhat)</td><td>unary specialization of the above.</td></tr><tr><td>radius terms</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>rg*</td><td>1 ∨ ||g*,u→u||l1,op ∨ ||g*,(y→u)||l1,op ≤ rg*</td></tr></table>

# a.5 efficient  $\ell_{1}$ , op projection

we describe an efficient implementation of the  $\ell_1$ , op projection step in the algorithms above. as with other spectral norms, it suffies to diagonalize and compute a projection of the singular values onto the corresponding vector-ball, which in this case is the ball:  $\{(z^{[i]}) : z^{[i]} = 0, i > m, \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} \|z^{[i]}\|_{\infty} \leq r\}$ ; an efficient algorithm for this projection step is given by quattoni et al. [2009].

# b comparison with past work & lower bounds

# b.1 comparison to prior work

tables 2 and 3 describe regret rates for existing algorithms for known system and unknown system settings, respectively. within each table, bold lines further divide the results into nonstochastic and stochastic/semi-adversarial regimes. specifically, stochastic noise means well conditioned noise that is bounded or light-tailed, non-stochastic noise means noise selected by an arbitrary adversary, and semi-adversarial noise is an intermediate regime described formally by assumption 6/ 6b. compared to past work in non-stochastic control, we compete with stabilizing ldcs, which strictly generalize state feedback control. we note however that for stochastic linear control with fixed quadratic costs, state feedback is optimal, up to additive constants that do not grow with horizon  $t$ .

table 2: comparison with prior work for known system. see above for explanation of relevant settings.

[2019a]</td><td>√t</td><td>full</td><td>adversarial lipschitz</td><td>nonstochastic</td><td>disturbance &amp; state feedback</td></tr><tr><td>theorem 2</td><td>√t</td><td>partial</td><td>adversarial lipschitz</td><td>nonstochastic</td><td>stabilizing ldc</td></tr><tr><td>cohen et al. (a) agarwal et al. [2019b], cohen et al. [2018] assume the knowledge of the noise model making the assumption stronger than simply knowing the system  
(b) pseudo-regret refers to the best comparator "outside the expectation". it is strictly weaker than regret.  
(c) the smoothness assumption is necessary to remove the need for the expected-gradient oracle, and can be removed if such a stronger oracle is provided.

table 3: comparison with prior work for unknown system. see above for explanation of relevant settings.

[2019] (d)</td><td>t2/3</td><td>full</td><td>adversarial lipschitz</td><td>nonstochastic</td><td>disturbance &amp; state feedback</td></tr><tr><td>theorem 3</td><td>t2/3</td><td>partial</td><td>adversarial lipschitz</td><td>nonstochastic</td><td>stabilizing ldc</td></tr><tr><td>abbasi-yadkori &amp; szepesvári [2011]</td><td>ed·√t (e)</td><td>full</td><td>fixed quadratic</td><td>stochastic</td><td>state feedback</td></tr><tr><td>dean et al. (d) to identify the system, hazan et al. [2019] assumes that the pair  $(a_{\star}, b_{\star})$  satisfies a strong contrabillity assumption. our nature's y's formulation dispenses with this assumption.  
(e) this bound is exponential in dimension  $d$ .  
(f) the authors in mania et al. [2019] present technical guarantees that can be used to imply  $t^{2/3}$  regret for the partially observed setting when combined with concurrent results. since the paper was released, stronger system identification guarantees can be used to establish  $\sqrt{t}$  regret for this setting [sarkar et al., 2019, tsiamis and pappas, 2019]. to our knowledge, this complete end-to-end result does not yet exist in the literature.  
(g) unlike theorem 5, smoothness is still necessary even when given access to the stronger oracle. alternatively, certain noise distributions (e.g. gaussian) can be used to induce smoothness.

# b.2 regret lower bounds for known systems

we formally prove our lower bound in the following interaction model:

definition b.1 (lower bound interaction model). we assume that  $\mathbf{x}_{t + 1} = a_{\star}\mathbf{x}_t + b_{\star}\mathbf{u}_t + \mathbf{w}_t$ , where  $\mathbf{w}_t$  are drawn i.i.d. from a fixed distribution. we assume that the learners' controllers  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  may depend arbitrarily on  $\mathbf{x}_t$  and  $(\mathbf{u}_s,\mathbf{x}_s)_{1\leq s < t}$ . for a policy class  $\pi$  and joint distribution  $\mathcal{d}$  over losses and disturbances, we define

$$
\mathrm {p s e u d o r e g r e t} _ {t} ^ {\mathbf {a l g}} (\pi , \mathcal {d}) := \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\mathbf {a l g}}, \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathbf {a l g}}) \right] - \inf _ {\pi \in \pi} \mathbb {e} _ {\mathcal {d}} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\pi}, \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}) \right] \leq \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathrm {r e g r e t} _ {t} (\pi) \right],
$$

informally, our lower bound states that  $\sqrt{t}$  regret is necessary to compete with the optimal state feedback controller for pseudo-regret in the fully observed regime, either when the noises are stochastic and loss is known to the learner, or the noises are constant and deterministic, and the losses stochastic. formally:

theorem 9. let  $d_x = 2$ , and  $d_u = 1$ ,  $a_\star = \mathbf{0}_{d_x \times d_x}$ ,  $b_\star = -e_1$ , and  $\pi$  denote the set of all state-feedback controllers of the form  $\mathbf{u}_t = k_v\mathbf{x}_t$ , for  $k_v = v \cdot e_1^\top$ . then for the interaction model of definition b.1, the following hold

1. fixed lipschitz loss & unknown i.i.d noise: fix a loss  $\ell(x, u) = |x[1]|$ , and a family of distributions  $\mathcal{p} = \{\mathcal{p}\}$  over i.i.d. sequences of  $\mathbf{w}_t$  with  $\| \mathbf{w}_t \| \leq 2$  for  $t \geq 2$

$$
\inf_{\mathbf{alg}}\max_{\mathcal{d}\in \{\mathbb{i}_{\ell_{t} = \ell}\} \otimes \mathcal{p}}\operatorname {pseudoregret}_{t}^{\mathbf{alg}}(\pi ,\mathcal{d})\geq -1 + \omega (t^{1 / 2}),
$$

where  $\{\mathbb{i}_{\ell_t = \ell}\} \otimes \mathcal{p}$  is the set of joint loss and noise distribution induced  $\ell_t = \ell$  and  $\mathbf{w}_t \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{p}$ .

2. i.i.d lipschitz loss & known deterministic noise then there exists a family of distributions  $\mathcal{p} = \{\mathcal{p}\}$  over i.i.d sequences of 1-lipschitz loss functions with  $0\leq \ell_t(0)\leq 1$  almost surely such that

$$
\inf_{\mathsf{alg}}\max_{\mathcal{d}\in \mathcal{p}\otimes \{i_{\mathbf{w}_{t} = (1,0)}\}}\operatorname {pseudoregret}_{t}^{\mathsf{alg}}(\pi ,\mathcal{d})\geq -1 + \omega (t^{1 / 2})
$$

where  $\mathcal{p} \otimes \{i_{\mathbf{w}_{t} = (0,1)}\}$  is the set of joint loss and noise distribution induced  $\ell_1, \ell_2, \ldots \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \mathcal{p}$  and  $\mathbf{w}_t = (1,0)$  for all  $t$ .

proof. let us begin by proving part 1. let  $\mathcal{p}$  denote the set of distributions  $\mathcal{p}_p$  where  $\mathbf{w}_t[2] = 1$  for all  $t$ , and  $\mathbf{w}_t[1] \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \operatorname{bernoulli}(p)$  for  $t \geq 1$ . let  $\mathbb{e}_p$  denote the corresponding expectation operator, and let  $\operatorname{pseudoregret}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}(t,p)$  denote the associated pseudoregret. we can verify

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t} [ 1 ] - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} [ 1 ] \\ 1 \end{array} \right], \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {k _ {v}} = v, \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {k _ {v}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t} [ 1 ] - v \\ 1 \end{array} \right]
$$

for  $\ell (x,u) = |x[1]|$ , we have

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {p} [ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell (\mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}, \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}) ] = \mathbb {e} _ {p} [ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} | \mathbf {w} _ {t} [ 1 ] - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} [ 1 ] | ].
$$

since  $\mathbf{w}_t[1] \in \{0, 1\}$ , we can assume  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}[1] \in [0, 1]$ , since projecting into this interval always decreases the regret. in this case, given the interaction model,  $\mathbf{w}_t[1] \mid \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}[1]$  is still bernoulli  $(p)$  distributed. therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} _ {p} [ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} | \mathbf {w} _ {t} [ 1 ] - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} [ 1 ] | ] = \mathbb {e} _ {p} [ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} (1 - p) \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} + p (1 - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}) ] \\ = p t + (1 - 2 p) \mathbb {e} _ {p} [ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} ] \\ = p t + (1 - 2 p) t \mathbb {e} _ {p} [ z _ {t} ], \\ \end{array}
$$

where we let  $z_{t} \coloneqq \frac{1}{t}\sum_{t = 1}^{t}\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\mathrm{alg}}\in [0,1]$ . on the other hand, for any  $v\in [0,1]$

$$
\mathbb {e} _ {p} \left[ \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \ell \left(\mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {k _ {v}}, \mathbf {u} ^ {k _ {v}}\right) \right] \leq 1 + t \{(1 - p) v + p (1 - v) \} = 1 + p t + (1 - 2 p) t v,
$$

where the additive 1 accounts for the initial time step. hence,

$$
\mathrm {p s e u d o r e g r e t} _ {t} ^ {\mathsf {a l g}} (t, p) \geq - 1 + \max _ {v \in [ 0, 1 ]} (1 - 2 p) t (\mathbb {e} _ {p} (z _ {t}) - v) = - 1 + t | 1 - 2 p | \cdot | \mathbb {e} _ {p} (z _ {t}) - \mathbb {i} (1 - 2 p \geq 0) |.
$$

the lower bound now follows from a hypothesis testing argument. since  $z_{t} \in [0,1]$ , it follows that there exists an  $\epsilon = \omega (t^{-1 / 2})$  such that (see e.g. kaufmann et al. [2016])

$$
\left| \mathbb {e} _ {p = 1 / 2 + \epsilon} (z _ {t}) - \mathbb {e} _ {p = 1 / 2 - \epsilon} (z _ {t}) \right| \leq \frac {7}{8}.
$$

combining with the previous display, this shows that for  $p \in \{1/2 - \epsilon, 1/2 + \epsilon\}$ ,

$$
\operatorname {p s e u d o r e g r e t} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} (t, p) \geq - 1 + t | 1 - 2 p | \omega (1) \geq - 1 + \omega (t \epsilon) = - 1 + \omega (t ^ {1 / 2}).
$$

this proves part 1. part 2 follows by observing that the above analysis goes through by moving the disturbance into the loss, namely  $\ell_t(x,u) = |x[1] - \mathbf{e}_t|$  where  $\mathbf{e}_t \stackrel{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim} \operatorname{bernoulli}(p)$  and  $\mathbf{w}_t[1] = 0$  for zero.

# c generalization to stabilized systems

in this section, we consider a generalization to settings where the system may not be internally stable; that is, where  $\rho(a_{\star}) \geq 1$ . throughout, we assume the system is stabilizable and detectable: a linear system is said to be stabilizable if, in the absence of perturbations, there is a state-feedback controller that drives the state of the system asymptotically to zero; a detectable system is one where, in absence of perturbations, the state asymptotically tends to zero as long as the observations are all zeros. relaxing the notions notions of controllability and observability respectively, these requirements do not impose any conditions on the stable modes of the system. in particular, we will employ these assumptions to guarantee the existence of a stabilizing observer-feedback control. see anderson and moore [2007] for an extensive discussion.

our general recipe is as follows:

1. we assume access to a stabilizing nominal controller  $\pi_0$ . this induces an dynamical system with exogenous inputs, or ldc-ex (definition c.3).  
2. the ldc-ex produces a control output,  $\pmb{\eta}_{t}$ . it's "natural" version  $\pmb{\eta}_{t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  (definition 3.1b) can be computed from input output data, and is what is used to parametrize the controller. this formulation is described in appendix c.2.  
3. in appendix c.3, we formally detail our controller parametrization for this framework, which we call drc-ex, or disturbance response control with exogenous inputs. we then provide the generalization of our main algorithm, which we term drc-gd-ex.  
4. in appendix c.4, we detail various examples of ldc-ex parametrizations.

(a) we show that the stable setting can be recovered as a special case, as well as the static-feedback control, and control with nominal stabilizing controllers which are themselves internally-stable (examples c.1 to c.3).  
(b) in general, unstable systems may require internally-unstable controllers to yield stable closed-loop dynamics. to this end, we describe an ldc-ex parametrization based on exact observer feedback (example c.4), which yields the classical youla parametrization [youla et al., 1976], and allows us to extend our results to arbitrary stabilizable and detectable systems.  
(c) the exact youla parametrization requires full system knowledge to construct an exact observer-feedback controller. to circumvent this, we demonstrate a convex parametrization based on approximate observer feedback, example c.5. this combines the classical youla parametrization with a perspective based on nature's  $\eta$ 's, which affords convex parametrization without an exact observer-feedback controller.

5. finally, in appendix c.5, we demonstrate that all above examples of drc-exparametrizations are fully expressive, in that they can approximate the dynamics of any stabilizing linear dynamic controller to arbitrary degrees of accuracy (theorems 10 and 1b).

# c.1 preliminaries

going forward, it will be useful to slightly formalize our notion of markov operators, which we shall interchangeably refer to as transfer operators. we define

definition c.1 (markov operator). let  $\mathcal{g}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the set of markov operators  $g = (g^{[i]})_{i \geq 0}$  with  $g^{[i]} \in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , such that  $\|g\|_{\ell_1, \mathrm{op}} < \infty$ . given a system  $(a, b, c, d)$  with input dimension  $d_{\mathrm{in}}$  and output dimension  $d_{\circ}$ , we let  $g = \text{transfer}(a, b, c, d) \in \mathcal{g}_{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the system  $g^{[0]} = d$  and  $g^{[i]} = ca^{i-1}b$ .

next, we state a computation of the joint evolution of a system under an ldc  $\pi$ :

lemma c.1. let  $(\mathbf{y}_t^\pi, \mathbf{x}_t^\pi)$  be the observation-state sequence produced on the execution of a ldc  $\pi$  on the lds parameterized via  $(a_\star, b_\star, c_\star)$ . for a given sequence of disturbances  $(\mathbf{e}_t, \mathbf{w}_t)$ , the joint evolution of the system may be described as

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {t + 1} ^ {\pi} \end{array} \right] = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & b _ {\star} c _ {\pi} \\ b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {a _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \end{array} \right] + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} \\ 0 & b _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {b _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t} \end{array} \right], \tag {c.1}
$$

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \end{array} \right] = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & 0 \\ d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {c _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \end{array} \right] + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i \\ 0 & d _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {d _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t} \end{array} \right]. \tag {c.2}
$$

we refer to this dynamical system as the closed-loop system in the main paper. finally, we define

$$
c _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, u} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right], \quad b _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, e} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} \\ b _ {\pi} \end{array} \right],
$$

and let  $g_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl},e\to u}\coloneqq \mathrm{transfer}(a_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl}},b_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl},e},c_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl},u},d_{\pi})$

proof. the dynamical equations may be verified as an immediate consequence of equation 2.1.

definition c.2 (markov operators for closed loop systems). given an ldc  $\pi$ , we define the systems,

$$
g _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, u \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} := \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} d _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}} + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} c _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}} a _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}} ^ {i - 1} b _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}}, \quad g _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, u \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} g _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, u \to y} ^ {[ i ]} \\ g _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, u \to u} ^ {[ i ]} \end{array} \right], \quad b _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ 0 \end{array} \right]
$$

where  $(a_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}, b_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}, c_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}, d_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}})$  are given by lemma c.1. furthermore, we define  $\psi_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}} = \psi_{g_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}}$  as the decay function of  $g_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}$ , namely,  $\psi_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}(n) = \sum_{i \geq n} g_{\pi,\mathrm{cl}}$ .

# c.2 linear dynamic controllers with exogenous inputs (ldc-ex)

in this section, let us set up a general stabilized parametrization. first, let us define the notion of an internal stabilizing controller:

definition c.3. an linear dynamic controller with exogenous inputs or ldc-ex, denoted by a policy  $\pi_0 = (a_{\pi_0},b_{\pi_0},c_{\pi_0},d_{\pi_0})$ , as well as matrices  $b_{\pi_0,u},c_{\pi_0,\eta},d_{\pi_0,\eta}$ , which selects inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  according to the following dynamics:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathring {\mathbf {s}} _ {t + 1} = a _ {\pi_ {0}} \mathring {\mathbf {s}} _ {t} + b _ {\pi_ {0}} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} + b _ {\pi_ {0}, u} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \\ \mathbf {\dot {u}} _ {t} = c _ {\pi_ {0}} \mathbf {\hat {s}} _ {t} + d _ {\pi_ {0}} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = c _ {\pi_ {0}, \eta} \mathring {\mathbf {s}} _ {t} + d _ {\pi_ {0}, \eta} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + \mathring {\mathbf {u}} _ {t} \\ \end{array}
$$

we refer to  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  as the exogenous input,  $\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_t$  as the internal input, and  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  as the total input. we refer to  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}\in \mathbb{r}^{d_\eta}$  as the control-output. the control policy  $\pi_0$  is called the nominal controller. lastly, we also define  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}\coloneqq (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}})\in \mathbb{r}^{d_y + d_u}$ , which we call the total output.

overloading notation, we will alternatively use  $\pi_0$  to refer to the policy control  $(a_{\pi_0},b_{\pi_0},c_{\pi_0},d_{\pi_0})$ , and to index objects associated with both  $\pi_0$  and the additional matrices  $b_{\pi_0,u},c_{\pi_0,\eta},d_{\pi_0,\eta}$ .

in an ldc-ex, the endogenous input  $\mathbf{\dot{u}}_t$  is chosen so that, in the absence of inputs - i.e.  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}\equiv 0$  - the joint dynamics of the system remain stable. this allows us to generalize to settings where the dynamics of the nominal system may not be stable. for somewhat sophisticated reasons, in stabilized systems, one can be restricted by using nature's y's for inputs. instead, we will base our inputs on nature's  $\eta$  's, defining  $\pmb{\eta}_{t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  to be the control-output in the absence of exogenous inputs:

definition 3.1b (natures u's, y's,  $\eta$  's). we define  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ ,  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ , and  $\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  as the sequence that arises when, for all  $s$ ,  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}} = 0$ . we set  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}})$ . we note that  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  coincide with  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\pi_0},\mathbf{y}_t^{\pi_0}$ , whose dynamics are given by lemma c.1 with the policy  $\pi \gets \pi_0$ .

rather than requiring the nominal system to be stable, we will use controllers based on  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  (or estimates thereof). this requires only that the  $\pi_0$  stabilize  $a_{\star}$ . formally:

assumption 1b (stabilized setting). we assume that an ldc-ex is stabilizing; namely that  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$  is stable, where  $a_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl}}$  be defined in lemma c.1.

in order to define our drc-ex parameterization, we need to introduce the following relevant transfer operators. we note that the  $a$  matrix in each of the following markov operators is  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$ , which is stable by the above assumption, so each of the following operators are stable:

definition 2.2b (markov operators for strongly stabilized system). fix an ldc-ex controller, and let  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}, b_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}, c_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}, d_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$  be as in lemma c.1, with  $\pi \gets \pi_0$ . further, define

$$
c _ {\pi_ {0}, \operatorname {c l}, \eta} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} d _ {\pi_ {0}, \eta} c _ {\star} & c _ {\pi_ {0}, \eta} \end{array} \right], \quad d _ {\pi_ {0}, \operatorname {c l}, \eta} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & d _ {\pi_ {0}, \eta} \end{array} \right],
$$

$$
b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {e x}} \end{array} \right], \quad d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ i \end{array} \right],
$$

and the transfer functions  $g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}\in \mathcal{g}^{(d_y + d_u)\times d_u}$  and  $g_{\mathrm{ex}\to \eta}\in \mathcal{g}^{d_\eta \times d_u}$  via

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}}\right)
$$

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} := \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \eta}, 0\right)
$$

$$
g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta} := \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \eta}, d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \eta}\right)
$$

$$
g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow (y, u)} = \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}\right),
$$

and will decompose  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)} = \begin{bmatrix} g_{\mathrm{ex} \to y} \\ g_{\mathrm{ex} \to u} \end{bmatrix}$  for appropriate  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to y} \in \mathcal{g}^{d_y \times d_u}$ ,  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to u} \in \mathcal{g}^{d_u \times d_u}$ .

we can now write a "nature's y's" representation of all relevant quantities:

lemma 3.1b. we have the following identities for  $(\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_t^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}})$ :

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \\ \mathbf {\overset {\circ} {u}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}
$$

moreover, we have the following identity for  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$ :

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}
$$

finally, we can express nature's  $y$ 's,  $u$ 's and  $\eta$ 's as functions of the noise via

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {(w, e) \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t - i} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t - i} \end{array} \right], \quad \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t - i} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t - i} \end{array} \right]
$$

the proof of the above lemma is a consequence of computation augmenting that of lemma c.1 computation, whose proof we omit in the interest of brevity. we now state the relevant generalization of assumption 3, which by the above lemma and a similar computation for the mapping of  $(w,e)\to (y,u)$ , holds for any bounded noise sequence:

assumption 3b (bounded nature's  $y$ ,  $u$ ,  $\eta$ ). we assume that that  $\mathbf{w}_t$  and  $\mathbf{e}_t$  are chosen by an oblivious adversary, and that  $\| \mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} \|_2 \coloneqq \| (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}, \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}) \|_2 \leq r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $\| \boldsymbol{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} \|_2 \leq r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  for all  $t$ .

# c.3 drc-ex parametrization and algorithm

let us now describe the drc-ex parametrization. throughout, we will suppress dependence on  $\pi_0$ .

definition 3.2b (disturbance response controller with exogenous inputs). a disturbance response controller with exogenous inputs (drc-ex), parameterized by a  $m$ -length sequence of matrices  $m = (m^{[i]})_{i=0}^{m-1}$ , chooses the control input as  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}} = \sum_{s=0}^{m-1} m^{[s]} \boldsymbol{\eta}_{t-s}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . for a fixed  $m$ , we denote the resultant inputs, outputs, and control-outputs  $(\mathbf{y}_t^m, \mathbf{u}_t^m, \boldsymbol{\eta}_t^m)$ , and let  $j_t(m)$  the loss functional. we also set  $\mathbf{v}^m = (\mathbf{y}^m, \mathbf{u}^m)$ .

parallel to the stable setting, if  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}$  is known exactly, one can exactly recover  $\pmb{\eta}_{t - s}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  via lemma 3.1b. when unknown, we can approximately recover  $\pmb{\eta}_{t - 1}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  using an estimate  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}$ , namely (line 7)

$$
\widehat {\pmb {\eta}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} := \pmb {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}
$$

thus, we propose to use the estimates  $\widehat{\pmb{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  to define our controller. moreover, to estimate the consequence of a given input, we also need to estimate the  $\mathbf{v}_t = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}})$  so can ascertain the baseline in the absence of exogenous input. thus we take

$$
\widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} := \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}.
$$

the above definitions give rise to the following counterfactual dynamics and losses:

definition 4.1b (counterfactual costs and dynamics, stabilized systems). let  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{nat}})$ , and  $\widehat{\mathbf{v}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  denote estimates of  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . we define the counterfactual costs and dynamics

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(m _ {t} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) := \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {m - 1} m _ {t} ^ {[ i ]} \cdot \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t - i} ^ {\text {n a t}}
$$

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right] := \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \cdot \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {t - i} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t - i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right),
$$

$$
f _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right] := \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right]\right)
$$

overloading notation, we let  $\mathbf{v}_t(m_t\mid \cdot)\coloneqq \mathbf{v}_t[m_t,\ldots ,m_t\mid \cdot ]$  denote the unary (single  $m_{t}$  ) specialization of  $\mathbf{v}_t$  , and lower case  $f_{t}(m|\cdot) = f_{t}[m,\dots,m|\cdot ]$  the specialization of  $f_{t}$  . throughout, we use parenthesis for unary functions of  $m_{t}$  , and brackets for functions of  $m_{t:t - h}$

the gradient feedback controller (algorithm 1) and estimation procedure (algorithm 2), and drc-gd algorithm for unknown algorithm (algorithm 3) are modified in algorithms algorithms 4 to 6, respectively.

algorithm 4: disturbance response controller via gradient descent, with exogenous inputs (drc-gd-ex)  
1 input: stepsize  $\eta_t$ , radius  $r$ , memory  $m$ , markov operators  $\hat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \rightarrow (y,u)}$ ,  $\hat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \rightarrow \eta}$ , rollout  $h$ .  
2 define  $\mathcal{m} = \mathcal{m}(m,r) = \{m = (m^{[i]})_{i=0}^{m-1} : \|m\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}} \leq r\}$ .  
3 initialize  $\mathbf{m}_1 \in \mathcal{m}$  arbitrarily.  
4 for  $t = 1, \ldots, t$  do  
5 observe  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}, \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}})$   
6 update  $\mathbf{s}_t, \mathbf{\bar{u}}_t, \boldsymbol{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  as in definition c.3  
7 estimate  $\hat{\mathbf{v}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  via  
8 choose the exogenous control input as  
9  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}} \gets \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbf{m}_t \mid \hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}) = \sum_{i=0}^{m-1} \mathbf{m}_t^{[i]} \hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t-i}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ .  
10 play total input  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}} + \mathbf{\bar{u}}_t$   
11 observe the loss function  $\ell_t$  and suffer a loss of  $\ell_t(y_t, u_t)$ .  
12 recalling  $f_t(\cdot|\cdot)$  from definition 4.1b, update the disturbance feedback controller as  
13  $\mathbf{m}_{t+1} = \pi_{\mathcal{m}}\left(\mathbf{m}_t - \eta_t \partial f_t\left(\mathbf{m}_t \mid \hat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \rightarrow (y,u)}, \hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}, \hat{\mathbf{v}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\right)\right)$   
14 end

# algorithm 5: estimation of unknown system

1 input: number of samples  $n$ , system length  $h$ .  
2 initialize

$$
\widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ 0 ]} = \left[\begin{array}{c}0 _ {d _ {y} \times d _ {u}}\\i _ {d _ {u}}\end{array}\right], \quad \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = 0 _ {(d _ {y} + d _ {u}) \times d _ {u}}, \quad \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ i ]} = 0 _ {d _ {\eta} \times d _ {u}}, \quad i > 0
$$

3 for  $t = 1,2,\ldots ,n$  do  
4 | play  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, i_{d_u})$ , recieve  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = (\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}, \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}})$  and  $\boldsymbol{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  
5 end  
6 estimate  $\widehat{g}_{u\to y}^{[1:h]}$  and  $\widehat{g}_{u\to u}^{[1:h]}$  via least squares:

$$
\widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ 1: h ]} \leftarrow \underset {g ^ {[ 1: h ]}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {t = h + 1} ^ {n} \| \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

$$
\widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ 0: h ]} \leftarrow \underset {g ^ {[ 0: h ]}} {\arg \min } \sum_ {t = h + 1} ^ {n} \| \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

7 return  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)},\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to \eta}.$

# c.4 examples of ldc's with exogenous inputs

let us now provide examples of possible ldc's with exogenous inputs  $\pi_0$  which can be used. the first three examples (examples c.1 to c.3) are only pertain to a subset of dynamical systems - namely those that are

# algorithm 6: drc-gd-ex for unknown system

1 input: stepsizes  $(\eta_t)_{t\geq 1}$ , radius  $r_{\mathcal{m}}$ , memory  $m$ , rollout  $h$ , exploration length  $n$  
2 run the estimation procedure (algorithm 2) for  $n$  steps with system length  $h$  to estimate

$$
\widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta}
$$

3 run the regret minimizing algorithm (algorithm 1) for  $t - n$  remaining steps with estimated markov

$$
\text {o p e r a t o r s} \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta}, \text {s t e p s i z e s} (\eta_ {t + n}) _ {t \geq 1}, \text {r a d i u s} r _ {\mathcal {m}}, \text {m e r r o n g} m, \text {r o l l o u t p a r a m e t e r} h.
$$

(a) internally stable, (b) stabilizable by static feedback, or (c) stabilized by an internally stable controller.

in general, the are certain pathological which are unstable, and cannot be stabilized by static feedback or internally stable controller (see e.g. halevi [1994]). for general systems, appendix c.4.1 describes an ldc-ex formulation based on powerful parametrization known as the "youla parametrization" youla et al. [1976], also attributed to kucera [1975], which uses an observer-feedback controller to provide an internally stabilizing, convex controller parametrization for arbitrary systems.

unfortunately, realizing an exact youla parametrization requires exact system knowledge. to adress this, we consider introduce an ldc-ex parametrization based on approximate youla parametrization. under mild conditions, we shall show that these parametrizations have the same expressive power as the exact youla parametrization, despite allowing for inexact system knowledge.
