example c.1 (stable system). the internally stable system case (assumption 1) corresponds to the setting where  $\rho(a_{\star}) < 1$ . hence, we can  $a_{\pi_0}, b_{\pi_0}, c_{\pi_0}, d_{\pi_0}$  to be identically zero,  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$ , corresponding to  $c_{\pi_0,\eta} = 0$  and  $d_{\pi_0,\eta} = i$ . this satisfies assumption 1b because  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}} = a_{\star}$ , which is stable by assumption. this identical to the stable system setting in the body of the paper.

example c.2 (static feedback). under static feedback, we take  $a_{\pi_0}, b_{\pi_0}, c_{\pi_0}$  to be zero, but set  $d_{\pi_0} = k$  for a static-feedback matrix  $k \in \mathbb{r}^{d_y \times d_x}$ . again, we set  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$ , corresponding to  $c_{\pi_0,\eta} = 0$  and  $d_{\pi_0,\eta} = i$ . from lemma c.1, the closed-loop matrix  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$  is given by  $a_{\star} + b_{\star}kc_{\star}$ . thus, we require  $k$  such that  $\rho(a_{\star} + b_{\star}kc_{\star}) < 1$ . for general partially observed systems, it may not be the case that such a  $k$  exists, even if the system is stabilizable (i.e., there exists a control policy  $\pi_0$  which stabilizes it). however, for stabilizable fully observed systems, such a  $k$  is always guaranteed to exist, and can be obtained by solving the discrete algebraic riccati equation, or dare [anderson and moore, 2007]. observe that static feedback reduces to the stable-system setting when  $k = 0$ .

example c.3 (stabilizing feedback). more generally, we can select a stabilizing controller  $\pi_0$  such that  $a_{\pi_0}, b_{\pi_0}, c_{\pi_0}, d_{\pi_0}$  need not be zero, but both the internal controller dynamics, and the closed-loop dynamics are stable. that is,  $\rho(a_{\pi_0}) < 1$  and  $\rho(a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}) < 1$ . yet again, we set  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = \mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$ , corresponding to  $c_{\pi_0,\eta} = 0$  and  $d_{\pi_0,\eta} = i$ . note that this strictly generalizes examples c.1 and c.2: static feedback is recovered by setting  $a_{\pi_0}, b_{\pi_0}, c_{\pi_0} = 0$  and  $d_{\pi_0} = k$ , and stable systems by setting  $d_{\pi_0} = 0$  as well.

# c.4.1 exact youla ldc-ex

as described above, certain pathological systems may not admit any stabilizing controller  $\pi_0$  satisfying example c.3, and thus no controllers satisfying either of the special cases examples c.1 and c.2. however, all stabilizable system and detectable systems do admit stabilizing controllers of the following form:

example c.4 (exact observer feedback). consider a stabilizable and detectable system, and fix matrices  $l, f$  that satisfy  $\rho(a_{\star} + b_{\star}f) < 1$  and  $\rho(a_{\star} + lc_{\star}) < 1$ . exact observer feedback with exogenous inputs denotes the internal state  $\mathbf{\tilde{s}}_t$  via  $\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_t \in \mathbb{r}^{d_x}$ , and has the dynamics

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = c _ {\star} \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}, \quad \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + f \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t}, \\ \end{array}
$$

with  $\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_1 = 0$ . this yields an ldc-ex  $d_{\eta} = d_y$ , with  $a_{\pi_0} = (a_\star + lc_\star + b_\star f)$ ,  $b_{\pi_0} = -l$ ,  $c_{\pi_0} = f$ ,  $d_{\pi_0} = 0$ ,  $c_{\pi_0, \eta} = c_\star$ , and  $d_{\pi_0, \eta} = -i$ .

note that the optimal lqg controller is an observer-feedback controller. however, for this parametrization, we don't need to know this optimal lqg controller. rather, any observer-feedback controller will suffice.

lemma c.2. under example c.4, following identities hold:

1.  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}^{[i]} = 0$  for all  $i > 0$ . in other words,  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = \pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  for all  $t$ , regardless of exogenous inputs.  
2. we have the identity.

$$
g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i _ {d _ {y}} \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} c _ {\star} (a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}) ^ {i - 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i _ {d _ {x}} & f \end{array} \right].
$$

3. we have the identity

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ i _ {d _ {u}} \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c} c _ {\star} \\ f \end{array} \right] (a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f) ^ {i - 1} b _ {\star}.
$$

4. we have the identity

$$
g _ {(w, e) \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i \\ 0 & 0 \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & 0 \\ 0 & - l \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} & b _ {\star} f \\ - l c _ {\star} & a _ {\star} + b f + l c _ {\star} \end{array} \right] ^ {i - 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & 0 \\ 0 & f \end{array} \right]
$$

moreover, via a change of basis, we can write

$$
g _ {(w, e) \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i \\ 0 & 0 \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & 0 \\ - l & - l \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f & b _ {\star} f \\ 0 & a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star} \end{array} \right] ^ {i - 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & - f \\ 0 & f \end{array} \right]
$$

proof. the first four computations may be verified directly. alternatively, lemma c.3 suffices to establish this while substituting  $(a_{\star},b_{\star},c_{\star}) = (\widehat{a},\widehat{b},\widehat{c})$ . for the last claim, a change of basis conjugating the  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$  matrix by  $t = \begin{bmatrix} i & 0\\ i & i \end{bmatrix}$ , via  $t^{-1}a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}t$  suffices.

in particular, since  $\rho(a_{\star} + b_{\star}f), \rho(a_{\star} + lc_{\star}) < 1$  hold by assumption due to stabilizability and detectability, all of the above systems are guaranteed to be stable.

# c.4.2 approximate youla ldc-ex (ldc-ex)

the previously suggested parameterization requires exact specification of the system parameters  $(a_{\star},b_{\star},c_{\star})$ . however, for an unknown system, one can only hope to estimate parameters approximately. this section details the effects of executing a youla controller with approximate estimates of the system parameters.

example c.5 (approximate youla ldc-ex). an approximate observer-feedback controller when given parameter estimates  $\widehat{a},\widehat{b},\widehat{c}$  and executed under the influence of exogenous inputs follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \\ \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t} = \widehat {c} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + f \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t}. \\ \end{array}
$$

note that  $\widehat{\pmb{\eta}}_t$  depends on the history of exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$ . still, we can give a closed form representation of the overall system dynamics, and the map from exogenous inputs to outputs/controls:

lemma c.3. set  $\delta_t \coloneqq \widehat{\mathbf{x}}_t - \mathbf{x}_t$  and  $\delta_{\mathrm{youl}} \coloneqq \widehat{a} - a_\star + l(\widehat{c} - c_\star)$ . then, the dynamics induced by example c.5 satisfy that

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} \\ \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t + 1} \end{array} \right] = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f & b _ {\star} f \\ \delta_ {\text {y o u l}} & \widehat {a} + l \widehat {c} \end{array} \right]} _ {:= a _ {\cdot , \text {i n}}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \end{array} \right] + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ \widehat {b} - b _ {\star} \end{array} \right]} _ {b _ {\cdot , \text {i n}}} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e x}} + \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & 0 \\ - i & - l \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t} \end{array} \right]
$$

and

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} \\ \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & 0 \\ \widehat {c} - c _ {\star} & \widehat {c} \\ f & f \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ i \\ 0 \end{array} \right] \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + \left[ \begin{array}{c} i \\ - i \\ 0 \end{array} \right] \mathbf {e} _ {t}.
$$

denoting by  $g_{\cdot,\mathrm{in}}$  the markov operator describing the map from  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}} \to (\mathbf{y}_t, \mathbf{u}_t)$ , we then have the identity that

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \end{array} \right] + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\hat {\gamma}, \text {i n}} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\text {e x}}.
$$

proof. let's change variables.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} = a _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + b _ {\star} f \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f\right) \mathbf {x} _ {t} + b _ {\star} f \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + \widehat {b} f \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \\ = (\widehat {a} + \widehat {b} f) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + l (\widehat {c} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} - c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t}) + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} - l \mathbf {e} _ {t} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \pmb {\delta} _ {t + 1} = \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} - \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - (a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}) \mathbf {x} _ {t} - l \mathbf {e} _ {t} - \mathbf {w} _ {t} + (\widehat {b} - b _ {\star}) \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \\ = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \pmb {\delta} _ {t} + (\widehat {a} - a _ {\star} + l (\widehat {c} - c _ {\star})) \mathbf {x} _ {t} - l \mathbf {e} _ {t} - \mathbf {w} _ {t} + (\widehat {b} - b _ {\star}) \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

once again, changing variables, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t} = \widehat {c} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} + (\widehat {c} - c _ {\star}) \mathbf {x} _ {t} - \mathbf {e} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + f \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} + f \mathbf {x} _ {t}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/9f7cf36a6a8903776578e8a09d912c01a1723f33c5c7d52c2fe6465c61cf0fa2.jpg)

# c.5 expressivity of drc-ex

in this section generalize the expressivity guarantee of theorem 1 to our more general setting. to begin, let us define a notion of an operator which translates the dynamics under the nominal controller  $\pi_0$  to target dynamics  $\pi$ :

definition c.4. given a dynamical system  $\pi$ , we say that  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  is a  $\pi_0 \to \pi$  conversion operator if the following under dynamics induced by any noise sequence  $(\mathbf{w}_t, \mathbf{e}_t)$ : if  $(\mathbf{y}_t^\pi, \mathbf{u}_t^\pi)$  are the input-output sequence under  $\pi$  (lemma c.1), then the sequence defined by

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \rightarrow \pi_ {0}} := \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi} ^ {[ t - i ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}
$$

satisfies the following for all  $t$ :

$$
\left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\end{array}\right] = \left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\end{array}\right] + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ t - i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \rightarrow \pi_ {0}}.
$$

in other words, if one selects exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex},\pi \rightarrow \pi_0}$ , then one recovers the dynamics of the controller  $\pi$ . note that, it is enough to show that one recovers the dynamics of  $\mathbf{u}_t^\pi$ , since the inputs and noise to the system uniquely determine the dynamics of  $\mathbf{y}_t$  via eq. (1.1). with the above definition, we define our comparator class accordingly:

definition 2.3b (decay functions & policy class). given an ldc-ex  $\pi_0$ , we define the comparator class  $\pi(\psi)$  as the set of all  $\pi$  for which there exists a  $\pi_0 \to \pi$  conversion operator  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  which decay dominated by  $\psi$ : that is,  $\psi_{g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}}(n) \leq \psi(n)$  for all  $n$ . moreover, we define  $r_{g_\star} := 1 \vee \| g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta} \|_{\ell_1, \mathrm{op}} \vee \| g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)} \|_{\ell_1, \mathrm{op}}$ , and  $\psi_{g_\star}(n) := \max \{\psi_{g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)}}(n), \psi_{g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}}(n)\}$ .

with this definition in mind, theorem 1b follows by direct analogy to theorem 1:

theorem 1b. let  $\pi(\psi)$  be as in definition 2.3b,  $r_{\psi} = \psi(0)$ , and let  $j_t(m)$  of the strongly stabilized drc controller (definition 3.2b). given a proper decay function  $\psi$  and  $\pi \in \pi(\psi)$ , there exists an  $m \in \mathcal{m}(m, r_{\psi})$  such that

$$
j _ {t} (m) - j _ {t} (\pi) \leq 2 l t r _ {\psi} r _ {g _ {*}} ^ {2} r _ {\text {n a t}} ^ {2} \psi (m). \tag {c.3}
$$

proof. the proof is analogous to the first part of the proof of theorem 1, where the control approximation identity (claim 5.6) is built into the definition of  $\psi(\cdot)$  by assumption. we omit the proof in the interest of simplicity.

while quite general, theorem 1b guarantees competition with policies whose conversion operators  $(\pi(\psi))$  in definition 2.3b) have reasonable decay, and unlike theorem 1, it does not make this explicit. thus it remains to show that this class  $\pi(\psi)$  is reasonable expressive.

in what follows, we will show that an analogue holds in all of our examples. let's make this formal:

definition c.5 (convolution of markov operator). let  $g_{1} \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{1} \times d_{0}}$ , and  $g_{2} \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{0} \times d_{2}}$ . we define  $g = g_{1} \odot g_{2}$  as the operator

$$
g ^ {[ i ]} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {i} g _ {1} ^ {[ j ]} \cdot g _ {2} ^ {[ i - j ]}.
$$

theorem 10. for any policy  $\pi$ , the matrix  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  can be represented as follows:

1. if the system is internally stable (example c.1),  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi} = g_{\pi, \mathrm{cl}, e \to u}$ , for  $\pi_0$  which is identically zero.  
2. if the system is stabilized by static feedback  $\pi_0$  (example c.2),  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi} = \bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi} \circ g_{\pi_0, y \to (y, u)}$  is as detailed in proposition c.1 since a static controller is internally stable too, with  $a_{\pi_0} = 0$ . furthermore, since  $\rho(a_{\pi_0, \mathrm{cl}}) = \rho(a_\star + b_\star k c_\star) < 1$ , both  $\bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  and  $g_{\pi_0, y \to (y, u)}$  exhibit geometric decay.  
3. if the system is stabilized by internally stable feedback (example c.3),  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi} = \bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi} \circ g_{\pi_0, y \to (y, u)}$  is as detailed in proposition c.1. in particular, both  $\bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  and  $g_{\pi_0, y \to (y, u)}$  exhibit geometric decay as long as  $\pi$  is stabilizing, since  $\rho(a_{\pi_0}) < 1$  and  $\rho(a_{\pi_0, \mathrm{cl}}) < 1$  
4. if the system is stabilized by exact observer feedback (example c.4), the  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  is as detailed in proposition c.2. the latter exhibits geometric decay as long as  $\pi$  is stabilizing.  
5. if the system is stabilized by inexact observer feedback (example c.5), then  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  is as proposition c.3 details. in particular, it is a convolution of three markov operators of stable systems, as long as  $\max \{ \rho(a_\star + b_\star f), \rho(\widehat{a} + \widehat{b}f), \rho(a_\star + lc_\star), \rho(\widehat{a} + l\widehat{c}) \} < 1$ .

in each of the above cases,  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  is either the markov operator of a stable system, or can be expressed by a convolution of two (example c.3) or three (example c.3) markov operators of stable systems.

specifically, we show that there we can represent  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  as an convolution of stable transfer operators. since a convolution of operators with geometric decay itself has geometric decay, we find that we obtain the same expressive power as in the stable system case.

# c.5.1 expressivity of internally stable feedback

let us begin by defining a closed form expression for the  $\pi_0\rightarrow \pi$  operator that arises under internally stable feedback:

definition c.6 (internally stable dynamical system conversion). given a nominal controller  $\pi_0$  given by  $(a_{\pi_0},b_{\pi_0},c_{\pi_0},d_{\pi_0})$ , and a target controller  $\pi$  given by  $(a_{\pi},b_{\pi},c_{\pi},d_{\pi})$ , and recalling the closed loop matrix  $a_{\pi ,\mathrm{cl}}$  from lemma c.1, define the matrices  $a_{\pi_0\to \pi},b_{\pi_0\to \pi},c_{\pi_0\to \pi}$  by

$$
a _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} a _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}} & 0 \\ & 0 \\ \hline b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & 0 & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right], \quad b _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} & - b _ {\star} \\ b _ {\pi} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{array} \right],
$$

$$
c _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi} := \left[\begin{array}{l l l}(d _ {\pi} - d _ {\pi_ {0}}) c _ {\star}&c _ {\pi}&- c _ {\pi_ {0}}\end{array}\right]
$$

and  $d_{\pi_0 \to \pi} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc} d_{\pi} & 0 \end{array} \right]$  define  $\bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi} := \mathsf{transfer}(a_{\pi_0 \to \pi}, b_{\pi_0 \to \pi}, c_{\pi_0 \to \pi}, d_{\pi_0 \to \pi})$ , and define:

$$
g _ {\pi_ {0}, y \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c} i \\ d _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i \geq 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ c _ {\pi_ {0}} a _ {\pi_ {0}} ^ {i - 1} b _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right].
$$

finally, we define the  $\pi_0\rightarrow \pi$  conversion operator

$$
g _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} ^ {[ i ]} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {i} \bar {g} _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} ^ {[ i - j ]} g _ {\pi_ {0}, y \to (y, u)} ^ {[ j ]}.
$$

proposition c.1. for any stabilizing  $\pi$  and internally stable  $\pi_0$ , the markov operator  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$  defined in definition c.6 is the convolution of two stable markov operators, and is a  $\pi_0 \to \pi$  conversion operator. that is, for all  $t$ , the exogenous inputs

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \rightarrow \pi_ {0}} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} \bar {g} _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {y} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}.
$$

produce the input-output pairs  $(\mathbf{y}_t^\pi, \mathbf{u}_t^\pi)$  via

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \to \pi_ {0}}.
$$

# c.5.2 expressivity of observer-feedback (youla parametrization)

proposition c.2. define the matrices

$$
a _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} := a _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}}, \quad b _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} - l \\ b _ {\star} \end{array} \right], \quad c _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} = \left[ d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} - f \right], \quad d _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} = d _ {\pi}
$$

then,

$$
g _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} = \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi}, b _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi}, c _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi}, d _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi}\right)
$$

is a  $\pi_0\to \pi$  conversion operator for the youla ldc-ex of example c.4. that is

$$
\left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}\end{array}\right] = \left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\end{array}\right] + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \rightarrow \pi_ {0}} \quad f o r \quad \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \pi \rightarrow \pi_ {0}} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {s - 1} g _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ i ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {s - i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}.
$$

the statement of the youla parametrization is standard, though varies source-to-source. we use the expression in cite megretski [2004, theorem 10.1].

# c.5.3 expressivity of approximative observer-feedback (drc-youla parametrization)

proposition c.3. let  $g_{\mathrm{yla},\pi}$  be as in proposition c.2, and define  $\bar{g}_{\mathrm{yla},\pi} \in \mathcal{g}^{d_u + d_y\times d_y}$  via

$$
\bar {g} _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ i ]} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} g _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ i ]} \\ i _ {d _ {y}} \cdot \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \end{array} \right].
$$

further, define the operators

$$
g _ {\rightarrow \star} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(\left[\begin{array}{c c}a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f&0\\\widehat {b} f - l c _ {\star}&\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}b _ {\star}&\widehat {b}\\l&l\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}f&- f\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}i&0\end{array}\right]\right)
$$

$$
g _ {\star \rightarrow :} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(\left[\begin{array}{c c}a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}&b _ {\star} f - l \widehat {c}\\0&\widehat {a} + \widehat {b} f\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}l\\l\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}c _ {\star}&- \widehat {c}\end{array}\right], i\right).
$$

then, the transfer operator  $g_{\pi_0 \to \pi} \coloneqq g_{\colon \to \star} \odot \bar{g}_{\mathrm{yla}, \pi} \odot g_{\star \to \star}$  is a  $\pi_0 \to \pi$  conversion operator for the approximate youla lcd-ex of example c.5.

# c.6 proofs of expressivity guarantees

# c.6.1 proof of proposition c.1

define

$$
\mathbf {w} _ {\pi_ {0}, t} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & b _ {\star} d _ {\pi_ {0}} \\ 0 & b _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t} \end{array} \right], \quad \mathbf {e} _ {\pi_ {0}, t} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} i \\ d _ {\pi} \end{array} \right] \mathbf {e} _ {t}
$$

from the closed loop matrices  $a_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}, c_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}}$  described in lemma c.1, the nominal system with exogenous inputs is then described by the equations

$$
\bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {x} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {\overset {.} {s}} _ {t} \end{array} \right] = a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ 0 \end{array} \right]} _ {b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} + \mathbf {w} _ {\pi_ {0}, t}
$$

$$
\bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} := \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {y} _ {t} \\ \mathring {\mathbf {u}} _ {t} \end{array} \right] = c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + \mathbf {e} _ {\pi_ {0}, t}. \tag {c.4}
$$

we then put eq. (c.4) in feedback with the following system via  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}} = \mathbf{u}_t^{\delta}$ :

$$
\mathbf {a} _ {t + 1} ^ {\delta} = a _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} b _ {\pi} & 0 \end{array} \right]} _ {:= \bar {b} _ {\pi}} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t}
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = c _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + \underbrace {\left[ d _ {\pi} - i \right]} _ {:= \bar {d} _ {\pi}} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t}. \tag {c.5}
$$

then, the joint dynamics of eqs. (c.5) and (c.4) are given by

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} \\ \mathbf {a} _ {t + 1} ^ {\delta} \end{array} \right] = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} + b _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} & b _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} c _ {\pi} \\ \bar {b} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} & a _ {\pi .} \end{array} \right]} _ {:= a _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} \\ \mathbf {a} _ {t + 1} ^ {\delta} \end{array} \right] + \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & b _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} \\ 0 & \bar {b} _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {b _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {\pi_ {0}, t} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {\pi_ {0}, t} \end{array} \right]
$$

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{l} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} \end{array} \right] = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} & 0 \\ \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0} , \mathrm {c l}} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]} _ {c _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}}} \left[ \begin{array}{l} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{l} i \\ \bar {d} _ {\pi} \end{array} \right] \mathbf {e} _ {\pi_ {0}, t}. \tag {c.6}
$$

first, we claim that, for all  $t$ , the system eq. (c.6) yields inputs an outputs equivalent to  $\mathbf{u}_t^\pi, \mathbf{u}_t^{\pi}$ :

lemma c.4. let  $\bar{\mathbf{y}}_t = (\mathbf{y}_t, \mathring{\mathbf{u}}_t)$  and  $\mathbf{u}_t^\delta$  be as given by eq. (c.6). then, for  $\mathbf{u}_t^\delta$  defined above,

$$
\forall t, \mathbf {y} _ {t} = \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} a n d \mathring {\mathbf {u}} _ {t} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}.
$$

in particular,  $\left[ \begin{array}{l}\mathbf{y}_t^\pi \\ \mathbf{u}_t^\pi \end{array} \right] = \sum_{i = 1}^t g_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl},u\to (y,u)}^{[i]}\mathbf{u}_t^\delta .$

proof. let us consider the update of the state  $\mathbf{x}_t$ :  $\mathbf{x}_{t + 1} = a_{\star}\mathbf{x}_{t + 1} + b_{\star}(\mathbf{\dot{u}}_t + \mathbf{u}_t^\delta) + \mathbf{w}_t = a_{\star}\mathbf{x}_{t + 1} + b_{\star}c_{\pi}\mathbf{a}_t^\delta +$ $b_{\star}d_{\pi}\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{en}} + \mathbf{w}_t$ . first, note that

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} = \mathring {\mathbf {u}} _ {t} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = d _ {\pi} \mathbf {y} _ {t} + c _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + d \pi \mathbf {e} _ {t}.
$$

thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} = a _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} + \mathbf {\delta} \mathbf {\dot {u}} _ {t} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star}\right) \mathbf {x} _ {t} + b _ {\star} c _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} \mathbf {e} _ {t} + \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {a} _ {t + 1} ^ {\delta} = a _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + b _ {\pi} \mathbf {y} _ {t} = a _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {e} _ {t} \\ = a _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + \left(b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} +\right) \mathbf {x} _ {t} + b _ {\pi} \mathbf {e} _ {t}. \\ \end{array}
$$

thus,  $(\mathbf{x}_t,\mathbf{a}_t^\delta)$  have the same evolution as  $(\mathbf{x}_t^\pi ,\mathbf{a}_t^\pi)$ , where  $\mathbf{a}_t^\pi$  is the internal state of the system when placed in feedback with  $\pi$ . thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {y} _ {t} = c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + \mathbf {e} _ {t} = c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\pi} + \mathbf {e} _ {t} = \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi} \\ \mathbf {\hat {u}} _ {t} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + c _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\delta} + d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {e} _ {t} = d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\pi} + c _ {\pi} \mathbf {a} _ {t} ^ {\pi} + d _ {\pi} \mathbf {e} _ {t} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi}. \\ \end{array}
$$

next, we show that  $\mathbf{u}_t^\delta$  can be represented as a linear function of the sequence  $\bar{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\pi_0} = (\mathbf{y}^{\pi_0},\mathbf{u}^{\pi_0})$ :

claim c.5. define

$$
c _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}, u} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right], \quad b _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}, e} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} \\ \bar {b} _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]
$$

then, the matrices  $n^{[0]} = \bar{d}_{\pi}$ ,  $n^{[i]} = c_{\delta \pi, \mathrm{cl}, u} a_{\delta \pi, \mathrm{cl}}^{i-1} b_{\delta \pi, \mathrm{cl}, e}$  satisfy

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} n ^ {[ i ]} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t - i} ^ {\pi_ {0}}, \quad w h e r e \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {s} ^ {\pi_ {0}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi_ {0}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right].
$$

proof. analogous to claim 5.6, and the fact that, in the absence of  $\mathbf{u}_t^\delta$ ,  $\bar{\mathbf{y}}_t = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{y}_t^{\pi_0} \\ \mathbf{u}_t^{\pi_0} \end{bmatrix}$ .

let us now show that  $n^{[i]}$  is given by  $\bar{g}_{\pi_0\to \pi}$ :

claim c.6. for all  $i \geq 0$ ,  $n^{[i]} = \bar{g}_{\pi_0 \to \pi}^{[i]}$ . as a consequence,

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} \bar {g} _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi} ^ {[ i ]} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t - i} ^ {\pi_ {0}}, \quad w h e r e \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {s} ^ {\pi_ {0}} = \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\pi_ {0}}, \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\pi_ {0}}\right).
$$

proof. by definition  $\bar{d}_{\pi} = d_{\pi_0 \to \pi}$ . to establish the identity, define the block permutation matrix  $t$ , where the blocks correspond to the  $\mathbf{x}_t, \mathring{\mathbf{s}}_t, \mathbf{a}_t^\delta$  states:

$$
t = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} i & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & i \\ 0 & i & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

since  $t^2 = i$ , it suffices to show that

$$
t a _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} t = a _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi}, \quad t b _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} = b _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi}, \quad t c _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} = c _ {\pi_ {0} \rightarrow \pi}.
$$

recall that

$$
a _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} + b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l , i n}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} & b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l , i n}} c _ {\pi} \\ \bar {b} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} & a _ {\pi}. \end{array} \right]
$$

we begin with

$$
\begin{array}{l} a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} + b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l , i n}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & b _ {\star} c _ {\pi_ {0}} \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ 0 \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} d _ {\pi} & - i \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & 0 \\ d _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & c _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] \\ = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & b _ {\star} c _ {\pi_ {0}} \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\star} \\ 0 \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} (d _ {\pi} - d _ {\pi_ {0}}) c _ {\star} & - c _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] \\ = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & 0 \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, recalling  $\bar{b}_{\pi} = [b_{\pi}\mid 0]$ , we have

$$
\bar {b} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} b _ {\pi} d _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & 0 \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{l l} (d _ {\pi} - d _ {\pi_ {0}}) c _ {\star} & - c _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & 0 \end{array} \right]
$$

finally, since  $b_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl,in}}c_{\pi} = \left[ \begin{array}{c}b_{\star}c_{\pi}\\ 0 \end{array} \right]$ , we have

$$
a _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} + b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}} d _ {\delta \pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} & b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}} c _ {\pi} \\ b _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}} & a _ {\pi}. \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & 0 & b _ {\star} c _ {\pi} \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi_ {0}} & 0 \\ b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & 0 & a _ {\pi} \end{array} \right]
$$

thus,

$$
t a _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}} t = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & b _ {\star} c _ {\pi} & 0 \\ b _ {\pi} c _ {\star} & a _ {\pi} & 0 \\ b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & 0 & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} a _ {\pi , \mathrm {c l}} & 0 \\ \hline b _ {\pi_ {0}} c _ {\star} & 0 & a _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] := a _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi}
$$

now, recall  $c_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc}c_\star & 0\\ d_{\pi_0}c_\star & c_\pi \end{array} \right]$  and  $b_{\pi_0,\mathrm{cl}} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc}i & b_\star d_{\pi_0}\\ 0 & b_{\pi_0} \end{array} \right]$ . then,

$$
\begin{array}{l} c _ {\delta \pi , \operatorname {c l}, u} t := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \bar {d} _ {\pi} c _ {\pi_ {0}, \operatorname {c l}} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right] t \\ := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} (d _ {\pi} - d _ {\pi_ {0}}) c _ {\star} & - c _ {\pi_ {0}} & c _ {\pi} \end{array} \right] t \\ := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} (d _ {\pi} - d _ {\pi_ {0}}) c _ {\star} & c _ {\pi} & - c _ {\pi_ {0}} \end{array} \right] = c _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array}{l} t b _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}, e} := t b _ {\delta \pi , \mathrm {c l}, e} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {i n}} \bar {d} _ {\pi} \\ \bar {b} _ {\pi} \end{array} \right] t \\ := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} & - b _ {\star} \\ 0 & 0 \\ b _ {\pi} & 0 \end{array} \right] t := \left[ \begin{array}{c c} b _ {\star} d _ {\pi} & - b _ {\star} \\ b _ {\pi} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{array} \right] = b _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/5135bea39d4f58c628afc4d750258ef980397ec027657d1523c6a0fb56a988bf.jpg)

we conclude the proof by showing that  $\bar{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\pi_0} = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\pi_0},\mathbf{u}_t^{\pi_0})$  can be represented in terms of  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\pi_0}$ :

claim c.7. recall  $g_{\pi_0}^{[i]} = \mathbb{i}_{i=0}d_{\pi_0} + \mathbb{i}_{i\geq 1}c_{\pi_0}a_{\pi_0}^{i-1}(b_{\pi_0} + b_{\pi_0,u}d_{\pi_0})$ . then,  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\pi_0} = \sum_{i=1}^t g_{\pi_0}^{[i]}\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{\pi_0}$  as a consequence,

$$
\bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t} ^ {\pi_ {0}} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\pi_ {0}, y \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {y} _ {t - i} ^ {\pi_ {0}}
$$

proof. directly from the ldc equations.

in sum,

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} ^ {[ i ]} \bar {\mathbf {y}} _ {t - i} ^ {\pi_ {0}} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {t - i - 1} \bar {g} _ {\pi_ {0} \to \pi} ^ {[ i ]} g _ {\pi_ {0}, y \to (y, u)} ^ {[ j ]} \mathbf {y} _ {t - i - j} ^ {\pi_ {0}},
$$

which concludes the proof.

# c.6.2 proof of proposition c.3

consider the system

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\star} = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} = c _ {\star} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {t + 1} ^ {\delta \pi} = a _ {\delta \pi} \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} + b _ {\delta \pi} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} = c _ {\delta \pi} \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} + d _ {\delta \pi} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} = f \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi}. \tag {c.7} \\ \end{array}
$$

from proposition c.1, the inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t$  coincide with  $\mathbf{u}_t^\pi$  for all  $t\geq 1$ . thus, if we set  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}} = f(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}_t^\star -\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_t) + \mathbf{u}_t^{\delta \pi}$ , the system

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} = f \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} = \widehat {c} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} \tag {c.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

also generates  $\mathbf{u}_t = \mathbf{u}_t^\pi$ . now, let us represent the above as a system with inputs  $\boldsymbol{\eta}_t^\star, \mathbf{u}_t^{\delta \pi}$ . we shall show that these can all be represented in terms of  $\hat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ , concluding the proof.

first, we write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\star} = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} + l \left(\mathbf {y} _ {t} - \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star}\right) + b _ {\star} f \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} - l c _ {\star} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + b _ {\star} f \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use the fact that  $\pmb{\eta}_t^\star = c_\star \tilde{\mathbf{x}}_t^\star -\mathbf{y}_t$  . next, we write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} \\ = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} + \widehat {b} f \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} \\ = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \widehat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} + (\widehat {b} f - l c _ {\star}) \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} + \widehat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last line we use  $\pmb{\eta}_t^\star = c_\star \tilde{\pmb{x}}_t^\star -\mathbf{y}_t$  . this gives that

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\star} \\ \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f & 0 \\ \widehat {b} f - l c _ {\star} & \widehat {a} + l \widehat {c} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} \\ \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c c} b _ {\star} & \widehat {b} \\ l & l \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} \end{array} \right]
$$

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} f & - f \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\star} \\ \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i & 0 \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star} \end{array} \right].
$$

thus, letting

$$
g _ {\rightarrow \star} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(\left[\begin{array}{c c}a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f&0\\\widehat {b} f - l c _ {\star}&\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}b _ {\star}&\widehat {b}\\l&l\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}f&- f\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}i&0\end{array}\right]\right)
$$

denote the transfer operator mapping  $\left[ \begin{array}{c}\mathbf{u}_t^{\delta \pi}\\ \boldsymbol {\eta}_t^\star \end{array} \right]\to \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  , we can render

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} g _ {\hat {\mathbf {\gamma}} \rightarrow \star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\delta \pi}\\\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star}\end{array}\right].
$$

next, for  $g_{\mathrm{yla},\pi} \coloneqq \mathrm{transfer}(a_{\mathrm{yla},\pi}, b_{\mathrm{yla},\pi}, c_{\mathrm{yla},\pi}, d_{\mathrm{yla},\pi})$  from proposition c.2, we have

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\delta \pi} = \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} g _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ s - j ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {j} ^ {\star},
$$

giving that, for  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  defined in eq. (c.8),

$$
\forall t, \quad \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} g _ {\hat {\tau} \rightarrow \star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \bar {g} _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ s - j ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star}., \quad \bar {g} _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ i ]} = \left[\begin{array}{l}g _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ i ]}\\i _ {d _ {y}} \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0}\end{array}\right] \tag {c.9}
$$

to conclude, let us represent  $\pmb{\eta}_t^\star$  in terms of  $\widehat{\pmb{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . here, we use the crucial fact that the dynamics of  $\pmb{\eta}_t^\star$  are non-counterfactual. thus, let us instead consider the following "natural" dynamics:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {x} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = a _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \mathbf {w} _ {t} \\ \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} = c _ {\star} \mathbf {x} _ {t} + \mathbf {e} _ {t} \\ \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = (\hat {a} + l \hat {c}) \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \hat {b} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = f \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} := \widehat {c} \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star , \mathrm {n a t}} := c _ {\star} \bar {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \end{array}
$$

from lemma c.2, the  $\eta$ -dynamics under exact observer feedback do not depend on the exogenous inputs; thus,  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\star,\mathrm{nat}} = \pmb{\eta}_t^\star$  for all  $t$ , where  $\pmb{\eta}^{\star,\mathrm{nat}}$  is defined in eq. (c.7). next, we can substitute

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - l \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + b _ {\star} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - l \widehat {c} \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + b _ {\star} f \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \left(a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}\right) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \left(b _ {\star} f - l \widehat {c}\right) \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \\ \end{array}
$$

furthermore, we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = (\widehat {a} + l \widehat {c}) \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - l \widehat {c} \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \widehat {b} f \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = (\widehat {a} + \widehat {b} f) \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + l \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

thus,

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t + 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star} & b _ {\star} f - l \widehat {c} \\ 0 & \widehat {a} + \widehat {b} f \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c} l \\ l \end{array} \right] \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}
$$

moreover,

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star , \text {n a t}} = c _ {\star} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} - \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} = c _ {\star} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} - \widehat {c} \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}},
$$

or in matrix form

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\star , \mathrm {n a t}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c _ {\star} & - \widehat {c} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \tilde {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \hat {\mathbf {x}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] + i \cdot \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}.
$$

hence, defining

$$
g _ {\star \rightarrow :} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(\left[\begin{array}{c c}a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}&b _ {\star} f - l \widehat {c}\\0&\widehat {a} + \widehat {b} f\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c}l\\l\end{array}\right], \left[\begin{array}{c c}c _ {\star}&- \widehat {c}\end{array}\right], i\right)
$$

as the  $\pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\rightarrow \pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  transfer operator, we see that

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {j} ^ {\star} = \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {j} ^ {\star , \mathrm {n a t}} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {j} g _ {\star \rightarrow \hat {\cdot}} ^ {[ j - i ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}.
$$

thus, from eq. (c.9), the exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  from eq. (c.8) satisfy

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {j} g _ {\hat {\imath} \rightarrow \star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \bar {g} _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} ^ {[ s - j ]} g _ {\star \rightarrow \hat {\imath}} ^ {[ j - i ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \\ = \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t} \left(g _ {: \rightarrow \star} \odot \bar {g} _ {\mathrm {y l a}, \pi} \odot g _ {\star \rightarrow :}\right) ^ {[ t - s ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  induces the desired inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^\pi$ , the proposition follows.

![](images/2b61c40546d9c6fca10799ab11eb4e40e29e9fd6d571a187bef3dce34053556f.jpg)

# d regret analysis: non-stochastic

while the theorems in the main paper hold for stable systems, the stated proofs and claims here hold for the more general setting of stabilizable systems, with the following modifications:

definition d.1 (modifications for the stabilized case). the following modifications are made for the stabilized setting of appendix c:

1. we are given access to a stabilizing controller satisfying assumption 1b  
2. we replace assumption 1 with assumption 1b.  
3.  $\psi_{g_\star}$  and  $r_{g_\star}$  are defined as in definition 2.3b.  
4. we replace algorithm 1 with algorithm 4, and algorithm 2 with algorithm 5.

(where we are granted access to a sub-optimal stabilizing controller).

# d.1 omitted proofs from section 5

in this section, we present all omitted proofs from section 5, and demonstrate that all bounds either hold verbatim in the more general stabilized system setting, or present generalizations thereof. this ensures that theorem 2 holds verbatim in the more general setting as well. before continuing, let us review some of the notation from the stabilized setting, and how the stable system setting can be recovered:

- we use  $\mathbf{v} = (\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u})\in \mathbb{r}^{d_y + d_u}$  to denote the pair of outputs and inputs on which the loss is measured. in particular,  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}} = (\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}})$ , and  $\mathbf{v}^m = (\mathbf{y}_t^m,\mathbf{u}_t^m)$ .  
- the exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  reduce to the inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t$  in the stable case.  
- the exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}$  are linear in  $\boldsymbol{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$  or estimates  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ ; in the stable case, these correspond to  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}, \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ .

next, we note that the regret decomposition is the same as in the stable case, given by eq. 5.3. we begin with a magnitude bound that generalizes lemma 5.1:

lemma 5.1b (magnitude bound). recall the notation with variants  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}},\mathbf{v}^m$  . for all  $t$  , and  $m,m_1,m_2,\dots \in$ $\mathcal{m}$  , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \max \left\{\left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} \right\| _ {2}, \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, m} \right\| _ {2}, \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m _ {t} \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| _ {2} \right\} \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \max \left\{\left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right]\right\|, \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \right\|, \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {m} \right\|\right\} \leq 2 r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. the proofs of all these bounds are similar; let us focus on the  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}}$ ,  $\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  sequence. we have  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}} := \sum_{s=t-h\vee 0}^{t}\mathbf{m}_t^{[t-s]}\mathbf{y}_s^{\mathrm{nat}}$ , from which holder's inequality implies  $\| \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}}\|_2 \leq r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}$ . then,  $\| \mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}\| = \| \mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} + \sum_{s=1}^{t}g_{\mathrm{ex}\to u}^{[t-s]}\mathbf{u}_s^{\mathrm{ex,alg}}\|_2 \leq r_{\mathrm{nat}} + \| g_{\mathrm{ex}\to u}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\| \mathbf{u}_s^{\mathrm{alg}}\|_2 \leq r_{\mathrm{nat}} + r_{g_\star}r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}} \leq 2r_{g_\star}r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}$ , since  $r_{\mathcal{m}}, r_{g_\star} \geq 1$ .

we now restate the burn-in bound, which can be checked to hold in the more general present setting:

lemma 5.2. we have that (burn-in loss)  $\leq 4lr_{g_{\star}}^{2}r_{\mathcal{m}}^{2}r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}(m + h)$

we now turn to the truncation costs (lemma 5.3):

lemma 5.3 (bound on truncation errors). we can bound

(algorithm truncation error) + (comparator truncation error)  $\leq 4ltr_{g_{\star}}r_{\mathcal{m}}^{2}r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}\psi_{g_{\star}}(h + 1)$ .

proof. let us bound the algorithm truncation cost; the comparator cost is similar. note that  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex,alg}} = \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathbf{m}_t)$ . by the magnitude bound (lemma 5.1b) and lipschitz assumption,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = m + h + 1} ^ {t} \ell_ {t} (\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}, \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}) - \sum_ {t = m + h + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} [ \mathbf {m} _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\star}, \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] \leq \\ = 2 l r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\psi} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \sum_ {t = m + h + 1} ^ {t} \left\| \left[\begin{array}{c c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} [ \mathbf {m} _ {t: t - h}&g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] - \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} [ \mathbf {m} _ {t: t - h}&g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}\end{array}\right] \right\| _ {2} \\ = 2 l r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\psi} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \sum_ {t = m + h + 1} ^ {t} \left\|\left[ \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {t - h - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ t - s ]} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \right]\right\| _ {2} \\ \leq 2 l t r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\psi} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \max _ {s \in [ t ]} \| \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} \| _ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

by lemma 5.1b, the above is at most  $2ltr_{g_{\star}}r_{\psi}^{2}r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}\psi_{g_{\star}}(h + 1)$ .

![](images/66deb0fa9adb8fc84c32ef38a1d264c185818f4bc1a20783261615a63733301c.jpg)

next, we turn to bounding the lipschitz constants. for this, we shall need the following bound:

lemma d.1 (norm relations). for any  $m \in \mathcal{k}$ , we have

$$
\| m \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {m} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \quad a n d \quad \max  _ {i = 0} ^ {h} \| m _ {t - i} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {m} \| m _ {t: t - h} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}
$$

proof. the first inequality follows from cauchy schwartz:

$$
\| m \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \| m ^ {[ i - 1 ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \| m ^ {[ i - 1 ]} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \sqrt {m} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}}.
$$

the second follows from using the first to bound

$$
\max _ {i = 0} ^ {h} \| m _ {t - i} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \max _ {i} \sqrt {m} \| m _ {t - i} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq \sqrt {m} \| m _ {t: t - h} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}.
$$

as a second intermediate step, we show that the maps  $m \mapsto \mathbf{y}_t(m \mid g_\star, \mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  and  $m \mapsto \mathbf{u}_t(m \mid g_\star, \mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}, \mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  are lipschitz:

lemma d.2 (lipschitz bound on coordinate mappings). for any  $m, \tilde{m} \in \mathcal{m}(m,r)$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} (\tilde {m} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq r _ {\text {n a t}} \| m - \tilde {m} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \leq \sqrt {m} r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\text {n a t}} \| m - \tilde {m} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly, for any  $m_{t:t - h},\tilde{m}_{t:t - h}\in \mathcal{m}(m,r)^{h + 1}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} [ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] - \mathbf {v} _ {t} [ \tilde {m} _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] \right\|, \\ \leq \sqrt {m} r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \max _ {s = t - h} ^ {t} \| m _ {s} - \tilde {m} _ {s} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. let us prove the bound for  $\| \mathbf{v}_t(m\mid g_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)},\pmb{\eta}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}) - \mathbf{v}_t(m'\mid g_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)},\pmb{\eta}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}})\| _2$ , for time varying  $m_{t:t - h}$  and  $\tilde{m}_{t:t - h}$  are similar. we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {v} _ {t} [ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] - \mathbf {v} _ {t} [ \tilde {m} _ {t: t - h} \| _ {2} = \left\| \sum_ {s = t - h} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} \left(\mathbf {u} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} s) - \mathbf {u} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\tilde {m} \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}})\right)\right\| \\ \leq r _ {g _ {\star}} \max  _ {s = t - h} ^ {t - 1} \left\| \mathbf {u} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \mathbf {u} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\tilde {m} \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} s) \right\| \\ = r _ {g _ {\star}} \max  _ {s = t - h} ^ {t - 1} \left\| \sum_ {j = s - m + 1} ^ {s} (m - \tilde {m}) ^ {[ s - j ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq r _ {\text {n a t}} r _ {g _ {\star}} \left\| m - \tilde {m} \right\| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \leq \sqrt {m} r _ {\text {n a t}} r _ {g _ {\star}} \left\| m - \tilde {m} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step uses lemma d.1.

we now present and prove the generalization of lemma 5.4 to the stabilized setting:

lemma 5.4b. define  $l_{f}\coloneqq l\sqrt{m} r_{\mathrm{nat}}^{2}r_{g_{\star}}^{2}r_{\mathcal{m}}$ . then, the functions  $f_{t}(\cdot \mid g_{\star},\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$  are  $l_{f}$ -lipschitz, and  $f_{t}[m_{t:t - h}\mid g_{\star},\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}]$  are  $l_{f}$ -coordinate-wise lipschitz on  $\mathcal{m}$  in the frobenius norm  $\| m\|_{\mathrm{f}} = \| [m^{[0]},\dots ,m^{[m - 1]}\| ]\|_{\mathrm{f}}$ . moreover, the euclidean diameter of  $\mathcal{m}$  is at most  $d\leq 2\sqrt{d} r_{\mathcal{m}}$ .

proof. let us bound the coordinate-lipschitz constant of  $f_{t}$ , the bound for  $f_{t}$  is similar.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| f _ {t} [ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \pmb {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] - f _ {t} [ \tilde {m} _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \pmb {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} ] \right| \\ = \left| \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \right]\right) - \ell_ {t} \left(\mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ \tilde {m} _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \right]\right)\right| \\ \leq l r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right] - \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ \tilde {m} _ {t: t - h} \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right]\right\| \\ \leq \sqrt {m} l r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} r _ {\mathcal {m}} \left(\max  _ {s \in [ t - h: t ]} \| m _ {s} - \tilde {m} _ {s} \| _ {\mathrm {f}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is by lemma d.2. the bound on the diameter of  $\mathcal{m}$  follows from lemma d.1

![](images/5d346ec0f8e095d2cae133961cd2f9c299e1f80286e0d4c6aa0318cf1dc23ef4.jpg)

# d.2 estimation bounds: proof of theorems 6 & 6b

we state a generalization of proof of theorems 6 for estimating both respose  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}$  and  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)}$ :

theorem 6b (guarantee for algorithm 5, generalization of theorem 6). let  $\delta \in (e^{-t}, t^{-1})$ ,  $n, d_u \leq t$ , and  $\psi_{g_\star}(h + 1) \leq \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}$ . define  $d_{\max} = \max \{d_y + d_u, d_\eta\}$ , and set

$$
\epsilon_ {g} (n, \delta) = \frac {h ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}}}{\sqrt {n}} c _ {\delta}, \quad w h e r e c _ {\delta} := 1 4 \sqrt {d _ {\mathrm {m a x}} + d _ {y} + \log \frac {1}{\delta}}, \quad a n d r _ {\mathbf {u , e s t}} := 3 \sqrt {d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)}.
$$

and suppose that  $n \geq h^4 c_{\delta}^2 r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}^2 r_{\mathcal{m}}^2 r_{g_\star}^2 + c_0 h^2 d_u^2$  for an appropriately large  $c_0$ , which can be satisfied by taking

$$
n \geq 1 7 6 4 (d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)) ^ {2} h ^ {4} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} r _ {g _ {\star}} ^ {2} + c _ {0} h ^ {2} d _ {u} ^ {2}.
$$

then with probability  $1 - \delta - n^{-\log^2 n}$ , algorithm 5 satisfies the following bounds

1.  $\epsilon_{g} \leq 1 / \max \{r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}, r_{\mathcal{m}} r_{g_{\star}}\}$ .  
2. for all  $t\in [n]$ $\| \mathbf{u}_t\| \leq r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}\coloneqq 3\sqrt{d_u + \log(1 / \delta)}$  
3. for estimation error is bounded as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} - g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ 0: h ]} - g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} ^ {[ 0: h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} + r _ {\mathbf {u}, \mathrm {e s t}} \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \leq \epsilon_ {g} \\ \| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} - g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ 1: h ]} - g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ 1: h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} + r _ {\mathbf {u}, \mathrm {e s t}} \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \leq \epsilon_ {g}. \\ \end{array}
$$

moreover, algorithm 2 satisfies the same for  $\widehat{g}, g_{\star}$ .

proof. let us focus on algorithm 5, the bound for algorithm 2 is the special case of  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)} = \widehat{g}$  and  $g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)} = g_{\star}$ .

the first bound of the lemma is strictly numerical. lets prove the second part of the lemma. using standard gaussian concentration (see e.g. vershynin [2018, section 4.2]):

claim d.3. with probability  $1 - \delta / 3$  and  $\delta \leq 1 / t \leq 1 / n$  and  $\delta \leq 1 / 3$ ,  $\| \mathbf{u}_t \| \leq \frac{4}{3} \sqrt{2} \sqrt{d_u \log 9 + \log (3n / \delta)} \leq r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}} := 3 \sqrt{d_u + \log(1 / \delta)}$  for all  $t \in [n]$ . denote this event  $\mathcal{e}^{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{bound}}$ .

let us turn to the last part of the lemma. to begin, let us bound the truncation error. we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {2}{\epsilon_ {g}} \cdot r _ {\mathbf {u}, \mathrm {e s t}} \| g _ {\star} ^ {[ > h ]} - \widehat {g} ^ {[ > h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \frac {2 r _ {\mathbf {u} , \mathrm {e s t}}}{h ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} c _ {\delta} \sqrt {n}} \\ \leq \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \frac {1}{h ^ {2} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \sqrt {n}} \\ \leq \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \leq 1 \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second inequality uses  $c_{\delta} \geq 2r_{\mathbf{u,est}}$ , the thir uses  $h^2 r_{\mathrm{nat}} \geq 1$ , and the four holds from our choice of  $\psi_{g_\star}(h + 1)$ . hence,

$$
r _ {\mathbf {u}, \text {e s t}} \| g _ {\star} ^ {[ > h ]} - \widehat {g} ^ {[ > h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \epsilon_ {g} / 2 \leq 1 / 6 \tag {d.1}
$$

, where the last step uses part 1 of the lemma.

let us now bound the estimation error. we begin by bounding  $\| \widehat{g}^{[0:h]} - g_{\star}^{[0:h]}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . to this end, define  $\pmb{\delta}_{t} = \mathbf{v}_{t} - \sum_{i=1}^{h} g_{\star}^{[i]}\mathbf{u}_{t}$ , and define  $\pmb{\delta} = [\pmb{\delta}_{n_1}^{\top}|\dots|\pmb{\delta}_n^{\top}]$ . simchowitz et al. [2019] develop error bounds in terms of the operator norm of  $\pmb{\delta}$ . in the subs subsection below, we provide a simplified and self-contained proof of the estimation guarantees from simchowitz et al. [2019]:

lemma d.4 (simplification of proposition 3.2 in simchowitz et al. [2019]). then, if  $n$  is sufficiently large that  $n \geq chd_u\log^4(n)$  for some universal constant  $c > 0$ , and  $\mathcal{e}_{\lambda}$  is the event that  $\|\delta\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \lambda$ , then, with probability  $1 - n^{-\log^2(n)} - \delta/4$

$$
\| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {l s}} - g _ {\star} ^ {[ 1: h ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {5 . 6}{n} \lambda \sqrt {(h + 1) (d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (h / \delta))}.
$$

in particular, for  $h \geq 2$ , we have the simplifid bound

$$
\| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {l s}} - g _ {\star} ^ {[ 1: h ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {5 . 6}{n} \lambda h \sqrt {d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)}
$$

observe that for a sufficiently large constant  $c_{0}$ , taking  $n \geq c_{0}h^{2}d_{u}^{2}$  implies our condition in the above lemma  $n \geq chd_{u}\log^{4}(n)$ . next, let us bound  $\|\pmb{\delta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . we have on the event  $\mathcal{e}^{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{bound}}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {n} \max  _ {t \in [ n ]} \| \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t} \| = \sqrt {n} \max  _ {t \in [ n ]} \| \mathbf {y} _ {t} - \sum_ {s = 1} ^ {h} g _ {\star} ^ {[ s ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - s} \| \\ = \sqrt {n} \max  _ {t \in [ n ]} \| \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \sum_ {s > h} g _ {\star} ^ {[ s ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - s} \| \leq \sqrt {n} \left(\max  _ {t \in [ n ]} \| \mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \| + \| g _ {\star} ^ {[ > h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \max  _ {t \in [ n ]} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t - s} \|\right) \\ \leq \sqrt {n} \left(r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \| g _ {\star} ^ {[ > h ]} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \cdot r _ {\mathbf {u}, \mathrm {e s t}}\right) \\ \leq \sqrt {n} \left(r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \frac {1}{6}\right) \tag {eq. (d.1)} \\ \leq \frac {7}{6} \sqrt {n} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used the assumed upper bound on  $\psi_{g_*}$  from plugging the above into lemma d.4 and using  $r_{\mathrm{nat}} \geq 1$  by assumption gives gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {l s}} - g _ {\star} ^ {[ 1: h ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {7}{3} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \sqrt {n} \cdot \frac {5 . 6}{n} h \sqrt {d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)} \\ = \frac {1 4 h \sqrt {d _ {\operatorname* {m a x}} + d _ {u} + \log (1 / \delta)}}{\sqrt {n}} = h c _ {\delta} / \sqrt {n}. \\ \end{array}
$$

thus,  $2\| \widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ls}} - g_{\star}^{[1:h]}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq 14h^2 c_\delta /\sqrt{n} := \epsilon_g(n,\delta)$ , as needed.

# d.2.1 proof of lemma d.4

we adopt the argument of simchowitz et al. [2019], but provide a simpler and self-contained proof. let us focus on the  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)}$  case, which we shall denote  $g_\star$  for the present argument. we denote the esimtate of  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)}$  and  $\widehat{g}$ . further, let  $\overline{\mathbf{u}}$  denote the matrix with rows  $\mathbf{u}_{t:t + h}^{\mathrm{ex,alg}}$  for  $1 \leq t \leq n - h - 1$ . moreover,

let  $\pmb{\delta}_{t} = \mathbf{y}_{t} - \sum_{i=1}^{h} g_{\star}^{[i]}\mathbf{u}_{t}$ , and let  $\pmb{\delta}$  denote the matrix with rows  $\pmb{\delta}_{t}$  for  $h + 1 \leq t \leq n$ . we then have the identity

$$
\widehat {g} - g _ {\star} = \left(\overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {u}}}\right) ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta}
$$

we can crudely bound

$$
\| \widehat {g} - g _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \| \overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {u}}}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}.
$$

let us now bound the operator norm of  $\| \overline{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\pmb {\delta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ . we have that the columns of  $\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\pmb{\delta}$  are of the form

$$
\left[ \left(\mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\right) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \right], \quad i \in \{0, 1, \dots , h \}
$$

thus, by tsiamis and pappas [2019, lemma a.1], and the definition of the operator norm (with  $s^{d_u - 1} \coloneqq \{v \in \mathbb{r}^{d_u} : \| v \| = 1\}$ ),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \sqrt {h + 1} \max  _ {i \in \{0, \dots , h \}} \| [ (\mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} ] \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \\ \leq \max  _ {i \in \{0, \dots , h \}} \max  _ {v \in s ^ {d _ {u} - 1}} \| (v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\text {e x}, \mathbf {a l g}} \delta_ {t}) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \| _ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

by the self-normalized martingale bounds (abbasi-yadkori et al. [2011, theorem 3]), and the fact that  $\delta_t$  is  $\mathcal{f}_{t - h - 1}$  measurable, where  $(\mathcal{f}_t)$  is the filtration generated by the random inputs, we have that with probability  $1 - \delta$

$$
\| (v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} (\boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + \lambda^ {2} i) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 2 \log \det (\frac {1}{\lambda^ {2}} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + i) + 2 \log (1 / \delta)
$$

in particular, if  $\lambda^2$  is any parameter such that the event  $\mathcal{e}_{\lambda} \coloneqq \| \pmb{\delta} \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \lambda$ , then we have that with probability  $1 - \delta$  that whenever  $\mathcal{e}_{\lambda}$  holds,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\lambda^ {2}} \left\| \left(v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathbf {a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\right) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \left\| \left(v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathbf {a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\right) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \left(\boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + \lambda^ {2} i\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq 2 \log \det (\frac {1}{\lambda^ {2}} \boldsymbol {\delta} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\delta} + i) + 2 \log (1 / \delta) \\ \leq 2 \log \det (2 i) + 2 \log (1 / \delta) = 2 \left(d _ {\max } \log 2 + \log (1 / \delta)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

so rearranging,

$$
\| (v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} \pmb {\delta} _ {t}) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \| _ {2} \leq \lambda \sqrt {2 (d _ {\max} \log 2 + \log (1 / \delta))}.
$$

next, by a standard covering argument vershynin [2018, section 4.2], we have that if  $\mathcal{s}_0$  is an  $1/5$ -net of  $\mathcal{s}^{d_u - 1}$ , then  $\max_{v \in \mathcal{s}^{d_u - 1}} \| (v^\top \mathbf{u}_{t - i}^{\mathrm{ex}, \mathrm{alg}} \delta_t)_{h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \|_2 \leq \frac{5}{4} \max_{v \in \mathcal{s}_0} \| (v^\top \mathbf{u}_{t - i}^{\mathrm{ex}, \mathrm{alg}} \delta_t)_{h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \|_2$ , and that we can take  $\log |\mathcal{s}_0| \leq d_u \log 9$ . thus, by a union bound over  $v \in \mathcal{s}_0$  and  $i \in \{0, \dots, h\}$ , the following holds with probability  $1 - \delta / 4$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \left(v ^ {\top} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \boldsymbol {\delta} _ {t}\right) _ {h + 1 \leq t \leq n} \| _ {2} \leq \frac {4 \sqrt {2}}{3} \lambda \sqrt {\left(\left(d _ {\max } + d _ {u}\right) \log 9 + \log \left(4 (h + 1) / \delta\right)\right)} \\ \leq \frac {4 \sqrt {2}}{3} \lambda \sqrt {\left(\left(d _ {\max } + d _ {u}\right) \log 9 + \log (8 h / \delta)\right)} \\ \leq 2. 8 \lambda \sqrt {d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (h / \delta)}, \\ \end{array}
$$

hence, we have that

$$
\| \widehat {g} - g _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 2. 8 \| (\overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {u}}}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \lambda \sqrt {(h + 1) (d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (h / \delta))}.
$$

finally, by constants in the argument modifying the arguments of oymak and ozay [2019, lemma c.2], we have that for any  $\epsilon$ , we can ensure that for  $t \geq c(\epsilon)(h + 1)d_u\log^4 (nd_u)$ , we can ensure  $\| (\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{u}})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq (1 - \epsilon)(n - (h + 1))^{-1}$  with probability  $1 - n^{-\log^2 n}$ . by enforcing  $n \geq (h + 1)/4$  and taking  $\epsilon = 1/2$ , we can obtain  $\| (\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{u}})^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq n/2$ , yielding

$$
\| \widehat {g} - g _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq 5. 6 \| (\overline {{\mathbf {u}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {u}}}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \lambda \sqrt {(h + 1) (d _ {\max } + d _ {u} + \log (h / \delta))},
$$

with probability  $1 - \delta / 4 - n^{-\log^2 n}$  on  $\mathcal{e}_{\lambda}$ .

# d.3 unknown system regret (section 6.3)

let us conclude with presenting the omitted proofs from section 6.3, and generalize to the stabilized case. the regret decomposition is identical to eq. (6.2), modifying the functions if necessary to capture their dependence on  $\widehat{\mathbf{u}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . throughout, we will assume  $\epsilon_g$  satisfies a generalization of condition 6.1 to the stabilized setting:

condition 6.1b (estimation condition). we assume that the event of theorem 6b holds (i.e. accuracy of estimates  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}$  and  $\widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow \eta}$ ), which entail  $\epsilon_g(n,\delta)\leq 1 / 2\max \{r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{g_\star},r_{\mathbf{u,est}}\}$ , where  $r_{\mathbf{u,est}} = r_{\mathbf{u,est}}(\delta)\coloneqq 3\sqrt{d_u + \log(1 / \delta)}$ . moreover, these entail that  $\| \widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to \eta}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}},\| \widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq 2r_{g_\star}$ . these also entail that  $\max_{s\leq n}\| \mathbf{u}_s^{\mathrm{alg}}\| \leq r_{\mathbf{u,est}}$ .

we begin with the following generalization of the stability guarantee of lemma 6.1:

lemma 6.1b (stability of  $\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}$ ). introduce the notation  $\overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}} \coloneqq 2\max \{r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}, r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}\}$ . then, for  $\epsilon_g \leq 1/2\max \{r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{g_\star}, r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}\}$  (satisfied by condition 6.1b) the following holds  $t \in [t]$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \right\| _ {2} \leq \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}}, \quad \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \right\| \leq r _ {\text {n a t}} + r _ {g _ {\star}} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} \leq 2 r _ {g _ {\star}} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}}, \\ \| \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \| _ {2}, \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \| _ {2} \leq 2 r _ {\text {n a t}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. the proof is analogous to that of lemma 6.1, but with the following modifications. let us sketch the major steps in the proof: we first establish the inequality  $\| \pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} - \widehat{\pmb{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| _2\leq \epsilon_g\| \mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{ex},\mathsf{alg}}\|_{2,\infty}$ , where we recall the notation  $\| \mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{ex},\mathsf{alg}}\|_{2,\infty} = \max_{s\in \{1,\ldots ,t\}}\| \mathbf{u}_s^{\mathrm{ex},\mathsf{alg}}\| _2$  introduced in the original proof. next, we can establish that

$$
\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathrm {a l g}} \| _ {2} \leq \max  \{r _ {\mathbf {u}, \mathrm {e s t}}, r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \} + \epsilon_ {g} r _ {\mathcal {m}} \| g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow \eta} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \| \mathbf {u} _ {1: t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \| _ {2, \infty}.
$$

by assumption,  $\| g_{\mathrm{ex}\to \eta}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq r_{g_\star}$  (definition 2.3b). hence, for  $\epsilon_g\leq 1 / 2\max \{r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{g_\star},r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}}\} \leq$ $1 / 2r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{g_\star}$ , we can recursively verify that  $\| \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{alg}}\| _2\leq 2\max \{r_{\mathbf{u},\mathrm{est}},r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}\}$ . lastly, we can bound  $\| \widehat{\mathbf{v}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}} - \mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| _2\leq \epsilon_g\| \mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{alg}}\|_{2,\infty}\leq r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  under the condition of the lemma, giving  $\| \widehat{\mathbf{v}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| \leq 2\| \mathbf{v}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\|$ . similarly, we can bound  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\eta}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| \leq 2\| \pmb{\eta}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\|$ .

in the stabilized setting, we shall need to slightly modify our magnitude bounds to account for that norms of the controls:

lemma d.5 (magnitude bounds for estimated system). suppose that condition 6.1b holds. then, for any  $t > n$ , and all  $m \in \mathcal{m}(m, r_{\mathcal{m}})$  and  $m_{t:t-h} \in \mathcal{m}(m, r_{\mathcal{m}})^{h+1}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} \|, \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \| \leq 2 r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right]\right\| _ {2} \leq 6 r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. we have that  $\| \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(m)\| _2 = \| \sum_{i = 0}^{m - 1}m^{[i]}\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t - i}^{\mathrm{nat}}\| \leq r_{\mathcal{m}}\max_{s\leq t}\| \widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}\| \leq 2r_{\mathcal{m}}r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  by lemma 6.1b. the bound on  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{alg}}$  specializes by setting  $m\gets \mathbf{m}_t$

by the same lemma, and the fact that  $\| \widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq \epsilon_g + \| g_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq 2r_{g_\star}$  by condition 6.1b,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left[ m _ {t: t - h} \mid \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right]\right\| _ {2} = \left\| \widehat {\mathbf {v}} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \sum_ {s = t - h} ^ {t} \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ t - s ]} \mathbf {u} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m _ {s} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| \\ \leq 2 r _ {\text {n a t}} + \left\| \widehat {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} \right\| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \max  _ {m \in \mathcal {m} (m, r _ {\mathcal {m}})} \max  _ {s \leq t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}) \right\| \\ \leq 2 r _ {\text {n a t}} + 4 r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\text {n a t}} \leq 6 r _ {g _ {\star}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} r _ {\text {n a t}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the bound on  $\| \mathbf{y}_t\left[m_{t:t - h}\mid \widehat{g},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}}\right]\| _2$  is similar.
