# e.3.2 proof of lemma e.4

for simplicity, let us use  $g_{\star}, \widehat{g}$  for  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}, \widehat{g}_{\mathrm{ex} \to \eta}$ . since  $m_{\star} \in \mathcal{m}(m_0, r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2)$ , we have  $m_{\star}^{[i]} = 0$  for all  $i \geq m_0$ . therefore, we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} \mid \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) (e.9) \\ = \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {s} ^ {\text {n a t}} \\ = \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \left(\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right)\right) \\ = \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \left(\left(\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} g _ {\star} ^ {[ s - j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}}\right) - \left(\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} - \sum_ {j = s - h} ^ {s} \widehat {g} ^ {[ j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}}\right)\right)\right) \\ = \underbrace {\left(\sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \left(\sum_ {j = s - h} ^ {s} \left(\widehat {g} ^ {[ j ]} - g _ {\star} ^ {[ j ]}\right) \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}}\right)\right)\right)} _ {:= \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {m a i n}}} \\ - \underbrace {\sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \sum_ {1 \leq j <   s - h} g _ {\star} ^ {[ s - j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}}} _ {:= \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {t r u n c}}} (e.10) \\ \end{array}
$$

here,  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{trunc}}$  is a lower order truncation term:

claim e.5. for  $t \geq n + m + 1$ , we have that  $\| \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{trunc}} \|_2 \leq \overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}} r_{\mathcal{m}} \psi_{g_\star}(h + 1)$ .

proof. we have that  $\| \mathbf{u}_j^{\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{alg}}\| _2\leq \overline{r}_{\mathbf{u}}$  by lemma 6.1b. this gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {t r u n c}} \right\| = \left\| \sum_ {s = (t - m _ {0} + 1) _ {+}} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \sum_ {1 \leq j <   s - h - 1} g _ {\star} ^ {[ s - j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}, \mathbf {a l g}} \right\| \\ \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} \sum_ {1 \leq j <   s - h} \| g _ {\star} ^ {[ s - j ]} \| \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1). \\ \end{array}
$$

to bound the dominant term  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{main}}$ , we express  $\mathbf{u}_j^{\mathrm{alg}}$  in terms of  $\hat{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  and the controller  $m_{\star}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}} = \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} \mid \hat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) = \sum_ {q = j - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {j} \mathbf {m} _ {j} ^ {[ j - q ]} \hat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {q} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \underbrace {\sum_ {q = j - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {j} m _ {\star} ^ {[ j - q ]} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {q} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}} _ {(b)} + \sum_ {q = j - m + 1} ^ {j} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} ^ {[ j - q ]} - m _ {\star} ^ {[ j - q ]}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where sum only over  $q \in \{(j - m_0)_+, \ldots, j\}$  in the bracketed term (b) because  $m_{\star}^{[n]} = 0$  for  $n > m_0$  since  $m_{\star} \in \mathcal{m}(m_0, r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2)$ . next, the equalities

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x , a l g}} = \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} \mid \hat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \\ = \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) + \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) - \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \\ = \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) + \left(\mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \right. \\ = \left(\sum_ {q = j - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {j} m _ {\star} ^ {[ j - q ]} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {q} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) + \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\star} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

and introducing the shorthand  $\delta_g^{[j]} \coloneqq \widehat{g}^{[j]} - g_\star^{[j]}$ , we can further develop

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {m a i n}} := \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \left(\widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {s} ^ {\text {n a t}} + \sum_ {j = s - h} ^ {s} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\text {e x , a l g}}\right) \\ := \underbrace{\sum_{s = t - m_{0} + 1}^{t}m_{\star}^{[t - s]} \left(\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{s}^{\mathrm{nat}} + \sum_{j = s - h}^{s}\sum_{q = j - m_{0} + 1}^{j}\delta_{g}^{[j]}m_{\star}^{[j - q]}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{q}^{\mathrm{nat}}\right)}_{:= \mathbf{u}_{t}^{\mathrm{apprx}}} \\ + \underbrace {\sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {j = s - h} ^ {s} \sum_ {q = j - m + 1} ^ {j} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1 : j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right)} _ {:= \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e r r}}}. \tag {e.11} \\ \end{array}
$$

here, the input  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{appx}}$  is represents the part of the input which can be represented as  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{appx}} = \mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(m_{\mathrm{appx}}|\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_t^{\mathrm{nat}})$  for some  $m_{\mathrm{appx}} \in \mathcal{m}(m,r_{\mathcal{m}})$ ; the remaining error term,  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{err}}$ , will be bounded shortly thereafter.

claim e.6 (existence of a good comparator). define the controller

$$
m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} ^ {[ i ]} = m _ {\star} ^ {[ i ]} i _ {i \leq m _ {0} - 1} + \sum_ {a = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} \sum_ {b = 0} ^ {h} \sum_ {c = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} m _ {\star} ^ {[ a ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ b ]} m _ {\star} ^ {[ c ]} \mathbb {i} _ {a + b + c = i},
$$

which depends only of  $m_{\star}$  and  $\epsilon_g$ . then,

1. we have the identity

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {a p p r x}} = \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right), \quad \forall t \geq n, \tag {e.12}
$$

2.  $\| m_{\mathrm{approx}} - m_{\star}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq \| m_{\star}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}^2\epsilon_g\leq r_\mathcal{m}^2\epsilon_g / 4.$  
3. if  $m \geq 2m_0 - 1 + h$  and  $\epsilon_g \leq \frac{1}{r_{\mathcal{m}}}$  (as ensured by condition e.1), then  $m_{\mathrm{approx}} \in \mathcal{m}(m, r_{\mathcal{m}})$ . proof. to verify eq. (e.12),

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} = \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {s = j - h} ^ {s} \sum_ {q = j - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {j} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ s - j ]} m _ {\star} ^ {[ j - q ]} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {q} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} m _ {\star} ^ {[ i ]} \hat {\eta} _ {t - q} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} + \sum_ {a = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} \sum_ {b = 0} ^ {h} \sum_ {c = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} m _ {\star} ^ {[ a ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ b ]} m _ {\star} ^ {[ c ]} \hat {\eta} _ {t - (a + b + c)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {2 (m _ {0} - 1) + h} \left(m _ {\star} ^ {[ i ]} i _ {i \leq m _ {0} - 1} + \sum_ {a = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} \sum_ {b = 0} ^ {h} \sum_ {c = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} m _ {\star} ^ {[ a ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ b ]} m _ {\star} ^ {[ c ]} \mathbb {i} _ {a + b + c = i}\right) \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {2 (m _ {0} - 1) + h} m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} ^ {[ i ]} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

next, since  $\| m_{\star} \| \leq r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2$ ,

$$
\| m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} - m _ {\star} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \sum_ {a = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} \sum_ {b = 0} ^ {h} \sum_ {c = 0} ^ {m _ {0} - 1} \| m _ {\star} ^ {[ a ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| \delta_ {g} ^ {[ b ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| m _ {\star} ^ {[ c ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \leq \| m _ {\star} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} \leq \frac {r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g}}{4},
$$

which verifies point 2. therefore, for  $\epsilon_g\leq 1 / r_{\mathcal{m}}$

$$
\left\| m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} \right\| \leq \left\| m _ {\star} \right\| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} + \left\| m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} - m _ {\star} \right\| _ {\ell_ {1}, \mathrm {o p}} \leq \frac {r _ {\mathcal {m}}}{2} + \frac {r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \varepsilon}{4} \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}}.
$$

moreover, by assumption on  $m \geq 2m_0 + h - 1$ , we have  $m_{\mathrm{apprx}}^{[i]} \geq 0$  for  $i > m \geq m_0 2(m_0 - 1) + h$ .

lastly, we control the error term. we shall do this incrementally via two successive claims. first, we "re-center"  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{err}}$  around the comparator  $m_0$ , rather than  $m_{\star}$ , and uses am-gm to isolate terms  $\| \mathbf{m}_j - m_{\mathrm{approx}}\| _f^2$ :

claim e.7. for  $m \geq 2m_0 - 1 + h$ , the following bound holds for all  $\tau > 0$

$$
\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e r r}} \| _ {2} \leq \frac {r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {3} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{2 \tau} + \frac {\tau}{2} \max _ {{j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h}} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

proof. using  $\| \hat{\pmb{\eta}}_q^{\mathrm{nat}}\| _2\leq 2r_{\mathrm{nat}}$  (lemma 6.1b) and  $\| m_{\star}\|_{\ell_1,\mathrm{op}}\leq r_{\mathcal{m}} / 2$  by assumption,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e r r}} \| _ {2} = \left\| \sum_ {s = t - m _ {0} + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {j = s - h} ^ {s} m _ {\star} ^ {[ t - s ]} \delta_ {g} ^ {[ j ]} \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} \epsilon_ {g} \max  _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\star} \mid \hat {\eta} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} \epsilon_ {g} \max  _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left(\left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

next, from (lemma 6.1b) and claim e.6, we can bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \leq \| m _ {\star} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \max  _ {s \leq j} \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {s} ^ {\text {n a t}} \| _ {2} \\ \leq 2 r _ {\text {n a t}} \| m _ {\star} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \| _ {\ell_ {1}, \text {o p}} \\ \leq \frac {r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g}}{2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

this yield

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e r r}} \| _ {2} \leq \frac {r _ {\text {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {3} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{2} + r _ {\mathcal {m}} \epsilon_ {g} \max  _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \hat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \frac {r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {3} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2}}{2 \tau} + \frac {\tau}{2} \max _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2} \left(\frac {r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} + \tau^ {- 1}}{2}\right) + \frac {\tau}{2} \max _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\mathrm {a p p r x}} | \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/a26041d66604b17faf5d5035621b5173b11b1fac5a4dfe7507c2390a679b38a6.jpg)

putting things together, we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\star} \mid \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} \\ = \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {t r u n c}} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {m a i n}} - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} (eq. (e.10)) \\ = \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {t r u n c}} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e r r}} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {a p p r x}} - \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e x}} \left(m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \hat {\mathbf {y}} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} (eq. (e.11)) \\ = \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {t r u n c}} + \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e r r}} \right\| _ {2} \quad (\text {b y c l a i m e . 6}) \\ \leq \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {t r u n c}} \right\| _ {2} + \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\text {e r r}} \right\| _ {2} \\ \leq \overline {{r}} _ {\mathbf {u}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} \psi_ {g _ {\star}} (h + 1) + r _ {\mathcal {m}} ^ {2} \epsilon_ {g} ^ {2} \left(\frac {r _ {\mathrm {n a t}} r _ {\mathcal {m}} + \tau^ {- 1}}{2}\right) \\ + \frac {\tau}{2} \max  _ {j = t - m _ {0} + 1 - h} ^ {t} \left\| \mathbf {u} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} \left(\mathbf {m} _ {j} - m _ {\text {a p p r x}} \mid \widehat {\boldsymbol {\eta}} _ {1: j} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ = (\text {r h s} \quad \text {e q .} \quad (\text {e . 5})) \\ \end{array}
$$

as needed.

# f establishing strong convexity

this appendix is devoted to establishing strong convexity of the drc and drc-ex parameterizations under semi-adversarial noise, described by assumption 6b in the previous appendix. the organization is as follows:

1. appendix f.1 introduces the necessary preliminaries to state our bound, including the markov operators of the dynamics that arise from an internal stabilizing controller, and the notion of the z-transform.  
2. appendix f.2 presents theorem 11, which describes the strong convexity of internally stabilized systems in terms of certain functionals of the z-transforms of relevant markov operators. combining with proposition f.1 which characterizes the behavior of these functionals, we this section concludes with the proof of proposition 7.1b. this section then specializes this bounds for systems with internal controllers which are given by static feedback (appendix f.2.2), and exact observer-feedback (appendix f.2.3).  
3. appendix f.3 addresses the proof of theorem 11.  
4. appendix f.4 establishes the proof of proposition 7.1. it borrows one lemm from the proof of theorem 11, but bypasses the z-transform to establish bounds via elementary principles.

5. appendix f.5 proves proposition f.1 via complex-analytic arguments. the focus is to obtain polynomial dependence in the horizon parameters, and no care is paid to specifying system-dependent constants.

# f.1 strong convexity preliminaries

transfer functions and z-transforms the strong convexity modulus is most succinctly described in the fourier domain, where we work with markov operators and their z-transforms. we recall the definition of an abstract markov operator as follows:

definition c.1 (markov operator). let  $\mathcal{g}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the set of markov operators  $g = (g^{[i]})_{i \geq 0}$  with  $g^{[i]} \in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , such that  $\|g\|_{\ell_1, \mathrm{op}} < \infty$ . given a system  $(a, b, c, d)$  with input dimension  $d_{\mathrm{in}}$  and output dimension  $d_{\circ}$ , we let  $g = \text{transfer}(a, b, c, d) \in \mathcal{g}_{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the system  $g^{[0]} = d$  and  $g^{[i]} = ca^{i-1}b$ .

we shall also use the notation

$$
g ^ {\top} = \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} (a, b, c, d) ^ {\top} = \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} (a ^ {\top}, c ^ {\top}, b ^ {\top}, d ^ {\top}),
$$

where  $(a^{\top}, c^{\top}, b^{\top}, d^{\top})$  is commonly referred to as the adjoint system. for an abstract markov operator  $g$ , its z-transform is the following power series:

definition f.1 (z-transform). for  $g \in \mathcal{g}_{d_{\mathrm{o}} \times d_{\mathrm{i n}}}$ , the  $z$ -transform is the mapping from  $\mathbb{c} \rightarrow \mathbb{c}^{d_{\mathrm{o}} \times d_{\mathrm{i n}}}$

$$
\check {g} (z): z \mapsto \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {n} g ^ {[ i ]} z ^ {- i}
$$

for finite-order linear dynamical systems, the z-transform can be expressed in closed form via:

lemma f.1. if  $g = \text{transfer}(a, b, c, d)$ , then  $\tilde{g}(z) = d + c(zi - a)^{-1}b$ .

closed loop dynamics: for stabilized systems, the relevant markov operators that arise correspond to the closed-loop dynamics of the nominal system placed in feedback with the stabilizing controller  $\pi_0$ . from lemma 3.1b, we recall the operators  $g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}$  and  $g_{(w,e)\to \eta}$  which satisfy:

$$
\left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {a l g}}\end{array}\right] = \left[\begin{array}{c}\mathbf {y} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\\\mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\end{array}\right] + \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {t - 1} g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} \mathbf {u} _ {t - i} ^ {\mathrm {e x}}
$$

and

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t - i} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t - i} \end{array} \right].
$$

the markov operators in terms of which we bound the strong convexity modulus are as follows:

definition f.2 (markov operators for strong convexity). recall the markov operators

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} := \text {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \mathrm {e x}}\right)
$$

$$
g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta} := \operatorname {t r a n s f e r} \left(a _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, b _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}}, c _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \eta}, d _ {\pi_ {0}, \mathrm {c l}, \eta}\right)
$$

from definition 2.2b. we define the noise transfer function as

$$
g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} = g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta} \sigma_ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \in \mathcal {g} ^ {d _ {\eta} \times (d _ {x} + d _ {u})},
$$

where the above notation is short hand for  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}^{[i]} = g_{(w,e)\to \eta}^{[i]}\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\frac{1}{2}}$  for all  $i$ . note that  $g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}$  has  $d_{\mathrm{in}} = d_u$  and  $d_{\circ} = d_y + d_u$ , whereas  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\top}$  has  $d_{\mathrm{in}} = d_y$  and  $d_{\circ} = d_x + d_u$ . we further define the function  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}}$  and  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{noise}}}$  denote the corresponding decay functions, which are proper by assumption 1b.

here,  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \rightarrow (y,u)}$  describes the dependence of  $(\mathbf{y}, \mathbf{u})$  on exogenous inputs  $\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{ex}}$ , and  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}$  is the transpose of the system which describes the effect that the noise in the system has on natures  $\mathbf{y}_t^{\mathrm{nat}}$ . since  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(m)$  is linear in natures  $y$ ,  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}$  needs to be sufficiently well conditioned (in a sense we will describe) to ensure strong convexity. note that  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}$  above need not be full-covariance, provided that it satisfies assumption 6b. moreover, since  $f_t(m)$  depends on  $\mathbf{u}_s^{\mathrm{ex}}(m)$  via the markov operator  $g_{\mathrm{ex} \rightarrow (y,u)}$ , this operator also needs to be sufficiently well conditioned.

# f.2 internally stabilized strong convexity and proof of proposition 7.1b

the relevant strong convexity parameter is bounded most precisely in terms of what we call "  $\mathcal{h}$  " functions, which describe the behavior of the z-transform  $\check{g} (z)$  of a markov operator along the torus:  $\mathbb{t}\coloneqq \{e^{\iota \theta}\mid \theta \in [0,2\pi ]\}$ :

definition f.3 ( $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}$ -functional). let  $d_{\mathrm{in}} \leq d_{\circ}^{13}$ ,  $g \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ . we define the  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}$  and  $\mathcal{h}_{\infty}$  functionals as

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {\min } [ g ] := \min  _ {\theta \in [ 0, 2 \pi ]} \sigma_ {d _ {\text {i n}}} (\check {g} (e ^ {\iota \theta})) \quad \text {a n d} \quad \| g \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {\infty}} := \max  _ {\theta \in [ 0, 2 \pi ]} \| \check {g} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {\text {o p}}.
$$

we will show that for  $h, k$  sufficiently large, the strong convexity parameter is lower bounded by  $\gtrsim \mathcal{h}_{\min}[g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)}] \cdot \mathcal{h}_{\min}[g_{\mathrm{noise}}]$ . unfortunately, for certain pathological systems, one or both of these terms may vanish. to ensure fast rates for all systems, we will need a more refined notion:

definition f.4. let  $d_{\mathrm{in}} \leq d_{\circ}$ ,  $g \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , and let  $\omega = (\omega^{[i]})_{i \geq 0}$  denote elements of  $\mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}} := \mathcal{g}^{1 \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , with  $\|\omega\|_{\ell_2}^2 := \sum_{i \geq 0} \|\omega^{[i]}\|_2^2$  and z-transform  $\tilde{\omega}$ . further, define  $\mathcal{w}_h := \{\omega \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}} : \| \omega \|_{\ell_2} = 1, \omega^{[i]} = 0, \forall i > h\}$ . we define the  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}$ -functional as

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g ] ^ {2} := \min  _ {\omega \in \mathscr {w} _ {h}} \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \| \check {g} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \check {\omega} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta .
$$

abusing notation, we also will write  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\tilde{g}]$  and other relevant functionals as a function of the z-transform, where convenient. we also note that, just as  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(\cdot)$  is not a norm,  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\cdot ]$  and  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[\cdot ]$  are not norms as well.

having defined the relevant functions, the following bound gives us a precise bound on the relevant strong convexity parameter. we consider the functions

$$
f _ {t; k} (m \mid g _ {\star}, \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {u} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) = \mathbb {e} [ f _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\star}, \mathbf {y} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {u} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} ],
$$

where  $\mathcal{f}_t$  is the filtration from assumption 6b. in what follows, we will adopt the shorthand  $f_{t;k}(m\mid g_\star,\mathbf{y}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}},\mathbf{u}_{1:t}^{\mathrm{nat}})$ . our main theorem is as follows:

theorem 11. fix,  $m,h$  and let  $k = m + 2h$ . further, define

$$
\begin{array}{l} \alpha_ {m, h} = \frac {1}{2} \cdot \mathcal {h} _ {[ m ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ] ^ {2} \cdot \mathcal {h} _ {[ m + h ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top} ] ^ {2} \\ \geq \frac {1}{2} \cdot \mathcal {h} _ {\min } [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ] ^ {2} \cdot \mathcal {h} _ {\min } [ g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top} ] ^ {2} := \alpha_ {\infty}, \\ \end{array}
$$

then

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[\left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \geq \alpha_ {m, h, k} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \geq \alpha_ {\infty} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2},
$$

provided that

$$
\psi_ {g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}} (h) \leq \frac {(\mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y , u)} ])}{8 (m + h)}, \quad a n d \quad \psi_ {g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}}} (h) \leq \frac {\mathcal {h} _ {[ m + h - 1 ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ]}{2 (m + h)}. \tag {f.1}
$$

thus, if each  $\ell_t$  is chosen by an oblivious adversary and is  $\alpha$ -strongly convex, the functions  $f_{t;k}(m)$  are  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}}\cdot \alpha_{m,h,k}$  and  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}}\cdot \alpha_{\infty}$  strongly-convex, provided that eq. (f.1) holds.

the above theorem is proved in appendix f.3. some remarks are in order:

1. while  $m, h$  are algorithm parameters,  $k$  appears only in the analysis. the constraints on  $h$  reflects how the  $m$ -history long inputs  $\mathbf{u}_t^{\mathrm{ex}}(m)$  must be given time to propagate through the system, and the constrain  $k \geq m + h$  reflects the sufficient excitation required from past noise to ensure the last  $m + h$  natures  $y$ 's are well conditioned.  
2. as we shall show in proposition f.1, the functional  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g]$  decays polynomially as a function of  $h$ . on the other hand, decay functions decay geometrically, so these constrains on  $m,h,k$  can always be satisfied for  $h$  and  $k$  sufficiently large.  
3. we consider  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\top}$  to insure that the input dimension is greater than output dimension, as per the restriction in f.3.

appendix f.2.2 provides a transparent lower bound on  $\alpha_{\infty}$  when the system is stabilized by an static feedback controller. for general controllers, however,  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)}]$  may be equal to zero. we introduce the following condition. however, we can show that  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g]$  degrades at most polynomially in  $h$ :

proposition f.1. let  $d_{\mathrm{in}} \leq d_{\mathrm{o}}$  and  $g = \operatorname{transfer}(a, b, c, d) \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{o}} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , with  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}} (d) > 0$ , or more generally, that  $g$  is then, there exists constants  $c, n$  depending only on  $g$  such that, for all  $h \geq 0$ ,  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g] \geq c / (h + 1)^n$ .

we are now in a place to prove our intended proposition:

# f.2.1 proof of proposition 7.1b

recall the settings  $h = \lfloor m / 3 \rfloor$ , and  $k = m + 2h$ . first, we lower bound  $\alpha_{m,h}$ . from proposition f.1, there exists constances  $c_1, c_2 > 0$  and  $n_1, n_2$  such that  $\mathcal{h}_{[m]}[g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)}] \geq c_1 m^{-n_1} \mathcal{h}_{[m + h]}[g_{\mathrm{noise}}^\top] \geq c_2 (m + h)^{-n_2} \geq \frac{c_2}{2^{n_2}} m^{n_2}$ , since  $h \leq m$ . thus, there exists some  $\alpha_{\mathrm{sys}} > 0$  and  $p_{\mathrm{sys}}$  such that  $\alpha_{m,h} \geq \alpha_{\mathrm{sys}} m^{p_{\mathrm{sys}}}$ .

now, let us show that there exist an  $m \geq m_{\mathrm{sys}}$  for which conditions of theorem 11 hold. from the stability assumption of the stabilized system (assumption 1b), there exists constants  $c > 0$  and  $\rho \in (0,1)$  for which  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)}}(n) \vee \psi_{g_{\mathrm{noise}}}(n) \leq c\rho^n$ . thus, for  $m \geq 4$  and  $h = \lfloor m/3 \rfloor \geq m/4$ , we have

$$
\left(\frac {\left(\mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} \left[ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y , u)} \right]\right)}{8 (m + h)}\right) \cdot \psi_ {g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}} (h) \leq \frac {c \rho^ {\lfloor m / 3 \rfloor}}{8 (m + \lfloor m / 3 \rfloor) c _ {1} \lfloor m / 3 \rfloor^ {- n _ {1}}},
$$

which is at most 1 for all  $m$  sufficiently large. a similar argument, tm applies to checking the bound  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{noise}}}(h) \leq \frac{\mathcal{h}_{[m + h - 1][g_{\mathrm{noise}}]}}{2(m + h)}$ .

# f.2.2 example: static feedback controllers

consider the static feedback setting (example c.2), where we have a stabilizing controller with  $a_{\pi_0} = 0$  and  $\pmb{\eta}_t = \mathbf{y}_t$ . for consistency with conventional notational, we set  $f = d_{\pi_0} \in \mathbb{r}^{d_u d_y}$ . this includes the full observation setting via the laws  $(a_\star + b_\star f)$ , but may also include settings with partial observation which admit a matrix  $f$  such that  $(a_\star + b_\star k c_\star)$  is stable: note that taking  $k = 0$  subsumes full-feedback as well. the proposition shows that  $\alpha_{\infty}$  from theorem 11 admits a transparent lower bound:

proposition f.2. consider a static feedback controller with  $k = d_{\pi_0}$ , and recall  $\alpha_{\infty} := \frac{1}{2} \cdot \mathcal{h}_{\min}[g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y, u)}]^2$ .  $\mathcal{h}_{\min}[g_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\top}]^2$ . then,

1.  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}} \succeq \sigma^2 i$ , then  $\alpha_{\infty} \geq \frac{\sigma^2}{32} \min \left\{1, \|k\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{-2}\right\} \cdot \min \left\{1, \|b_{\star}k\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{-2}\right\}$ .  
2. if only  $\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^2 > 0$  (but  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}$  may not be positive definite), then

$$
\alpha_ {\infty} \geq \frac {\sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2}}{1 6} \min  \left\{1, \| k \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {- 2} \right\} \cdot \frac {\sigma_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} (c _ {\star}) ^ {2}}{(1 + \| a _ {k} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}) ^ {2}}
$$

3. finally, if  $k = 0$ , then

$$
\alpha_ {\infty} \geq \frac {\sigma_ {\mathbf {e}} ^ {2}}{2} + \frac {\sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2}}{2} \frac {\sigma_ {\operatorname* {m i n}} (c _ {\star})}{(1 + \| a _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}) ^ {2}}.
$$

note that if  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(c_\star) > 0$ , then we only need  $\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^2 > 0$  to ensure  $\alpha_{\infty} > 0$ . in particular, with with  $c_\star = i$ , we recover the bounds from agarwal et al. [2019b], even with stabilizing feedback. note that, unlike agarwal et al. [2019b], these bounds don't require any assumptions on the system, or any approximate diagonalizability. $^{14}$  it order to illustrate how useful it is to the represent strong convexity in terms of z-transform and  $\mathcal{h}$ -functionals, we provide a proof of the above proposition

proof of proposition f.2. in static feedback, we have a stabilizing controller with  $a_{\pi_0} = 0$ , and set  $d_{\pi_0} = k$  and  $a_k = a_\star + b_\star kc_\star$ , and  $\tilde{a}_k(z) = (zi - a_k)^{-1}$ . then, we can verify

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c} 0 \\ i \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c} c _ {\star} \\ k c _ {\star} \end{array} \right] a _ {k} ^ {i - 1} b _ {\star}, g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} c _ {\star} a _ {k} ^ {i - 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & k \end{array} \right]
$$

thus,

$$
\check {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} (z) = \left[\begin{array}{c}c _ {\star} \check {a} _ {k} (z) b _ {\star}\\i + k c _ {\star} \check {a} _ {k} (z) b _ {\star}\end{array}\right], \quad \check {g} _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} (z) ^ {\top} = \sigma_ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {1 / 2} \left[\begin{array}{c}\check {a} _ {k} (z) ^ {\top} c _ {\star} ^ {\top}\\i + b _ {\star} ^ {\top} k ^ {\top} \check {a} _ {k} (z) ^ {\top} c _ {\star} ^ {\top}\end{array}\right].
$$

we now invoke a simple lemma:

lemma f.2. consider a matrix of the form  $w = \left[ \begin{array}{c} yz \\ i + xz \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb{r}^{(d_1 + d) \times d}$ , with  $y \in \mathbb{r}^{d_1 \times d_1}$ ,  $x, z^\top \in \mathbb{r}^{d \times d_1}$ . then,  $\sigma_{\min}(w) \geq \frac{1}{2} \min \{1, \frac{\sigma_{\min}(y)}{\|x\|_{\mathrm{op}}}\}$ .

proof of lemma f.2. consider  $\| wv\| _2$  for  $v\in \mathbb{r}^d$  with  $\| v\| = 1$ . if  $\| zv\| _2\leq 1 / 2\| x\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ , then

$$
\| w v \| _ {2} \geq \| i + x z v \| _ {2} \geq 1 - \| x \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| z v \| _ {2} \geq 1 - \frac {1}{2} = \frac {1}{2}.
$$

otherwise,  $\| wv\| _2\geq \| yzv\| \geq \sigma_{\min}(y)\cdot \| zv\| \geq \frac{\sigma_{\min}(y)}{2\|x\|_{\mathrm{op}}}$

by lemma f.2, we see that

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {\min } [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ] \geq \frac {1}{2} \min  \{1, \| k \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {- 1} \},
$$

and if  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}\succeq \sigma^2 i$  , then

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {\min } \left[ g _ {\text {n o i s e}} ^ {\top} \right] \geq \frac {\sigma}{2} \min  \{1, \| b _ {\star} k \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {- 1} \}.
$$

this establishes the first result of the proposition. moreover, if we just have state noise  $\sigma_w^2$  but possibly no observation noise, then  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[g_{\mathrm{noise}}] \geq \sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(c_\star)\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\check{a}_k] \geq \frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(c_\star)}{1 + \|a_j\|_{\mathrm{op}}}$ , where we note that  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(\check{a}_k(z)) = \frac{1}{\|\check{a}_k(z)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}} = \frac{1}{1 + \|zi - a_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}}$ , which is at least  $\frac{1}{1 + \|a_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}}$  for  $z \in \mathbb{t}$ .

lastly, when  $k = 0$ , we can directly lower bound  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}]\geq 1$ , and lower bound  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[g_{\mathrm{noise}}]^2\geq$ $\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^{2}\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(c_{\star})^{2}\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\check{a}_{k}]^{2} + \mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[i + b_{\star}^{\top}k^{\top}\check{a}_{k}(z)^{\top}c_{\star}^{\top}]^{2}\sigma_{\mathbf{e}}^{2}$  by specializing  $f = 0$  in the argument adopted for the previous part of the proposition,  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\mathbb{i} + b_{\star}^{\top}k^{\top}\check{a}_{k}(z)^{\top}c_{\star}^{\top}]\geq \frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(c_{\star})}{1 + \|a_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}$ , and by setting  $k = 0$ ,  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[i + b_{\star}^{\top}k^{\top}\check{a}_{k}(z)^{\top}c_{\star}^{\top}]^{2} = i.$

# f.2.3 example: youla ldc-ex with exact observer feedback

in general, static feedback is not sufficient to stabilize a partially observed linear dynamic system. let us consider what arises from the the youla ldc-ex parametrization. from lemma c.2, we have

$$
g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i _ {d _ {y}} \end{array} \right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} c _ {\star} (a _ {\star} + l c _ {\star}) ^ {i - 1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i _ {d _ {x}} & f \end{array} \right].
$$

and

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \left[\begin{array}{l}0\\i _ {d _ {u}}\end{array}\right] + \mathbb {i} _ {i > 0} \left[\begin{array}{l}c _ {\star}\\f\end{array}\right] (a _ {\star} + b _ {\star} f) ^ {i - 1} b _ {\star}.
$$

thus, introducing  $\check{a}_{bf}(z) = (zi - (a + bk))^{-1}$ , and  $\check{a}_{lc}(z) = (zi - (a - lc))^{-1}$ , we have

$$
\check {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} (z) = \left[\begin{array}{c}0\\i _ {d _ {u}}\end{array}\right] + \left[\begin{array}{c}c _ {\star}\\f\end{array}\right] \check {a} _ {b f} (z) b _ {\star} = \left[\begin{array}{c}c _ {\star} \check {a} _ {b f} (z) b _ {\star}\\i _ {d _ {u}} + f \check {a} _ {b f} (z) b _ {\star}\end{array}\right]
$$

moreover,

$$
\check {g} _ {(w, e) \to \eta} (z) = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 0 & i _ {d _ {y}} \end{array} \right] + c _ {\star} \check {a} _ {l c} (z) \left[ \begin{array}{c c} i _ {d _ {x}} & f \end{array} \right],
$$

giving

$$
\check {g} _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} (z) = \sigma_ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \left[ \check {a} _ {l c} (z) ^ {\top} c _ {\star} ^ {\top} i _ {d _ {y}} + f ^ {\top} \check {a} _ {l c} (z) ^ {\top} c _ {\star} ^ {\top} \right].
$$

from lemma f.2, we have

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {\mathrm {m i n}} [ \check {g} _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} (z) ] \geq \min \left\{1, \frac {1}{2 \| f \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}} \right\}.
$$

lower bounding  $\tilde{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}(z)$  is a little trickier. define  $x(z) = zi - a_{\star}$ . we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} i _ {d _ {u}} + f \check {a} _ {b f} (z) b _ {\star} = i + f (z i - a _ {\star} - b _ {\star} f) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star} = i + f (x (z) - b _ {\star} f) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star} \\ = i + f (x (z) - b _ {\star} f) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star} \\ = - \left((- i) - f (x (z) + b _ {\star} (- i) f\right) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star}) \\ = - (- i + f x (z) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star}) ^ {- 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

then,

$$
\sigma_ {\mathrm {m i n}} (i _ {d _ {u}} + f \check {a} _ {b f} (z) b _ {\star}) = \frac {1}{\| - i + f x (z) ^ {- 1} b _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}} \leq \frac {1}{1 + \| f \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| b _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| x (z) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}.
$$

substituting  $x(z) = (zi - a_{\star})$ , we have

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {\mathrm {m i n}} [ \check {g} _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ] \geq \frac {1}{1 + \| f \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \| b _ {\star} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} \max _ {z \in \mathbb {t}} \| (z i - a _ {\star}) ^ {- 1} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}}
$$

in otherwise, if the eigenvalues of  $a_{\star}$  are bounded away from 1 in magnitude, then  $\mathcal{h}_{\mathrm{min}}[\check{g}_{\mathrm{ex}\to (y,u)}] > 0$ , yielding a bound of  $\alpha_{\infty} > 0$ .

# f.3 proof of theorem 11

the proof of theorem 11 proceeds by first representing the strong convexity in terms of the toeplitz operator defined below:

definition f.5. let  $k,\ell,h\in \mathbb{n}$  with  $k\geq h\geq 0$  , and  $\ell >0$  . given a markov operator  $g\in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{o}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  with  $g\in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{o}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  , let  $g_{\ell}\in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{o}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the markov operator with  $g_{\ell}^{[i]} = \mathbb{i}_{i\leq \ell}g^{[i]}$  . the toeplitz operator is defined by

$$
\mathsf {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 1 ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ g _ {\ell} ^ {[ h ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ h - 1 ]} & \ldots & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 1 ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ g _ {\ell} ^ {[ h + 1 ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ h ]} & \ldots & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 1 ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ g _ {\ell} ^ {[ k ]} & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ k - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & g _ {\ell} ^ {[ k - h ]} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {r} ^ {(k + 1) d _ {\circ} \times (h + 1) d _ {\mathrm {i n}}}
$$

we use the shorthand  $\mathsf{t o e p}_{h;k}(g) = \mathsf{t o e p}_{h;k,k}(g)$ .

our first lemma establishes strong convexity in terms of the above operator:

lemma f.3. for any  $m = (m^{[i]})_{i=0}^{m-1}$ , we have the bound

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[ \left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \geq \underline {{\alpha}} _ {m, h, k} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}
$$

where  $\| m\|_{\mathrm{f}}^2 \coloneqq \sum_{i = 0}^{m - 1}\| m^{[i]}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^2$  and

$$
\underline {{\alpha}} _ {m, h, k} = \sigma_ {d _ {u} m} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m - 1; m + h - 1, h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)})) ^ {2} \cdot \sigma_ {d _ {y} (m + h)} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} (g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top})) ^ {2}
$$

next, we show that the smallest singular value of a toeplitz operator is lower bounded by the  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g]$

lemma f.4. let  $g \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{o}} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  be a markov operator, which in particular means  $\| g \|_{\ell_1, \mathrm{op}} < \infty$ . further, let  $\ell, k, h \in \mathbb{n}$ , with  $\ell \geq 1$ , and  $k \geq h \geq 1$ . finally, set  $c_1 = c_2$  if  $\ell \geq k$ , and otherwise, let  $c_1 = (1 - \tau)$ ,  $c_2 = \frac{1}{\tau} - 1$  for some  $\tau > 0$ . then,

$$
\sigma_ {d _ {\text {i n}} (h + 1)} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g)\right) ^ {2} \geq c _ {1} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g ] ^ {2} - c _ {2} (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (k - h \vee \ell) ^ {2},
$$

where  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g]$  is as in definition f.4.

the above lemma is proved in appendix f.3.2. theorem 11 noq follows readily:

proof of theorem 11. from lemma f.3, the functions  $f_{t;k}(m) = \mathbb{e}[f_t(m)\mid \mathcal{f}_{t - k}]$  are  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}}\cdot \underline{\alpha}_{m,h,k}$  strongly-convex, where

$$
\underline {{\alpha}} _ {m, h, k} = \sigma_ {d _ {u} m} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m - 1; m + h - 1, h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)})) ^ {2} \cdot \sigma_ {d _ {y} (m + h)} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} (g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top})) ^ {2}
$$

applying lemma f.4 with  $\tau = \frac{1}{8}$

$$
\sigma_ {d _ {u} m} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m - 1; m + h - 1, h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right)\right) ^ {2} \geq \frac {7}{8} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} \left[ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} \right] ^ {2} - 7 (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}} (h) ^ {2}
$$

. taking

$$
\psi_ {g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}} (h) \leq \frac {h + 1 (\mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y , u)} ]) ^ {2}}{8},
$$

we obtain

$$
\sigma_ {d _ {u} m} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m - 1; m + h - 1, h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right)\right) ^ {2} \geq \frac {3}{4} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} \left[ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} \right] ^ {2}
$$

further, applying lemma f.4 with  $\ell = k$ , we obtain

$$
\sigma_ {d _ {u} (m + h)} \left(\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} \left(g _ {\text {n o i s e}}\right)\right) ^ {2} \geq \mathcal {h} _ {[ m + h - 1 ]} \left[ g _ {\text {n o i s e}} \right] ^ {2} - (m + h - 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g _ {\text {n o i s e}}} (k - (m + h - 1)) ^ {2}.
$$

taking  $k = m + 2h$ , it suffices that  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{noise}}}(h)^2 \leq \frac{\mathcal{h}_{[m + h - 1][g_{\mathrm{noise}}]}}{2(m + h)}$ , we obtain  $\sigma_{d_u(m + h)}(\mathsf{t}\mathsf{o}\mathsf{e}\mathsf{p}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\mathrm{noise}}))^2 \geq \frac{3}{4}\mathcal{h}_{[m + h][g_{\mathrm{noise}}}^2$ . thus,

$$
\underline {{\alpha}} _ {m, h, k} \geq \frac {3}{4} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ] ^ {2} \cdot \frac {3}{4} \mathcal {h} _ {[ m + h - 1 ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ] ^ {2} \geq \frac {1}{2} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)} ] ^ {2} \mathcal {h} _ {[ m + h - 1 ]} [ g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ] ^ {2} := \alpha_ {m, h, k}.
$$

since  $\mathcal{h}_{[h]}[g] \geq \mathcal{h}_{\min}[g]$ , we conclude that  $\alpha_{m,h} \geq \alpha_{\infty}$ . therefore,

$$
\mathbb {e} \left[\left\| \mathbf {v} _ {t} \left(m \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\text {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}}\right) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\text {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \geq \alpha_ {m, h, k} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \geq \alpha_ {\infty} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \tag {f.2}
$$

finally, we observe that if  $f(z) = \ell(xz + v)$  is a function with a random variable  $x$  and  $\alpha$ -strongly convex loss  $\ell$ , then  $\mathbb{e}[f(z)]$  is  $\alpha \cdot \alpha'$  strongly convex as long as  $\mathbb{e}[\|xz\|_2^2] \geq \alpha' \|z\|_2^2$  for all  $z$ . this means that eq. (f.2) entails that  $f_{t;k}$  is both  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}} \cdot \alpha_{m,h,k}$  and  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}} \cdot \alpha_{\infty}$ -strongly convex.

![](images/1111efe2871e6a8aab9ebff5df00e8145d6d1b8861a8b70b322f61e39b1d2792.jpg)

# f.3.1 proof of lemma f.3

for simplicity, we assume that  $m \in \mathcal{m}(m + 1, r_{\mathcal{m}})$ ; this simplifies the indexing. further, introduce the row-toeptliz operator

$$
\operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} g ^ {[ 0 ]} & g ^ {[ 1 ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ h ]} \end{array} \right].
$$

further, let us introduce the shorthand

$$
\delta \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m) = \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m \mid g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}, \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) - \mathbf {v} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}, \quad \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) := \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m \mid \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {1: t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}).
$$

we can directly check that

$$
\delta \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m) = \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)}) \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \\ \dots \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t - h} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \end{array} \right].
$$

moreover,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {u} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \\ \dots \\ \mathbf {u} _ {t - h} ^ {\mathrm {e x}} (m) \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} m ^ {[ 0 ]} & m ^ {[ 1 ]} & \dots & m ^ {[ m ]} & 0 & \dots \\ 0 & m ^ {[ 0 ]} & \dots & m ^ {[ m - 1 ]} & m ^ {[ m ]} & \dots \\ \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots & & \\ \dots & \dots & m ^ {[ 0 ]} & m ^ {[ 1 ]} & \dots & m ^ {[ m ]} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \dots \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t - (m + h)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right] \\ = \mathsf {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \left[ \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \\ \dots \\ \boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t - (m + h)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \end{array} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

letting  $\mathbf{n}_{t:t - (m + h)}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  denote the vector above, this us gives the compact representation:

$$
\delta \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m) = \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h)} ^ {\text {n a t}}.
$$

recall that, to establish the lemma, we wish to lower bound

$$
\mathbb {e} [ \| \delta \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} ] \geq \underline {{\alpha}} _ {h, m, k} \| m \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2},
$$

where  $\| m\|_{\mathrm{f}}^2 \coloneqq \sum_{i = 0}^{m - 1}\| m^{[i]}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^2$ . to this end, define the random variable

$$
\mathbf {n} _ {t; t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} := \mathbf {n} _ {t; t - (m + h)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} - \mathbb {e} [ \mathbf {n} _ {t; t - (m + h)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} ]. \tag {f.3}
$$

since  $\mathbf{n}_{t;(t - (m + h);k)}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  is uncorrelated with  $\mathbb{e}[\mathbf{n}_{t;(t - (m + h)}^{\mathrm{nat}}\mid \mathcal{f}_{t - k}]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \left\| \delta \mathbf {v} _ {t} (m) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} \left[\left\| \mathrm {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}) \mathrm {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h)} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \\ = \mathbb {e} \left[\left\| \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); l} ^ {\text {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \\ + \left\| \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\text {n a t}} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right]\right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \quad (\text {b y}) \\ \geq \mathbb {e} \left[\left\| \mathrm {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}) \mathrm {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \\ = \left\| \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \times \sigma_ {d _ {y} (1 + m + h)} \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} (\mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right]\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

thus, to conclude the proof, it suffices to establish that, for  $\mathbf{n}_{t; t - (m + h); k}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  defined in eq. (f.3), we have

$$
\sigma_ {d _ {y} (1 + m + h)} \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \left(\mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right]\right) \geq \sigma_ {d _ {y} (m + 1 + h)} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {\text {n o i s e}}\right)\right) ^ {2} \tag {f.4}
$$

and

$$
\left\| \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \geq \sigma_ {d _ {u} (m + 1)} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m; m + h, h} \left(g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)}\right)\right) ^ {2} \tag {f.5}
$$

note that  $m$  in the above display in fact corresponds to  $m - 1$  in the statement of the lemma, since for the proof we assume  $m \in \mathcal{m}(m + 1, r)$  to simplify indices.

let us now establish both equations in the above display.

proving eq. (f.4) recall that  $g_{(w,e)\to \eta}$  denotes the markov operator mapping disturbances to outputs, which satisfies by lemma 3.1b the following

$$
\boldsymbol {\eta} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {t} g _ {(w, e) \to \eta} ^ {[ i ]} \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {w} _ {t - i} \\ \mathbf {e} _ {t - i} \end{array} \right].
$$

then, since  $\mathbf{n}_{t; t - (m + h)}^{\mathrm{nat}}$  is the component of nature's  $y$ 's depending only on noises  $(\mathbf{w}_s, \mathbf{e}_s)$  for  $s \in \{t - k, t - k + 1, \ldots, t\}$ , we deduce:

$$
\mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} = \mathsf {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} (g _ {(w, e) \to \eta}) \left[ \begin{array}{l} \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {e} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \\ \mathbf {w} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \end{array} \right] \\ \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {e} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \\ \mathbf {w} _ {t - 1} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \end{array} \right] \\ \dots \\ \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {e} _ {t - k} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \\ \mathbf {w} _ {t - k} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} \end{array} \right] \end{array} \right],
$$

where we have defined the toeplitz transpose operator

$$
\operatorname {t o e p t r a n s} _ {h; k} (g) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c} g ^ {[ 0 ]} & g ^ {[ 1 ]} & g ^ {[ 2 ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ k - h ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ k ]} \\ 0 & g ^ {[ 0 ]} & g ^ {[ 1 ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ k - h - 1 ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ k - 1 ]} \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & 0 & g ^ {[ 0 ]} & \ldots & g ^ {[ k - h ]}. \end{array} \right]
$$

thus, letting  $\mathrm{diag}_{k + 1}(\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}})$  denote a block diagonal matrix with  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}$  along the diagonal, we have from assumption 6b

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {e} \left[ \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} \left(\mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}\right) ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] \\ = \operatorname {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta}\right) \operatorname {d i a g} _ {k + 1} \left(\sigma_ {\text {n o i s e}}\right) \operatorname {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta}\right) ^ {\top} \\ = \mathsf {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta} \cdot \sigma_ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\frac {1}{2}}\right) \mathsf {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {(w, e) \rightarrow \eta} \cdot \sigma_ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\frac {1}{2}}\right) ^ {\top} \\ = \operatorname {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {\text {n o i s e}}\right) \operatorname {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} \left(g _ {\text {n o i s e}}\right) ^ {\top}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we use the convention  $g_{(w,e)\to \eta}\cdot \sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\frac{1}{2}}$  denotes the markov operator whose  $i$ -th component is  $g_{(w,e)\rightarrow \eta}^{\left[t\right]}\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\frac{1}{2}}$ , and recall the definition  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}\coloneqq g_{(w,e)\rightarrow \eta}\cdot \sigma_{\mathrm{noise}}^{\frac{1}{2}}$  (definition f.2).

the following fact is straightforward:

claim f.5. for all  $\sigma_d(\mathrm{toeptrans}_{h;k}(g)) = \sigma_d(\mathrm{toep}_{h;k}(g^\top))$  for all  $d$ .

thus, combining the above with claim f.5,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {d _ {y} (1 + m + h)} \left(\mathbb {e} [ \mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}} (\mathbf {n} _ {t: t - (m + h); k} ^ {\mathrm {n a t}}) ^ {\top} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} ]\right) \geq \sigma_ {d _ {y} (1 + m + h)} \left(\mathsf {t o e p t r a n s} _ {m + h; k} (g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top})\right) ^ {2} \\ = \sigma_ {d _ {y} (1 + m + h)} \left(\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h; k} (g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top})\right) ^ {2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

concluding the proof of eq. (f.4).

proof of eq. (f.5) this bound is a direct consequence of the following claim, which thereby concludes the proof of lemma f.3.

claim f.6. suppose that  $g$  and  $m$  are of conformable shapes. then,

$$
\| \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \geq \sigma_ {d _ {u} (m + 1)} (\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m; m + h, h} (g ^ {\top})) \left\| \left[ \begin{array}{c} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ m ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \dots \\ m ^ {[ m ]} \end{array} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}
$$

proof. keeping the convention  $m^{[i]} = 0$  for  $i > m$ , we can write

$$
\begin{array}{l} \operatorname {t o e p r o w} _ {h} (g) \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; m + h} (m) \\ = \left[ g ^ {[ 0 ]} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \mid g ^ {[ 0 ]} m ^ {[ 1 ]} + g ^ {[ 1 ]} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \mid \dots \mid \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ h - i ]} \mid \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ 1 + h - i ]} \mid \dots \mid \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ m + h - i ]} \right]. \\ = \left[ \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ 0 ]} \mid \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ 1 ]} \mid \dots \quad \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ m + h ]} \right], \quad \text {w h e r e} \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ j ]} := \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {m + h} \mathbb {i} _ {i \leq h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ j - i ]}. \\ \end{array}
$$

the above block-row matrix has frobenius norm equal to that of the following block-column matrix,

$$
\overline {{\boldsymbol {n}}} := \left[ \begin{array}{c} \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \dots \\ \boldsymbol {n} ^ {[ m + h ]} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {0} \mathbb {i} _ {i \leq h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ - i ]} \\ \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {1} \mathbb {i} _ {i \leq h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ 1 - i ]} \\ \dots \\ \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {m + h} \mathbb {i} _ {i \leq h} g ^ {[ i ]} m ^ {[ m + h - i ]}. \end{array} \right]
$$

which can be expressed as the product

$$
\mathcal {n} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c c c} g ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 & 0 & 0 & \ldots & 0 & \ldots \\ g ^ {[ 1 ]} & g ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 & 0 & \ldots & \ldots & 0 \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ g ^ {[ h ]} & g ^ {[ h - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & g ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & \ldots & 0 \\ 0 & g ^ {[ h ]} & g ^ {[ h - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & g ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ 0 & \ldots & 0 & g ^ {[ h ]} & g ^ {[ h - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & g ^ {[ 0 ]} \end{array} \right] \cdot \left[ \begin{array}{c} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ m ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \ldots \\ m ^ {[ m ]} \\ 0 \\ \ldots \\ 0 \end{array} \right],
$$

where we have use  $m^{[i]} = 0$  for  $i > m$ . let us denote the first  $m$  column blocks of the above matrix as  $x_{m}$ . letting  $g_{h}^{[i]} = \mathbb{i}_{i\leq h}g_{h}^{[i]}$ , we have

$$
x _ {m} := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} g _ {h} ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ g _ {h} ^ {[ 1 ]} & g _ {h} ^ {[ 0 ]} & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots & \dots \\ g _ {h} ^ {[ m ]} & g _ {h} ^ {[ m - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & g _ {h} ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ \dots & \dots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots \\ g _ {h} ^ {[ h + m ]} & g _ {h} ^ {[ m + h - 1 ]} & \ldots & \ldots & \ldots & g _ {h} ^ {[ h ]} \end{array} \right]
$$

then, we have that  $\overline{\mathbf{n}} = x_{m}\left[ \begin{array}{c}m^{[0]}\\ m^{[1]}\\ \dots \\ m^{[m]} \end{array} \right]$  and in particular,

$$
\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {n}}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} \geq \sigma_ {d _ {u} (m + 1)} (x _ {m}) \left\| \left[ \begin{array}{c} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ m ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \dots \\ m ^ {[ m ]} \end{array} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}
$$

to conclude, we recognize  $x_{m}$  are the matrix  $\mathsf{toep}_{m;m + h,h}(g)$ , so that

$$
\left\| \overline {{\boldsymbol {n}}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \geq \sigma_ {d _ {u} (m + 1)} (\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m; m + h, h} (g)) \left\| \left[ \begin{array}{c} m ^ {[ 0 ]} \\ m ^ {[ 1 ]} \\ \dots \\ m ^ {[ m ]} \end{array} \right] \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}}
$$
