# f.3.2 proof of lemma f.4

let  $g = (g^{[i]})_{i\geq 0}$  be a markov operator. we define its z-series as the series

$$
\check {g} (z) := \sum_ {i \geq 0} z ^ {- i} \cdot g ^ {[ i ]}.
$$

our goal is to prove a lower bound on  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(\mathsf{t}\mathsf{o}\mathsf{e}\mathsf{p}_{h;k}(g))$ . introduce a "signal"  $\{\omega^{[i]}\}_{i\geq 0}$ , and annotate the  $\ell_2$ -ball  $\mathcal{w}_h\coloneqq \{\omega :\sum_{i = 0}^h\| \omega^{[i]}\| _2^2\leq 1,\omega^{[i]} = 0,\forall i > h\}$ . let us introduce the convention  $g^{[i]} = 0$  for  $i < 0$ . then, we can express

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {\min } \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g)\right) ^ {2} = \min  _ {\omega \in \mathscr {w} _ {h}} \left\| \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g) \left(\omega^ {[ 0 ]}, \dots , \omega^ {[ h ]}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \min  _ {\omega \in \mathcal {w} _ {h}} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) \leq \ell} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \tag {f.6} \\ \end{array}
$$

let us first pass to the  $k,\ell \to \infty$  limit.

lemma f.7. let  $c_{1} = c_{2}$  if  $\ell \geq k$ , and otherwise, let  $c_{1} = (1 - \tau)$ ,  $c_{2} = \frac{1}{\tau} - 1$  for some  $\tau > 0$ . then, for  $\| g \|_{\ell_{1,\mathrm{op}}} < \infty$ , we have

$$
\sigma_ {\min } (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g)) \geq c _ {1} \min  _ {\omega \in \mathcal {w} _ {h}} \sum_ {i \in \mathbb {z}} \left\| \sum_ {j \in \mathbb {z}} \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) \leq \ell} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - c _ {2} (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (k - h) ^ {2},
$$

where if  $\ell \geq k$ ,  $c_{1}, c_{2}$  sa  $c_{1} = c_{2} = 1$ .

$c_{1}, c_{2}$  satisfy either, or,

proof. for  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathsf {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g) (\omega^ {[ 0 ]}, \dots , \omega^ {[ h ]}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) \leq \ell} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} (g ^ {[ i - j ]} - \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) > \ell} g ^ {[ i - j ]}) \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} (g ^ {[ i - j ]} - \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) > \ell \mathrm {o r} i > k} g ^ {[ i - j ]}) \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last line we use that  $\ell \leq k$ . let us introduce the shorthand  $\mathbb{i}_{i,j} \coloneqq \mathbb{i}_{(i-j) > \ell \mathrm{or} i > k}$ . using the elementary vector inequality  $\|v + w\|_2^2 \geq (1 - \tau)\|v\|_2^2 + (1 - \frac{1}{\tau})\|w\|_2^2$ . this gives

$$
\left\| \mathrm {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g) (\omega^ {[ 0 ]}, \dots , \omega^ {[ h ]}) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq c _ {1} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - c _ {2} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

where  $c_{1} = (1 - \tau)$  and  $c_{2} = (\frac{1}{\tau} - 1)$ , and where all sums converge due to  $\| g \|_{\ell_{1}, \mathrm{op}} < 1$ . moreover, one can see that if  $\ell \geq k$ , then we can simplify the above argument and take  $c_{1} = c_{2} = 1$ , as in this case

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {k} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} (g ^ {[ i - j ]} - \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) > \ell} g ^ {[ i - j ]}) \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {i \geq k} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}
$$

observe that since  $\omega$  has  $\ell_2$ -norm bounded by 1, we have

$$
\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \| [ \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i ]} ] | \dots | \mathbb {i} _ {(i - j) > \ell} g ^ {[ i - h ]} ] \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \leq \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \| \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}
$$

thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \| \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2} \leq \left(\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} \| \mathbb {i} _ {i, j} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right) ^ {2} \\ \leq (h + 1) \left(\sum_ {i \geq 0} \| \mathbb {i} _ {i \leq \min \{\ell , k - h \}} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \| _ {\mathrm {o p}}\right) ^ {2} = (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (\ell \wedge k - h) ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

thus, we conclude that, for any  $\tau > 0$ ,

$$
\left\| \operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; k, \ell} (g) \left(\omega^ {[ 0 ]}, \dots , \omega^ {[ h ]}\right) \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \geq (1 - \tau) \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\infty} \left\| \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {h} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + (1 - \frac {1}{\tau}) (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (\min  \{\ell , k - h \}) ^ {2}.
$$

finally, since  $\omega^{[j]} = 0$  for  $j \notin \{0, \dots, h\}$ , and  $g^{[i - j]} = 0$  for  $j \geq 0$  and  $i < 0$ , we can pass to a double-sum over all indices  $i, j \in \mathbb{z}$ .

next, for each  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ , we introduce

$$
g _ {* \omega} ^ {[ i ]} := (g * \omega) ^ {[ i ]} = \sum_ {j \in \mathbb {z}} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]},
$$

and let  $\tilde{g}_{*\omega}[z]$  denote its z-transform. by the convolution theorem,

$$
\check {g} _ {* \omega} (z) = \check {g} (z) \check {\omega} (z),
$$

where  $\tilde{\omega}(z) = \sum_{i \in \mathbb{z}} \omega^{[i]} z^i$  is the z-transform induced by  $\omega$ . moreover, by parseval's indentity,

$$
\sum_ {i \in \mathbb {z}} \| g _ {* \omega} ^ {[ i ]} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \| \check {g} _ {* \omega} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta = \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {\mathrm {i}} ^ {2 \pi} \| \check {\mathrm {g}} (\mathrm {e} ^ {\iota \theta}) \check {\omega} (\mathrm {e} ^ {\iota \theta} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta = \mathcal {h} _ {[ \mathrm {h} ]} [ \mathrm {g} ] ^ {2}
$$

therefore, for  $c_{1}, c_{2}$  as in lemma f.7,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {\min } \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {h; k} (g)\right) \geq c _ {1} \min  _ {\omega \in \mathcal {w} _ {h}} \sum_ {i \in \mathbb {z}} \left\| \sum_ {j \in \mathbb {z}} g ^ {[ i - j ]} \omega^ {[ j ]} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - c _ {2} (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (\ell \wedge k - h) ^ {2} \\ = c _ {1} \min  _ {\omega \in \mathcal {w} _ {h}} \sum_ {i \in \mathbb {z}} \| g _ {* \omega} ^ {[ i ]} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - c _ {2} (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (\ell \wedge k - h) ^ {2} \\ = c _ {1} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g ] ^ {2} - c _ {2} (h + 1) ^ {2} \psi_ {g} (\ell \wedge k - h) ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

# f.4 proof of proposition 7.1 (strong convexity for the stable case)

for stable systems – that is, systems without a stabilizing controller – we can directly lower bound the strong convexity without passing to the z-transform. this has the advantage of not requiring the conditions on  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{ex}\rightarrow (y,u)}}$  and  $\psi_{g_{\mathrm{noise}}}$  stipulated by theorem 11. as our starting bound, we recall from lemma f.3 the bound that  $f_{t;k}(m)$  are  $\alpha_{\mathrm{loss}}\cdot \underline{\alpha}_{m,h,k}$  strongly-convex, where

$$
\underline {{\alpha}} _ {m, h, k} = \sigma_ {d _ {u} m} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m - 1; m + h - 1, h} (g _ {\mathrm {e x} \rightarrow (y, u)})) ^ {2} \cdot \sigma_ {d _ {y} (m + h)} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} (g _ {\mathrm {n o i s e}} ^ {\top})) ^ {2}
$$

the followign lemma bounds the quantities in the above display, directly implying proposition 7.1:

lemma f.8. for any  $m \geq 1$  and any  $k \geq m + h$ , we have that  $\sigma_{d_u(h + 1)}(\mathsf{toep}_{h;m + h - 1}(g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)})) \geq 1$ , and  $\sigma_{d_u(m + h)}(\mathsf{toep}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\mathrm{noise}}))^2 \geq \sigma_{\mathbf{e}}^2 + \frac{\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^2 \sigma_{\min}(c_\star)^2}{(1 + \|a_\star\|_{\mathrm{op}})^2}$

proof. in the stable case, we have that

$$
g _ {\mathrm {e x} \to (y, u)} ^ {[ i ]} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} g _ {\star} ^ {[ i ]} \\ i _ {d _ {u}} \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0}. \end{array} \right]
$$

thus, for  $m \geq 1$ ,  $\mathsf{toep}_{m-1;m+h-1,h-1}(g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)})$  can be repartioned so as to contain a submatrix  $i_{d_u m \times d_u m}$ . thus,  $\sigma_{d_u m}(\mathsf{toep}_{h;m+h-1}(g_{\mathrm{ex} \to (y,u)})) \geq 1$ .

to lower bound  $\sigma_{d_y(m + h)}(\mathsf{t}\mathsf{o}\mathsf{e}\mathsf{p}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\mathrm{noise}})^\top)^2$ , we use the diagonal covariance lower bound  $\sigma_{\mathrm{noise}} = \left[ \begin{array}{cc}\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^2 i_{d_x} & 0\\ 0 & \sigma_{\mathbf{e}}^2 i_{d_y} \end{array} \right]$ . for this covariance,  $g_{\mathrm{noise}}$  takes the bform

$$
g _ {\text {n o i s e}} ^ {[ i ]} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbb {i} _ {i \geq 1} \cdot (c _ {\star} a ^ {[ i - 1 ]}) ^ {\top} \sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} \\ \mathbb {i} _ {i = 0} \cdot i _ {d _ {u}} \sigma_ {\mathbf {e}}. \end{array} \right]
$$

thus,  $\mathsf{t o e p}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\mathrm{noise}})$  can be partitioned as a two-block row matrix, where one block is an

$$
x = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \sigma_ {\mathbf {e}} \cdot i _ {(h + m) d _ {u}} \\ \mathbf {0} _ {(k - (m + h - 1)) d _ {u} \times (m + h) d _ {u}} \\ \sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} \mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} (g _ {\star , \mathbf {w}} ^ {\top}), \end{array} \right]
$$

where  $g_{\star ,\mathbf{w}} = \mathbb{i}_{i\geq 1}c_{\star}a_{\star}^{i - 1}$ . from this structure (and use the short hand)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {d _ {y} (m + h)} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} \left(g _ {\text {n o i s e}}\right) ^ {\top}\right) ^ {2} \\ = \sigma_ {(m + h) d _ {y}} ^ {2} (x) \\ = \sigma_ {(m + h) d _ {y}} \left(\sigma_ {\mathbf {e}} ^ {2} i _ {(h + m) d _ {y}} + \sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} \operatorname {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} \left(g _ {\star , \mathbf {w}} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {\top} \operatorname {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} \left(g _ {\star , \mathbf {w}} ^ {\top}\right)\right) \\ \geq \sigma_ {\mathbf {e}} ^ {2} + \sigma_ {\mathbf {w}} ^ {2} \cdot \sigma_ {(m + h) d _ {y}} (\mathsf {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} (g _ {\star , \mathbf {w}} ^ {\top})) ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

it remains to lower bound  $\sigma_{(m + h)d_u}(\mathsf{toep}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\star ,\mathbf{w}}^\top))^2$ . we can recognize that, for  $k \geq m + h$ ,  $\mathsf{toep}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\star ,\mathbf{w}}^\top)$  has an  $(m + h)d_y \times (m + h)d_y$  submatrix which takes the form

$$
\left(\operatorname {d i a g} (\underbrace {c _ {\star} , \dots , c _ {\star}} _ {m + h \text {t i m e s}}) \cdot \operatorname {p o w t o e p} _ {m + h} (a _ {\star})\right),
$$

where we have defined

$$
\operatorname {p o w t o e p} _ {p} (a) := \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} i & a & a ^ {2} & \ldots & a ^ {p - 1} \\ 0 & i & a & \ldots & a ^ {p - 2} \\ \ldots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \ldots & i \end{array} \right]
$$

thus,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sigma_ {(m + h) d _ {y}} \left(\operatorname {t o e p} _ {m + h - 1; k} \left(g _ {\star , \mathbf {w}} ^ {\top}\right)\right) \geq \sigma_ {\min } \left(c _ {\star}\right) \cdot \sigma_ {\min } \left(\operatorname {p o w t o e p} _ {m + h} (a)\right) \\ = \sigma_ {\min } \left(c _ {\star}\right) \cdot \left\| \operatorname {p o w t o e p} _ {m + h} (a) ^ {- 1} \right\| _ {\text {o p}} \\ \end{array}
$$

we can verify by direct computation that

$$
\operatorname {p o w t o e p} (a) ^ {- 1} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} i & - a & 0 & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ 0 & i & - a & 0 & \ldots & 0 \\ \ldots \\ 0 & 0 & \ldots & 0 & 0 & i \end{array} \right],
$$

giving  $\| \mathrm{powtoep}_{m + h}(a)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1 + \| a\|_{\mathrm{op}}$  . thus,  $\sigma_{(m + h)d_y}(\mathsf{t o e p}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\star ,\mathbf{w}}^{\top}))\geq \frac{\sigma_{\min}(c_\star)}{1 + \|a\|_{\mathrm{op}}},$  yielding  $\sigma_{d_y(m + h)}(\mathsf{t o e p}_{m + h - 1;k}(g_{\mathrm{noise}})^\top)^2\geq \sigma_{\mathbf{e}}^2 +\sigma_{\mathbf{w}}^2\left(\frac{\sigma_{\min}(c_\star)}{1 + \|a\|_{\mathrm{op}}}\right)^{-1}$  as needed.

# f.5 proof of proposition f.1

the proof of all subsequence lemmas are provided in sequence at the end of the section. as we continue, set  $\mathbb{t} := \{e^{\iota \theta} : \theta \in [2\pi]\} \subset \mathbb{c}$ . for  $\omega \in \mathcal{g}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , we the following signal norms:

definition f.6 (signal norms). for a complex function  $p(z):z\to \mathbb{c}^d$  , we define

$$
\| p \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {2}} ^ {2} := \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \| p (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} d \theta , \quad \| p \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {\infty}} := \max  _ {\theta \in [ 0, 2 \pi ]} \| p (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2}
$$

by parseval's theorem,  $\| \omega \|_{\ell_2} = \| \check{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{h}_2}^2$  whenever  $\| \omega \|_{\ell_2} < \infty$ . importantly, whenever  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ , the signals  $\check{\omega}$  are not too "peaked", in the sense that they have bounded  $\mathcal{h}_{\infty}$ -norm:

lemma f.9. suppose that  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ . then,  $\check{\omega}$  is a rational function,  $\| \check{\omega} \|_{\mathcal{h}_2} = 1$ , and  $\| \check{\omega} \|_{\mathcal{h}_{\infty}}^2 \leq h + 1$ .

using this property, we show that integrating against  $\check{\omega}$ , for  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ , is lower bounded by integrating against the indicator function of a set with mass proportional to  $1 / h$ :

lemma f.10 (holder converse). let  $\mathcal{c}(\epsilon)$  denote the set of lebesgue measure subsets  $\mathcal{c} \subseteq [0,2\pi]$  with lebesgue measure  $|\mathcal{c}| \geq \epsilon$ . then,

$$
\mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g ] = \min  _ {\omega \in \mathscr {w} _ {h}} \| \check {g} (z) \check {\omega} (z) \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {2}} \geq \frac {1}{8 \pi} \cdot \min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} (\frac {\pi}{h + 1})} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c}} \sigma_ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} \left(\check {g} (e ^ {\iota \theta})\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta
$$

the next steps of the proof argue that the function  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\left(\check{g}(e^{\iota \theta})\right)^2$  can be lower bounded by a function which, roughly speaking, cannot spend "too much time" close to zero. first, we verify that  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(g(z))$  can only reach zero finitely many times:

lemma f.11. let  $g = (a, b, c, d)$  be a markov operator from  $\mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}} \to \mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{o}}}$ . suppose that  $d_{\mathrm{o}} \geq d_{\mathrm{in}}$ , and  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(\mathcal{d}) > 0$ . then,  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(\mathcal{g}(z)) = 0$  for at most finitely many  $z \in \mathbb{c}$ .

using this property, we lower bound  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(\tilde{g}(e^{\iota \theta}))^2$  by an analytic function. recall that  $f: \mathbb{r} \to \mathbb{r}$  is analytic if it is infinitely differentiable, and for each  $x \in \mathbb{r}$ , there exists a radius  $r$  such that, for all  $\delta \in (0, r)$ , the taylor series of  $f$  converges on  $(x - \delta, x + \delta)$ , as is equal to  $f$ .

lemma f.12. there exists a non-negative, analytic, function  $f: \mathbb{r} \to \mathbb{r}$ , which is not identically zero such that, for any  $\theta \in \mathbb{r}$ ,  $\sigma_{\min}(\tilde{g}(e^{\iota \theta}))^2 \geq f(\theta)$ , for all  $\theta \in \mathbb{r}$ .

finally, we use the fact that analytic functions cannot spend "too much time" close to zero (unless of course they vanish identically):

lemma f.13. let  $f: \mathbb{r} \to \mathbb{r}$  be a real analytic, nonnegative, periodic function with period  $2\pi$ , which is not identically zero. then, there exists a constant  $c > 0$  and  $n \in \mathbb{n}$  depending on  $f$  such that the following holds: for all  $\epsilon \in (0, 2\pi]$ , and any set  $\mathcal{c} \subseteq [0, 2\pi]$  of lebesgue measure  $|\mathcal{c}| \geq \epsilon$ , then,

$$
\int_ {\mathcal {c}} f (\theta) \mathrm {d} \theta \geq c \epsilon^ {n}.
$$

the proof of proposition f.1 follows from applying the above lemmas in sequence. recalling that  $\mathcal{c}\left(\frac{\pi}{h + 1}\right)$  denotes the set of  $\mathcal{c} \subset [0,2\pi]$  with  $|\mathcal{c}| \geq \frac{\pi}{h + 1}$ , for some non-vanishing, periodic, analytic  $f$ , we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {h} _ {[ h ]} [ g (z) ] \geq \frac {1}{8 \pi} \cdot \min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} (\frac {\pi}{h + 1})} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c}} \sigma_ {d _ {\text {i n}}} \left(\check {g} \left(e ^ {\iota \theta}\right)\right) ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta (lemma f.10) \\ \geq \frac {1}{8 \pi} \cdot \min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} \left(\frac {\pi}{h + 1}\right)} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c}} f (\theta) \mathrm {d} \theta (lemma f.12) \\ \geq \frac {c}{8 \pi} \min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} \left(\frac {\pi}{h + 1}\right)} | \mathcal {c} | ^ {n} (lemma f.13) \\ \geq \frac {c ^ {\prime}}{8 \pi} (h + 1) ^ {n}, \\ \end{array}
$$

for some  $c' > 0$ , and  $n \in \mathbb{n}$ .

# f.5.1 proof of lemma f.9

proof. since  $\check{\omega}(z) = \sum_{i=0}^{h} \omega^{[i]} z^{-i}$ ,  $\check{\omega}(z)$  is rational. the bound  $\|\check{\omega}\|_{\mathcal{h}_2} = 1$  follows from parseval's identity with  $\sum_{i \geq 0} \|\omega^{[i]}\|_2 = 1$  for  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ . the third point explicitly uses that  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$  is an  $h + 1$ -length signal. namely, by cauchy-schwartz,

$$
\| \check {\omega} (z) \| _ {2} = \| \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} z ^ {i} \omega^ {[ i ]} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \sqrt {\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} | z ^ {i} | ^ {2}} \sqrt {\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} \| \omega^ {[ i ]} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \stackrel {(i)} {=} \sqrt {\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {h} | z ^ {i} | ^ {2}},
$$

where we use that the  $\ell_2$  norm of  $\omega$  is bounded by 1. if  $z = e^{\iota \theta}$ , then  $|z^i|^2 = 1$ , so  $\|\check{\omega}(z)\|_2 \leq \sqrt{h + 1}$ .

# f.5.2 proof of lemma f.10

we argue that rational functions  $p$  with unit  $\mathcal{h}_2$ -norm and bounded  $\mathcal{h}_{\infty}$  norm must be large on a set of sufficiently large measure:

lemma f.14. let  $|\cdot|$  denote lebesgue measure. let  $p$  be a rational function on  $\mathbb{c}$  with  $\| p\|_{\mathcal{h}_2} = 1$  and  $\| p\|_{\mathcal{h}_{\infty}}^2 \leq b$ . then, there exists a lebesgue measurable  $\mathcal{c}$  which is a finite union of intervals with lebesgue measure  $|\mathcal{c}| \geq \frac{\pi}{b}$  for which

$$
\forall z \in \mathcal {c}, \| p (z) \| _ {2} \geq \frac {1}{2}.
$$

proof. let  $\mathcal{c}_t\coloneqq \{\theta \in [0,2\pi ]:\| p(e^{\iota \theta})\| _2\geq t\}$ , which is lebesgue measurable by rationality of  $p$ . then, by a chebyshev-like arugment,

$$
\begin{array}{l} 1 = \| p \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {2}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c} _ {t}} \| p (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| + \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {\theta \in [ 0, 2 \pi ] - \mathcal {c} _ {t}} \| p (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| ^ {2} \\ \leq \frac {\left| \mathcal {c} _ {t} \right| \| p \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {\infty}} ^ {2}}{2 \pi} + (1 - \frac {\left| \mathcal {c} _ {t} \right|}{2 \pi}) t \\ \leq \frac {b | \mathcal {c} _ {t} |}{2 \pi} + t \\ \end{array}
$$

hence,  $\frac{|\mathcal{c}_t|}{2\pi} \geq \frac{1 - t}{b}$ . in particular, if we  $\mathcal{c} = \mathcal{c}_{1/2}$ , then  $\frac{|\mathcal{c}_t|}{2\pi} \geq \frac{1}{2b}$ , as needed.

we can now prove lemma f.10 as follows. for each  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ , let  $\mathcal{c}_{\omega}$  denote the corresponding subset of  $[0,2\pi]$  guaranteed by lemma f.14, that is  $\| \check{\omega}(e^{\iota \theta}) \|_2 \geq \frac{1}{2} \mathbb{i}(\theta \in \mathcal{c}_{\omega})$ . then, for  $\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \check {g} (z) \omega (z) \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {\infty}} = \frac {1}{2 \pi} \min  _ {\omega \in \mathscr {w} _ {h}} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \| g (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \check {\omega} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} d \theta \\ \geq \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma_ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} (g (e ^ {\iota \theta})) ^ {2} \| \check {\omega} (e ^ {\iota \theta}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta \\ \geq \frac {1}{2 \pi} \int_ {0} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma_ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} (g (e ^ {\iota \theta})) ^ {2} \cdot (\frac {1}{2} \mathbb {i} (\theta \in \mathcal {c} _ {\omega})) ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta \\ = \frac {1}{8 \pi} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c} _ {\omega}} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma_ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} (g (e ^ {\iota \theta})) ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta . \\ \end{array}
$$

since each set  $\mathcal{c}_{\omega} \subset [0,2\pi]$  has lebesgue measure at least  $\pi/(h+1)$ , and since  $\mathcal{c}(\pi/(h+1))$  denotes the collection of all subsets with this property,  $\min_{\omega \in \mathcal{w}_h} \| \tilde{g}(z)\omega(z) \|_{\mathcal{h}_\infty}$  is lower bounded by

$$
\min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} (\pi / (h + 1))} \frac {1}{8 \pi} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c} _ {\omega}} ^ {2 \pi} \sigma_ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} (g (e ^ {\iota \theta})) ^ {2} \mathrm {d} \theta ,
$$

as needed.

# f.5.3 proof of lemma f.11

proof. next, note that if  $g = (a,b,c,d)$ , then  $\check{g}(z) = d + c(zi - a)^{-1}b$ . since  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(d) > 0$ , there exists a projection matrix  $p \in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}d_{\mathrm{o}}}$  such that  $pd$  is rank  $d_{\mathrm{in}}$ . moreover, if  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(p\check{g}(z)) = 0$ , then  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}\left(\check{g}(z)\right) = 0$ , so it suffices to show that  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(pg(z)) = 0$  for only finitely many  $z$ . since  $pg(z) \in \mathbb{r}^{d_{\mathrm{in}} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  is square-matrix valued, it suffices to show that determinant  $\operatorname*{det}(pg(z)) = 0$  for at most finitely  $z$ . since  $\operatorname*{det}$  is a polynomial function, and  $p\check{g}(z)$  has rational-function entries, (this can be verified by using cramers rule), there exists polynomials  $f, g$  such that  $\operatorname*{det}(pg(z)) = \frac{f(z)}{g(z)}$  for  $z \in \mathbb{c}$ . this means that either  $\operatorname*{det}(pg(z)) = 0$  for all  $z \in \mathbb{c}$ , or is identically zero on  $\mathbb{c}$ . let us show that the second option is not possible. consider taking  $z \to \infty$  (on the real axis). then  $\lim_{z \to \infty} \check{g}(z) = \lim_{z \to \infty} d + c(zi - a)^{-1}b = d$ . hence,  $\lim_{z \to \infty} \operatorname*{det}(pg(z)) = \operatorname*{det}(pd) > 0$ , since  $\sigma_{d_{\mathrm{in}}}(pd) > 0$ .

# f.5.4 proof of lemma f.12

proof. we have the following lower bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lambda_ {\min } (\check {g} (z) ^ {\mathsf {h}} \check {g} (z)) = \frac {\prod_ {i = 1} ^ {d _ {\text {i n}}} \lambda_ {i} \check {g} (z) ^ {\mathsf {h}} \check {g} (z)}{\prod_ {i = 1} ^ {d _ {\text {i n}} - 1} \lambda_ {i} \check {g} (z) ^ {\mathsf {h}} \check {g} (z)} \\ \geq \frac {\prod_ {i = 1} ^ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}}} \lambda_ {i} \check {g} (z) ^ {\mathsf {h}} \check {g} (z)}{\left(\max  _ {z \in \mathbb {t}} \| \check {g} (z) \| _ {\mathrm {o p}} ^ {2}\right) ^ {d _ {\mathrm {i n}} - 1}} \\ \geq \frac {\operatorname* {d e t} (\check {g} (z) ^ {\mathsf {h}} \check {g} (z))}{\| \check {g} \| _ {\mathcal {h} _ {\infty}} ^ {2 (d _ {\mathrm {i n}} - 1)}} := \phi (z) \\ \end{array}
$$

by assumption,  $\| \tilde{g}(z) \|_{\mathcal{h}_{\infty}} < \infty$ , and so  $\phi(z)$  only vanishes when  $\det(\tilde{g}(z)^{\mathsf{h}} \tilde{g}(z))$  does, which itself only vanishes when  $\lambda_{\min}(\tilde{g}(z)^{\mathsf{h}} \tilde{g}(z)) = 0$ . by lemma f.11, this means that  $\phi(z)$  is not identically zero on  $\mathbb{t}$ .

now, let  $\phi (\theta) = \operatorname *{det}(\tilde{g} (e^{\iota \theta})^{\mathsf{h}}\tilde{g} (e^{\iota \theta}))$ . it suffices to show that this function is real analytic. we argue by expressing  $\phi (\theta) = \phi_2(\phi_1(\theta))$ , where  $\phi_{1}$  is a real analytic map from  $\mathbb{r}\to (\mathbb{r}^2)^{d_\circ \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , and  $\phi_{2}$  an analytic map from  $(\mathbb{r}^2)^{d_\circ \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}\rightarrow \mathbb{r}$ .

let  $\operatorname{embed}:\mathbb{c}^{d_{\mathrm{o}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}\to (\mathbb{r}^{2})^{d_{\mathrm{o}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$  denote the cannonical complex to real embedding. we define  $\phi_1(\theta) =$ $\operatorname {embed}(\check{g} (e^{\iota \theta})$  . to see that  $\phi_1(\theta)$  is real analytic, we observe that the map  $z\mapsto e^{\iota \theta}$  is complex analytic, and since  $\tilde{g}$  is a rational function,  $u\mapsto \tilde{g} (u)$  is analytic away from the poles of  $\tilde{g}$  . since  $\tilde{g}$  has no poles  $u\in \mathbb{t}$  (by assumption of stability/bounded  $\mathcal{h}_{\infty}$  norm), we conclude that  $z\mapsto \tilde{g} (e^{\iota z})$  is complex analytic at any  $z\in \mathbb{r}$  . thus,  $\theta \mapsto \operatorname {embed}(\tilde{g} (e^{\iota z}))$  is real analytic for  $\theta \in \mathbb{r}$

second, given  $x \in (\mathbb{r}^2)^{d_{\circ} \times d_{\mathrm{in}}}$ , let  $\phi_2(x) = \operatorname*{det}((\operatorname{embed}^{-1}(x))^{\mathsf{h}}(\operatorname{embed}^{-1}(x)))$ . it is easy to see that  $\phi_2(x)$  is a polynomial in the entries of  $x$ , and thus also real analytic. immediately, we verify that  $\phi(\theta) = \phi_2(\phi_1(\theta))$ , demonstrating that  $\phi$  is given by the composition of two real analytic maps, and therefore real analytic.

# f.5.5 proof of lemma f.13

we begin with a simple claim:

claim f.15.  $f$  has finitely many zeros on  $[0,2\pi ]$

proof. since  $f$  is real analytic on  $\mathbb{r}$ , it can be extended to a complex analytic function  $\bar{f}$  on an open subset  $u \subset \mathbb{c}$  containing the real line  $\mathbb{r}$ . since  $f$  is not identically zero on  $\mathbb{r}$  assumption,  $\bar{f}$  is not identically zero on  $\mathbb{r}$ , and thus by "principle of permanence",  $\overline{f}$  can have no accumulation points of zeros on  $u$ . in particular, its restriction  $f$  can have no accumulation points of zeros on  $\mathbb{r}$ . as  $[0,2\pi]$  is compact,  $f$  has finitely many zeros on  $[0,2\pi]$ .

we now turn to the proof of our intended lemma:

proof of lemma f.13. let  $\theta_1, \ldots, \theta_m$  denote the zeros on  $f(\theta)$  which lie on  $[0, 2\pi)$ , of which there are finitely many by the above argument. the taylor coefficients of  $f$  cannot be all zero at any of these  $\theta_i$ , for otherwise analyticity would imply that  $f$  would locally vanish. thus, by taylor's theorem, at each zero  $\theta_i$ , we have that for some constants  $c_i > 0$ ,  $r_i > 0$ ,  $n_i \in \mathbb{n}$ ,

$$
f (\theta) \geq c _ {i} | \theta - \theta_ {i} | ^ {n _ {i}}, \forall \theta \in \mathbb {r}: | \theta - \theta_ {i} | \leq r _ {i}
$$

letting  $c = \min_i c_i$ ,  $n = \max_i n_i$ , and  $r_i = \min \{1, \min_i r_i\}$ , we have that for all

$$
f (\theta) \geq c | \theta - \theta_ {i} | ^ {n}, \forall \theta \in \mathbb {r}: | \theta - \theta_ {i} | \leq r
$$

by shrinking  $r$  if necessary, we may assume that the intervals  $i_{i} = [\theta_{i} - r,\theta_{i} + r]$  are disjoint, and that there exists a number  $\theta_0$  such that  $[\theta_0,2\pi +\theta_0]\supset \bigcup_{i = 1}^{m}i_{i}$ . by periodicity, one can check then  $f(\theta)$  only vanishes on  $[\theta_0,2\pi +\theta_0]$  at  $\{\theta_1,\ldots ,\theta_m\} \subset \bigcup_{i = 1}^{m}\mathrm{interior}(i_i)$ . compactness of the set  $s\coloneqq [\theta_0,2\pi +\theta_0] - \bigcup_{i = 1}^{m}\mathrm{interior}(i_i)$  and the fact that  $f(\theta)\neq 0$  for all  $\theta \in s$  implies that  $\inf_{\theta \in s}f(\theta) > 0$ . by shrinking  $c$  if necessary, we may assume  $\inf_{\theta \in s}f(\theta)\geq c$ . therefore, we have shown that

$$
\forall \theta \in [ \theta_ {0}, 2 \pi + \theta_ {0} ], \quad f (\theta) \geq \underline {{\mathrm {f}}} (\theta) := c \left(\mathbb {i} \left(\min  _ {i \in [ m ]} | \theta - \theta_ {i} | > r\right) + \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {i} ((\theta - \theta_ {i}) > r) | \theta - \theta_ {i} | ^ {n}\right).
$$

now, let  $\mathcal{c}(\epsilon)$  denote the set of subsets  $\mathcal{c} \subset [\theta_0, 2\pi + \theta_0]$  with lebesgue measure  $\epsilon$ . by translation invariance of the lebesgue measure, and periodicity of  $f(\theta)$ , it suffices to show that, for constants  $c', n'$ , the following holds for all  $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ , the following holds

$$
\min  _ {\mathcal {c} \in \mathcal {c} (\epsilon)} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c}} \underline {{\mathrm {f}}} (\theta) \mathrm {d} \theta \geq c ^ {\prime} \epsilon^ {n ^ {\prime}}. \tag {f.7}
$$

in fact, by shrinking  $c'$  if necessary, it suffices to show that the above holds only for  $\epsilon \in (0, 2mr)$ . examining the above display, we that any set of the form  $\mathcal{c} \coloneqq \{\theta : \underline{\mathrm{f}}(\theta) \leq t\}$  with  $|\mathcal{c}| = \epsilon$  is a minimizer. assuming the restriction  $\epsilon < 2mr$ , this implies that the minimum (f.7) is attained by the set  $\mathcal{c}_{\epsilon} \coloneqq \bigcup_{i} i_i(\epsilon)$ , where we define the intervals  $i_i(\epsilon) = [\theta_i - \epsilon/2m, \theta_i + \epsilon/2m]$ . we can compute then that,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \int_ {\theta \in \mathcal {c} _ {\epsilon}} \underline {{\mathrm {f}}} (\theta) \mathrm {d} \theta = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {\theta_ {i} - \epsilon / 2 m} ^ {\theta_ {i} - \epsilon / 2 m} \underline {{\mathrm {f}}} (\theta) \mathrm {d} \theta \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {\theta_ {i} - \epsilon / 2 m} ^ {\theta_ {i} - \epsilon / 2 m} c | \theta - \theta_ {i} | ^ {n} d \theta \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \int_ {- \epsilon / 2 m} ^ {\epsilon / 2 m} c | \theta | ^ {n} d \theta \\ = 2 c m \int_ {0} ^ {\epsilon / 2 m} | r | ^ {n} d \theta \\ = \frac {2 c m}{n + 1} (\epsilon / 2 m) ^ {n + 1}, \\ \end{array}
$$

which has the desired form.

# g gradient descent with conditional strong convexity

we begin by recalling conditions 7.1, 7.2 and 8.1 under which we argue the subsequent bounds. first:

condition 7.1 (unary regularity condition (urc) for conditionally-strongly convex losses). suppose that  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$ . let  $f_t \coloneqq \mathcal{k} \to \mathbb{r}$  denote a sequence of functions and  $(\mathcal{f}_t)_{t \geq 1}$  a filtration. we suppose  $f_t$  is  $l_{\mathrm{f}}$ -lipschitz, and  $\max_{x \in \mathcal{k}} \| \nabla^2 f_t(x) \|_{\mathrm{op}} \leq \beta$ , and that  $f_{t;k}(x) \coloneqq \mathbb{e}[f_t(x) \mid \mathcal{f}_{t-k}]$  is  $\alpha$ -strongly convex on  $\mathcal{k}$ .

note that, by jensen's inequality,  $f_{t;k}$  are  $\beta$ -smooth and  $l_{\mathrm{f}}$ -lipschitz on  $\mathcal{k}$ . second, we recall the with-memory analogue:

condition 7.2 (with-memory regularity condition (w mrc)). suppose that  $\mathcal{k} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  and  $h \geq 1$ . we let  $f_t \coloneqq \mathcal{k}^{h+1} \to \mathbb{r}$  be a sequence of  $l_{\mathrm{c}}$  coordinatewise-lipschitz functions with the induced unary functions  $f_t(x) \coloneqq f_t(x, \ldots, x)$  satisfying condition 7.1.

lastly, we formalize the fashion in which the iterates are generated:

condition 8.1. we suppose that  $z_{t + 1} = \pi_{\mathcal{k}}(z_t - \eta g_t)$ , where  $\pmb{g}_t = \nabla f_t(z_t) + \pmb{\epsilon}_t$ . we further assume that the gradient descent iterates applied for  $t \geq t_0$  for some  $t_0 \leq k$ , with  $z_0 = z_1 = \dots = z_{t_0} \in \mathcal{k}$ . we assume that  $\| g_t\| _2 \leq l_{\mathbf{g}}$ , and  $\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{k}) \leq d$ .

the remainder of the section is as follows. section g.1 proves lemma g.1, which relates the regret on the non-conditioned unary sequence  $f_{t}$  to standard strongly convex  $\frac{\log t}{\alpha}$ , plus additional correction for the errors  $\epsilon_{t}$ , the negative regret, and a correction  $\epsilon_{t}^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  for the mismatch between  $f_{t}$  and  $f_{t;k}$ . for  $k = 0$ ,  $f_{t;k} = t_{t}$  and  $\epsilon_{t}^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  is zero, recovering proposition 8.1. next, section g.2 proves lemma g.2, which bounds the terms  $\epsilon_{t}^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  in terms of a mean-zero sequence  $z_{t}(z_{\star})$  depending on the comparator  $z_{\star}$ .

next, section g.3.1 states and proves our main high-probability regret bound for unary functions, theorem. lastly, section g.3.3 extends

# g.1 basic regret lemma and proposition 8.1

we begin by proving the following "basic" inequality for the unary setting, which provides a key intermediate regret bound addressing both conditional strong convexity and error in the gradients, as well as incorporating negative regret:

lemma g.1 (basic inequality for conditional-expectation regret). consider the setting of conditions 7.1 and 8.1. for step size  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \forall z _ {\star} \in \mathcal {k}, \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t; k} (z _ {\star}) \leq - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {6 l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log (t + 1) \\ + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \pmb {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \pmb {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle + \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{2}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where we define  $\pmb{\epsilon}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}}\coloneqq \nabla f_t(z_t) - \nabla f_{t;k}(z_t)$

note that proposition 8.1 in the body arises in the special case where  $k = 0$ . for  $k > 1$ , the error  $\epsilon_t^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  is required to relate the updates based on  $\nabla f_t(z_t)$  to those based on  $\nabla f_{t;k}(z_t)$ , the latter of which corresponding to functions which are strongly convex.

proof. let  $\pmb{g}_t \coloneqq \nabla f_t(z_t) + \pmb{\epsilon}_t$ . from [hazan et al., 2016, eq. 3.4], strong convexity of  $f_{t;k}$  implies that

$$
2 \left(f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {\star}\right)\right) \leq 2 \nabla_ {t; k} ^ {\top} \left(z _ {t} - z _ {\star}\right) - \alpha \| z _ {\star} - z _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \tag {g.1}
$$

now, we let gradient descent correspond to the update  $\mathbf{y}_{t + 1} = z_t - \eta_{t + 1}\pmb {g}_t$ , where  $\pmb {g}_t = \nabla_{t;k} + \pmb {\epsilon}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}} + \pmb {\epsilon}_t$ , for stochastic error  $\pmb{\epsilon}_{t}^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  and deterministic noise  $\pmb{\epsilon}_{t}$ . the pythagorean theorem implies

$$
\left\| z _ {t + 1} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} - \eta_ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \eta_ {t + 1} ^ {2} \left\| \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} \right\| ^ {2} - 2 \eta_ {t + 1} \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left(z _ {t} - z _ {\star}\right), \tag {g.2}
$$

which can be re-expressed as

$$
- 2 \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} ^ {\top} \left(z _ {t} - z _ {\star}\right) \geq \frac {\left\| z _ {t + 1} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} - \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\eta_ {t + 1}} - \eta_ {t + 1} \| \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} \| ^ {2} \tag {g.3}
$$

furthermore, using the elementary inequality  $ab \leq \frac{a^2}{2\tau} + \frac{\tau}{2} b^2$  for any  $a, b$  and  $\tau > 0$ , we have that for any  $\tau > 0$

$$
\begin{array}{l} - \left\langle \boldsymbol {g} _ {t}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle = - \left\langle \nabla_ {t; k} + \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle - \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle \\ \leq - \left\langle \nabla_ {t; k} + \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle + \frac {\alpha \tau}{2} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{2 \alpha \tau} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \tag {g.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

combining equations (g.3) and (g.4), and rearranging,

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \overline {{\nabla}} _ {t; k} ^ {\top} \left(z _ {t} - z _ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {\left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| z _ {t + 1} - z _ {\star} \right\| ^ {2}}{\eta_ {t + 1}} + \eta_ {t + 1} \| \boldsymbol {g} _ {t} \| ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\tau \alpha} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \tau \alpha \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| ^ {2} - 2 \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle \\ \leq \frac {\left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| ^ {2} - \left\| z _ {t + 1} - z _ {\star} \right\| ^ {2}}{\eta_ {t + 1}} + 2 \eta_ {t + 1} l ^ {2} + \left(2 \eta_ {t + 1} + \frac {1}{\tau \alpha}\right) \left\| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \tau \alpha \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| ^ {2} - 2 \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle , \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used  $\| g_t\| _2^2\leq 2(\| \nabla f(z_t)\| _2^2 +\| \pmb {\epsilon}_t\| _2^2)\leq 2(l^2 +\| \pmb {\epsilon}_t\| _2^2)$ . combining with (g.1), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t; k} (z _ {\star}) \leq \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left(\frac {1}{\eta_ {t + 1}} - \frac {1}{\eta_ {t}} - (1 - \tau) \alpha\right) \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} 2 \eta_ {t + 1} l ^ {2} + \left(\frac {1}{\tau \alpha} + 2 \eta_ {t + 1}\right) \| \pmb {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \pmb {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle + \frac {\| z _ {k + 1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\eta_ {1 + k}} \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, let us set  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ ,  $\tau = \frac{1}{3}$ , and recall  $d = \mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{k})$  and  $\| \nabla_t \|_2^2 \leq l_{\mathrm{f}}^2$ . then, we have that

1.  $\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{t}\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{t+1}} - \frac{1}{\eta_t} - (1 - \tau)\alpha\right)\|z_t - z_\star\|_2^2 = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{t}(\alpha/3 - 2\alpha/3)\|z_t - z_\star\|_2^2$ , which is equal to  $\frac{-\alpha}{6}\sum_{t=1}^{t}\|z_t - z_\star\|$  
2.  $\left(\frac{1}{\tau\alpha} + 2\eta_{t+1}\right) \leq \frac{6}{\alpha}$ , and  $2\sum_{t=k+1}^{t}\eta_{t+1}l_{\mathrm{f}}^{2} \leq 2 \cdot 3l_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\log(t+1)/\alpha$  
3.  $\frac{\|z_{k + 1}\|_2^2}{\eta_{1 + k}}\leq \alpha (k + 1)d^2 /3.$

putting things together,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {\star}\right) \leq - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + 6 l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \log (t + 1) \\ + \frac {3}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle + \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{3} \\ \end{array}
$$

finally, to conclude, we bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{3} - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {(k + 1) \alpha d ^ {2}}{3} + \frac {(k + 1) \alpha d ^ {2}}{6} - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = \frac {(k + 1) \alpha d ^ {2}}{2} - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/17b95a59edea0d54859e007eb390e53c99ddf77c1a03b4f64e72fc372f422657.jpg)

# g.2 de-biasing the stochastic error

the next step in the proof is to unpack the stochastic error term from lemma g.1, yielding a bound in terms of a mean-zero sequence  $z_{t}(z_{\star})$ :

lemma g.2 (de-biased regret inequality). under conditions 7.1 and 8.1 step size  $\eta_t = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ , the following bound holds deterministically for any  $z_\star \in \mathcal{k}$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) \leq \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{2} + \frac {6 l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k l _ {\mathbf {g}} (6 \beta + 1 2 l _ {\mathrm {f}})}{\alpha} \log (t + 1) + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) - \frac {\alpha}{6} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we define  $z_{t}(z_{\star})\coloneqq (f_{t;k} - f)(z_{t - k}) - (f_{t;k} - f)(z_{\star}) + \langle \nabla (f_{t} - f_{t;k})(z_{t - k}),z_{t - k} - z_{\star}\rangle .$

one can readily check that  $\mathbb{e}[z_t(z_\star) \mid \mathcal{f}_{t - k}] = 0$ .

proof. let  $z_{\star} \in \mathcal{k}$  denote an arbitrary competitor point. we recall that  $f_{t;k} \coloneqq \mathbb{e}[f_t \mid \mathcal{f}_{t-k}]$ , and set  $\pmb{\epsilon}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}} \coloneqq \nabla f_t(z_t) - \nabla f_{t;k}(z_t)$ . proceeding from lemma g.1, there are two challenges: (a) first, we wish to convert a regret bound on the conditional expectations  $f_{t;k}$  of the functions to the actual functions  $f_t$  and (b) the errors  $\langle \pmb{\epsilon}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}}, z_t - z_{\star} \rangle$  do not form a martingale sequence, because the errors  $\pmb{\epsilon}_t^{\mathrm{stoch}}$  are correlated with  $z_t$ . we adress both points with a decoupling argument. begin by writing

$$
\boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\mathrm {s t o c h}} = \nabla f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - \nabla f _ {t; k} (z _ {t}) = \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t - k}) + \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t}) - \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t - k}).
$$

we then have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle = \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \nabla \left(f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}\right) \left(z _ {t - k}\right), z _ {t - k} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle \\ + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \nabla \left(f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}\right) \left(z _ {t}\right) - \nabla \left(f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}\right) \left(z _ {t - k}\right), z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle + \left\langle \nabla \left(f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}\right) \left(z _ {t - k}\right), z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \right\rangle \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $f_{t;k}$  and  $f_t$  are  $l$ -lipschitz and  $\beta$ -smooth, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \langle \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t}) - \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t - k}), z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \rangle + \langle \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t; k}) (z _ {t - k}), z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \rangle \right| \\ \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} 2 \beta \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \| _ {2} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} + 2 l _ {\mathrm {f}} \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \| \quad \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} 2 (\beta d + l _ {\mathrm {f}}) \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \|. \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly, we decouple,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t; k} (z _ {\star}) = \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left(f _ {t; k} - f\right) (z _ {t - k}) - \left(f _ {t; k} - f\right) (z _ {\star}) \\ + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t - k}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) + f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t - k}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

similarly, we can bound

$$
\left| \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t - k}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) + f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t - k}\right) \right| \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {2} 2 l _ {\mathrm {f}} \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \|.
$$

putting the above together, we find that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t; k} (z _ {\star}) + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle \leq \underbrace {\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star})} _ {(i)} \\ + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \underbrace {(f _ {t ; k} - f) (z _ {t - k}) - (f _ {t ; k} - f) (z _ {\star}) + \langle \nabla (f _ {t} - f _ {t ; k}) (z _ {t - k}) , z _ {t - k} - z _ {\star} \rangle} _ {:= z _ {t} (z _ {\star})} + \underbrace {\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} (2 \beta d + 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}}) \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - k} \|} _ {(i i i. a)}, \\ \end{array}
$$

to conclude, let us bound the term (iii.a):

$$
\begin{array}{l} (i i i. a) \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} (2 \beta d + 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}}) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \| z _ {t - i} - z _ {t - k - j} \| \\ \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} (2 \beta d + 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}}) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \eta_ {t + 1} \| \nabla f _ {t} (z _ {t}) + \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| \\ \leq k l _ {\mathbf {g}} \left(2 \beta d + 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}}\right) \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \eta_ {t + 1} \leq \frac {3}{\alpha} \cdot k l _ {\mathbf {g}} \left(2 \beta d + 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}}\right) \log (t + 1). \\ \end{array}
$$

hence,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t; k} \left(z _ {\star}\right) + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} ^ {\text {s t o c h}}, z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\rangle \\ \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} (z _ {\star}) + \frac {k l _ {\mathbf {g}} (6 \beta d + 1 2 l _ {\mathrm {f}})}{\alpha} \log (t + 1), \\ \end{array}
$$

lemma g.2 follows directly from combining the above with lemma g.1.

# g.3 high probability regret

# g.3.1 high probability for unary functions

our main high-probability guarantee for unary functions is as follows:

theorem 12. consider a sequence of functions  $f_{1}, f_{2}, \ldots$  satisfying conditions 7.1 and 8.1. then, with step size  $\eta_{t} = \frac{3}{\alpha t}$ , the following bound holds with probability  $1 - \delta$  for all  $z_{\star} \in \mathcal{k}$  simultaneously:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) - \left(\frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \\ \lesssim \alpha k d ^ {2} + \frac {k d l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k l _ {\mathrm {f}} l _ {\mathbf {g}} + k \beta l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

proof. starting from lemma g.2, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{2} + \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k (\beta + l _ {\mathrm {f}}) l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha}\right) \log (t + 1) + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \underbrace {\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}} _ {(i)} - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left\| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we recall  $z_{t}(z_{\star}) \coloneqq (f_{t;k} - f)(z_{t - k}) - (f_{t;k} - f)(z_{\star}) + \langle \nabla (f_{t} - f_{t;k})(z_{t - k}), z_{t - k} - z_{\star} \rangle$ . we now state a high-probability upper bound on term (i), proved in section g.3.2 below:

lemma g.3 (point-wise concentration). fix a  $z_{\star} \in \mathcal{k}$ . then, with probability  $1 - \delta$ , the following bound holds

$$
\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} (z _ {\star}) - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha}\right) \log \left(\frac {k (1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha t d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right),
$$

where  $\log_+(x) = \log (x \vee 1)$ .

together with  $k \leq t$  and some algebra, the following holds probability  $1 - \delta$  for any fixed  $z_{\star} \in \mathcal{k}$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) \leq \frac {\alpha d ^ {2} (k + 1)}{2} + \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k (\beta + l _ {\mathrm {f}}) l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha}\right) \log (t + 1) + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ + \mathcal {o} \left(\frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha}\right) \log \left(\frac {t (1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

to extend from a fixed  $z_{\star}$  to a uniform bound, we adopt a covering argument. note that the only terms that depend explicitly on the comparators  $z_{\star}$  are  $-f_t(z_{\star})$  and  $\| z_t - z_{\star}\| _2^2$ . we then establish the following bound:

claim g.4. let  $\mathcal{n}$  denote a  $d / t$ -cover of  $\mathcal{k}$ . then, for any  $z_{\star} \in \mathcal{k}$ , there exists a  $z \in \mathcal{n}$  with

$$
\left| - \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \left(f _ {t} \left(z _ {\star}\right) - f _ {t} (z)\right) + \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \| z _ {t} - z \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right| \lesssim \frac {l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} + \alpha d ^ {2}.
$$

proof.  $\left|\| z_{t} - z_{\star}\|_{2}^{2} - \| z_{t} - z\|_{2}^{2}\right| = |\langle z_{t} - z_{\star},(z_{t} - z_{\star}) - (z_{t} - z)\rangle +\langle z_{t} - z_{\star},(z_{t} - z_{\star}) - (z_{t} - z)\rangle |\leq 2d\| z_{\star} - z\|$ . moreover,  $|f_t(z) - f_t(z_\star)|\leq l_{\mathrm{f}}\| z - z_\star \|$ . from the triangle inequality, we have that the sum in the claim is bounded by  $(l_{\mathrm{f}} + \alpha d)t\| z - z_{\star}\| \leq l_{\mathrm{f}}d + \alpha d^2\lesssim l_{\mathrm{f}}^2 /\alpha +\alpha d^2$ .

next, we bound the size of our covering

claim g.5. there exists an  $d / t$  covering of  $\mathcal{n}$  with cardinality at most  $(1 + 2t)^d$ .

proof. observe that  $\mathcal{k}$  is contained in ball of radius  $d$ . set  $\epsilon = d / t$ . by a standard volumetric covering argument, it follows that we can select  $|\mathcal{n}| \leq ((d + \epsilon / 2) / (\epsilon / 2))^d = (1 + \frac{2d}{\epsilon})^d = (1 + 2t)^d$ .

absorbing the approximation error of  $l^2 / \alpha + \alpha d^2$  from claim g.4, and applying a union bound over the cover from claim g.4, we have with probability  $1 - \delta$  that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) - \left(\frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {6}{\alpha} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {t} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right) \\ \lesssim \alpha d ^ {2} k + \frac {l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} + k (\beta + l _ {\mathrm {f}}) l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {k l ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {t (1 + 2 t) ^ {d} (1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right) \\ \lesssim \alpha k d ^ {2} + \frac {k d l ^ {2} + k (\beta + l _ {\mathrm {f}}) l _ {\mathbf {g}}}{\alpha} \log (t) + \frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/c0878d79cf51ca16536a1c27b9c3fd03371bf3987e53a6cfcdf356a61ff512a4.jpg)

# g.3.2 proof of lemma g.3

for simplicity, drop the dependence on  $z_{\star}$ , and observe that, since  $f_{t}, f_{t;k}$  are  $l_{\mathrm{f}}$ -lipschitz, we can bound  $|z_t| \leq 4l_{\mathrm{f}} \| z_t - z_{\star} \|_2$ . moreover,  $\mathbb{e}[z_t \mid \mathcal{f}_{t-k}] = 0$ . we can therefore write

$$
\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} = \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} u _ {t} \cdot \overline {{z}} _ {t},
$$

where we set  $u_{t} \coloneqq 4l_{f}\| z_{t} - z_{\star}\|_{2}$  and  $\overline{z}_t \coloneqq z_t / u_t$ . we can check that

$$
\left| \bar {z} _ {t} \right| \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \leq 1 \text {a . s .} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \mathbb {e} \left[ \bar {z} _ {t} \mid \mathcal {f} _ {t - k} \right] = 0.
$$

hence,  $\overline{z}_t$  is a bounded, random variable with  $\mathbb{e}[\overline{z}_t\mid \mathcal{f}_{t - k}] = 0$  multiplied by a  $\mathcal{f}_{t - k}$ -measurable nonnegative term. note that this does not quite form a martingale sequence, since  $\overline{z}_t$  has mean zero conditional on  $\mathcal{f}_{t - k}$ , not  $\mathcal{f}_{t - 1}$ .

this can be addressed by a blocking argument: let  $t_{i,j} = k + i + jk - 1$  for  $i \in [k]$ , and  $j \in \{1, \dots, t_i\}$  where  $t_i := \max \{j : t_{i,j} \leq t\}$ . then, we can write

$$
\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} u _ {t} \cdot \bar {z} _ {t} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \left(\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} u _ {t} \cdot \bar {z} _ {t _ {i, j}}\right)
$$

now, each term in the inner sum is a martingale sequence with respect to the filtration  $\{\mathcal{f}_{t_{i,j}}\}_{j\geq 1}$ . moreover,  $\overline{z}_{t_{i,j}}\mid \mathcal{f}_{t_{i,j - 1}}$  is  $\frac{1}{4}$ -sub-gaussian. we now invoke the a modification of simchowitz et al. [2018, lemma 4.2 (b)], which follows straightforwardly from adjusting the last step of its proof

lemma g.6. let  $x_{j}, y_{j}$  be two random processes. suppose  $(\mathcal{g}_j)_{j\geq 0}$  is a filtration such that  $(x_{j})$  is  $(\mathcal{g}_j)$ -adapted,  $y_{j}$  is  $(\mathcal{g}_{j-1})$  adapted, and  $x_{j} \mid \mathcal{g}_{j-1}$  is  $\sigma^2$  subgaussian. then, for any  $0 < \beta_{-} \leq \beta_{+}$ ,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left[ \mathbb {i} \left(\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} y _ {j} ^ {2} \leq \beta_ {+}\right) \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} x _ {j} y _ {j} \geq u \max  \left\{\sqrt {\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} y _ {j} ^ {2}}, \beta_ {-} \right\} \cdot \right] \leq \log \lceil \frac {\beta_ {+}}{\beta_ {-}} \rceil \exp (- u ^ {2} / 6 \sigma^ {2})
$$

for each  $i$ , apply the above lemma with  $\beta_{-} = l_{\mathrm{f}}^{2} / \alpha$  and  $\beta_{+} = \max \{tl_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}d^{2},l_{\mathrm{f}}^{2} / \alpha \}$ ,  $x_{j} = \overline{z}_{t_{i,j}}$  and  $y_{j} = u_{t_{i,j}}$ ,  $\sigma^2 = 1/4$ , and  $u = \sqrt{3\log(1/k\delta)/2}$ . then, we have  $\sum_{j=1}^{t_i} y_j^2 \leq \beta_+$  almost surely, so we conclude

that, with probability  $(1 + \log (1\vee \alpha td^{2}))\delta$  , the following holds for any  $\tau >0$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \forall i: \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} u _ {t} \cdot \bar {z} _ {t _ {i, j}} \leq \beta_ {-} \sqrt {3 \log (k / \delta) / 2} + \sqrt {\frac {3}{2} \log (k / \delta) \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} u _ {t _ {i , j}} ^ {2}} \\ = \beta_ {-} \sqrt {3 \log (k / \delta) / 2} + \sqrt {\frac {3 \cdot 1 6 l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{2} \log (k / \delta) \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} \| z _ {t _ {i , j - 1}} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ = \beta_ {-} \sqrt {3 \log (k / \delta) / 2} + \sqrt {2 4 l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \log (k / \delta) \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} \| z _ {t _ {i , j - 1}} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ \leq \frac {l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} (\sqrt {3 \log (k / \delta) / 2} + \frac {1 2}{\tau} \log (k / \delta)) + \frac {\tau}{2} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} \| z _ {t _ {i, j - 1}} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \leq l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \log (k / \delta) \left(\frac {3}{2 \alpha} + \frac {1 2}{\tau}\right) + \frac {\tau}{2} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} \| z _ {t _ {i, j - 1}} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, with probability with probability  $1 - (1 + \log (\alpha td^2))\delta$  , for any  $\tau ,\tau_1 > 0$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t _ {i}} u _ {t} \cdot \bar {z} _ {t _ {i, j}} \\ \leq k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \log (k / \delta) \left(\frac {3}{2 \alpha} + \frac {2 7}{\tau}\right) + \frac {\tau}{2} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t - k} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$\tau = \alpha /6,\delta \gets \delta /(1 + \log (1\vee \alpha td^2))$  , we have that with probability  $1 - \delta$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} z _ {t} - \frac {\alpha}{1 2} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| z _ {t - k} - z _ {\star} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ \lesssim \frac {k l _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2}}{\alpha} \log \left(\frac {k (1 + \log_ {+} (\alpha t d ^ {2})}{\delta}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/2a45ca6614591c07220c2f441c17c8687213b82f238c5b2bc806ddb97de19a09.jpg)

# g.3.3 high probability regret with memory: proof of theorem 8

proof. we reiterate the argument of anava et al. [2015]. decompose

$$
\sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \tilde {f} _ {t} (z _ {t}, z _ {t - 1}, z _ {t - 2}, \dots , z _ {t - h}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) = \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} f _ {t} (z _ {t}) - f _ {t} (z _ {\star}) + \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \tilde {f} _ {t} (z _ {t}, z _ {t - 1}, z _ {t - 2}, \dots , z _ {t - h}) - f _ {t} (z _ {t}).
$$

we can bound the first sum directly from theorem 12. the second term can be bounded as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \tilde {f} _ {t} \left(z _ {t}, z _ {t - 1}, z _ {t - 2}, \dots , z _ {t - h}\right) - f _ {t} \left(z _ {t}\right) \leq l _ {\mathrm {c}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \| \left(0, z _ {t - 1} - z _ {t}, \dots , z _ {t - h} - z _ {t}\right) \| _ {2} \\ \leq \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \| z _ {t} - z _ {t - i} \| _ {2} \\ \leq l _ {\mathrm {c}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \| z _ {t - j + 1} - z _ {t - j} \| _ {2} \\ \leq l _ {\mathrm {c}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} \eta_ {t - j + 1} \| \boldsymbol {g} _ {t - j} \| \\ \leq h l _ {\mathrm {c}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \eta_ {t - j + 1} \| \boldsymbol {g} _ {t - j} \| \\ \leq h l _ {\mathrm {c}} l _ {\mathbf {g}} \sum_ {t = k + 1} ^ {t} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {h} \eta_ {t - j + 1} \\ \leq h ^ {2} l _ {\mathrm {c}} l _ {\mathbf {g}} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {t} \eta_ {t + 1} \lesssim \frac {h ^ {2} l _ {\mathrm {c}} l _ {\mathbf {g}} \log t}{\alpha} \\ \end{array}
$$

this establishes the desired bound.
